Long-term behavior of the master equation on countable system space and approximation method of the (stationary) solutions via finite subsystems in the thermodynamic limit
Bernd Michael Fernengel , Thilo Gross , Wolfram Just \text{Bernd Michael Fernengel},\text{Thilo Gross},\text{Wolfram Just} (1) HIFMB Oldenburg,(2) Institute of Mathematics University of Rostock

Abstract




1
Abstract

The Master equation - also called the differential Chapman-Kolmogorov equation - is a linear differential equation, which describes the probability evolution in a discrete system. While this is well understood, if the underlying state space is finite, the mathematics required for the treatment of
infinite dimensional systems is way more complicated and advanced.

In this paper we provide sufficient criteria for the rates, which guarantee that the infinite dimensional master equation is both well defined and that it can be approximated by finite subsystems in the thermodynamic limit. Moreover, we lay out the required assumptions for the time limit for an infinite dimensional system to exist, and when it can be interchanged with the limit of a large system. We give sufficient criteria, when these two limits commute and demonstrate on various examples, when they do not.

2
Introduction

Master equations describe the time evolution of probabilities in a system. For a discrete state space, this dynamic comes from the transition between different states, which means that the system can be modeled as a directed, weighted graph, where the directed links model the transitions and the non-negative weights the transition rate.

The master equation can be viewed a a differential version of the Chapman-Kolmogorov equation and is usually formulated as the following initial value problem

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}P^{(i)}(t)

\displaystyle={\sum}\limits_{{j\in\Omega}\atop{j\neq i}}\;\big(\,P^{(j)}(t)\,\gamma_{j\to i}-P^{(i)}(t)\,\gamma_{i\to j}\,\big)

\displaystyle P^{(i)}(t=0)

\displaystyle=P^{(i)}_{0},

or in matrix-vector notation

\displaystyle\dot{\boldsymbol{P}}(t)

\displaystyle=\,\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}(t)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}(t=0)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{P}_{0}

with

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(i,j)}

\displaystyle=\,\begin{cases}\gamma_{j\to i}&\text{ , for }i\neq j\\
-{\sum}\limits_{k\in\Omega}\gamma_{j\to k}&\text{ , for }i=j\,.\end{cases}

From a mathematical point of view, natural questions to ask are whether unique solutions of the master equations exist, if the solutions possesses the desired properties of a probability distribution (being non-negative and normalized) and under which condition the long-term behavior
t\to\infty
exists.

For a finite system, these questions can be answered: Not only does the solution exist for all times and satisfies all properties of a probability distribution, but it is also possible to compute not only the transition matrix
\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}
via its power series, but also the stationary solution, for which an explicit expression in terms of the transition rates exists
[
MG13
,
Haa17
,
FD22
]
.

Whereas for finite dimensional systems one can rely on any standard textbook of ordinary differential equation (see for example
[
Kön06
,
FK04
,
Van92
,
Hon12
]
), the literature is considerably more sparse in the countable infinite dimensional case. This is due to the fact that one has to deal with operators on the non-reflexive Banach space
l^{1}(\mathbb{N})
.

This paper is meant to close this gap, or at least part of it. In our research we tried to combine aspects of different theories, such as Markov chains
[
Bré20
,
Dou+18
,
Pri13
]
, operator theory
[
FK04
,
Wer11
]
, C0-semigroups
[
EN00
,
BFR17
]
, and infinite graphs
[
Die24
]
.

In addition to the questions about master equations listed above, we are interested in how the infinite dimensional case is linked to the (well understood) finite dimensional one, that is, whether there is a
finite subsystem
capturing the essential dynamics of the infinite system. This is not only handy when transferring the formulas for the stationary solution from the
finite
dimensional - to the
infinite
dimensional case, but also when it comes to numerical approximations. This means that we have two different limits to consider, the
time limit
and the limit of the
system size
, which we call
thermodynamic
limit. Or more more formally: Given a countable, infinite system
\Omega
and a finite subsystem
F\subseteq\Omega
,
|F|<\infty
, there are four possible limits one can consider

-

The time limit
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}^{F}\left(t\right)

-

The limit of the system size
\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}^{F}\left(t\right)

-

The time limit, followed by the limit of the system size:
\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}^{F}\left(t\right)

-

The limit of the system size, followed by the time limit:
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}^{F}\left(t\right)

The following figure illustrates serves as an illustration:

t<\infty
F\subseteq\Omega\atop|F|<\infty
|\Omega|=\infty
``t=\infty"
time limit
thermodynamic
limit
\Large\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t)
\Large\boldsymbol{P}^{F}_{\infty}
\Large\boldsymbol{P}(t)
\Large\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}
\Large\boldsymbol{P}_{*}
t\to\infty
t\to\infty
|F|\to\infty
|F|\to\infty

Figure 1
:
Illustrating the time limit and the thermodynamic limit.

Note that it is apriori not clear if all of the limits are well defined and if this diagram commutes.

Before defining in detail, what we mean by the notation
\boldsymbol{P}^{F}
or the thermodynamic limit
|F|\to\infty
we want to illustrate on two simple examples, that the diagram depicted in figure
1
is not necessarily commutative.

We start by looking at figure
2
, where the probability flows from left to right, but is ‘reshuffled’ . It can be shown - and we hope the readers believe us at this point, details are in chapter
12
- that for suitable rates the time limit for the infinite dimensional system exists, and can be approximated by the sequence of increasing subsets
F_{n}=\{-n,\dots,n\}
. However, when a different sequence of increasing subsets in chosen, e.g.
\widetilde{F}_{n}=\{-n,\dots n+1\}
is chosen, then this limit does not exist, since
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t)=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\boldsymbol{E}_{n+1}=\lightning
.

While the previous argument was based on the topology of the network, the other case is based on a suitable choice of rates. We consider the network depicted in figure
3
, which is a linear chain with one open end on one side, and a trapping state on the other. For
p\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
, every state
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
can be shown to be transient, that is there is a non-zero chance that a trajectory stating at a state
n\in\mathbb{N}
does not return. Informally speaking, there are two attracting ‘forces’, one is the state
0
, the other is infinity. When we consider now a finite network
F_{n}=\{0,\dots,n\}
, the attracting force at infinity vanishes, meaning that the limiting solution of the finite subnetwork is
\boldsymbol{E}_{0}
. This mean that the limit of stationary solutions of
finite
systems exists
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{n}}(t)=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\boldsymbol{E}_{0}=\boldsymbol{E}_{0}
, while the
time
limit of the
infinite
dimensional systems does
not
exist, only the pointwise limit
\boldsymbol{P}(t)\xlongrightarrow[\text{pointwise}]{t\to\infty}(p_{*},0,0,0,\dots)
, for some
p_{*}\in(0,1)
, which is not a probability measure.

:
The linear chain with an open end on one side, and a trapping state on the other

for
q\in(0,1)
and
p\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
.

We hope that these - hand waving - arguments are enough to convince the reader that the interchanging of the two limits is no trivial procedure, but requires careful handling.

The outline of the paper is the following:

While section
3
is a reminder of the connection between master equations and Markov chains, we prove the existence and uniqueness of the solution of the
infinite
dimensional master equation
\boldsymbol{P}(t)
in section
4
.

After the definition of a
finite subsystem
in section
5
, we show that the solution of the infinite dimensional master equation can be approximated by the solution
\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t)
of finite subsystems in the thermodynamic limit
\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t){\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.6641}\xlongrightarrow{|F|\to\infty}}\boldsymbol{P}(t)
.

Chapter
6
summarizes equivalent conditions for steady states of - both discrete-time and continuous-time - Markov chains, in the language that we require later on, in particular showing that the existence of stationary solutions is closely related to the Markov chain being
positive recurrent
.

In section
7
we show that the solution of the infinite dimensional master equation converges to a stationary probability distribution, if the associated system is both irreducible and positive recurrent.

While neither the time limit nor the limit of the system size is necessarily uniform, it is shown in section
8
that if the stationary solutions of the finite subsystems converges, it will converge to a stationary solution of the infinite dimensional system. Moreover, if the stationary solutions are of a special form, we can show that the thermodynamic limit of the stationary solution for finite systems exists
\boldsymbol{P}^{F}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}){\color[rgb]{0.9258,0.3945,0}\xlongrightarrow{|F|\to\infty}}\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{P}_{0})
and hence the the diagram of figure
1
commutes. In particular, the long-term behavior of an infinite dimensional master equation of an irreducible system is not only well defined, but the explicit form of this stationary solution can also be recovered by looking at stationary solutions of corresponding finite subsystems and letting the system size tend to infinity.

In each of the following sections
9
,
10
,
11
and
12
an example is being discussed in order to demonstrate the implications of our theorems. Chapter
14
summarizes our work and points to possible generalizations.

3
From Markov chains to master equations

Markov chains are special kinds of stochastic processes that satisfy the so-called
Markov property
, which states that the probability for the next event depends only on the current state and not on the previous history. Both the underlying state space
\Omega
as well as the time can be discrete or continuous. From here on, we will assume, that
\Omega
is a
discrete, countable infinite
space.

3.1
Discrete-time Markov chains

Let
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a discrete-time Markov chain on
\Omega
, where the transition probabilities are given by

\displaystyle Q^{(i,j)}

\displaystyle:=q_{j\to i}:=\mathcal{P}\left(X_{n+1}=i\,|\,X_{n}=j\right)

(1)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}\left(X_{n+1}=i\,|\,X_{n}=j,\dots,X_{0}=j_{0}\right)\text{ (compare \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{privault2013understanding}{}{}]})}.

This specifies the transition matrix
Q\in[0,1]^{\Omega\times\Omega}
, which is a column-stochastic, countable matrix that satisfies

\displaystyle Q^{(i,j)}

\displaystyle\geq 0\text{ , for all }i,j\in\Omega\text{ and }

(2)

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i\in\Omega}Q^{(i,j)}

\displaystyle=1\text{ , for all }j\in\Omega.

Our system
\mathcal{S}
is fully described by a
countable, directed, weighted graph

\mathcal{S}=(\Omega,\mathcal{E},q)
(which we will call a
network
), where the nodes are given by the set of states
\Omega
and the edges
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\Omega\times\Omega
(also called
links
) are a set of
ordered
pairs of states which indicate the transition between these states. The strength of a link is given by its weight function

\displaystyle q:\;\;\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle\to[0,1]

(3)

\displaystyle(j,i)

\displaystyle\mapsto q_{j\to i}.

When there is no transition from state
j
to state
i
, the associated transition probability vanishes,
q_{j\to i}=0
.

To keep the notation simple, we do not distinguish between the index
n\in\mathbb{N}
and the state
\omega_{n}\in\Omega
with index
n
:

\displaystyle\omega_{n}\;\;\,\widehat{=}

\displaystyle n

(4)

\displaystyle\Omega=\{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}.\dotsc,\}\,\widehat{=}

\displaystyle\{1,2.\dotsc,\}=\mathbb{N}.

We call
\boldsymbol{P}\in(\mathbb{R})^{\Omega}
a
probability vector
if
\boldsymbol{P}\in[0,1]^{\Omega}
and
\|\boldsymbol{P}\|_{1}=1
, that is a vector with non-negative entries that sum up to one. When
\boldsymbol{P}(n)
is a probability vector describing the probability distribution of
\Omega
at the time
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, then
\boldsymbol{P}(n+1):=Q\,\boldsymbol{P}(n)
is a probability distribution at the next time step
n+1
.

Definition 1

(
Stationary solution
)
.

A probability vector is called
stationary solution
of the Markov chain if its probability distribution does not change after another time step
(Q\,\boldsymbol{P}=\boldsymbol{P})
, that is, if it is an eigenvector of the transition matrix
Q
to the eigenvalue
\lambda=1
.

3.2
Continuous-time Markov chains

Given a time homogeneous, continuous-time Markov process
(X_{t})_{t\geq\,0}
on the state space
\Omega
, then we have for all time points
t_{1}<\dots<t_{n}<t_{n+1}
and all states
j_{1},\dots,j_{n-1},i,j\in\Omega

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(X_{t_{n+1}}

\displaystyle=i\,|\,X_{t_{n}}=j,\dots,X_{t_{0}}=j_{0}){\mathrel{\to}^{\text{Markov}}}\mathcal{P}(X_{t_{n+1}}=i\,|\,X_{t_{n}}=j).

(5)

Time homogeneous means that the transition matrix, whose components are defined as

\displaystyle Q^{(i,j)}(t_{2},t_{1})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{P}(X_{t_{2}}=i\,|\,X_{t_{1}}=j)\text{ for }t_{1}<t_{2}

(6)

depends only on the time difference, that is

\displaystyle Q^{(i,j)}(t_{2},\,t_{1}){\mathrel{\to}^{\text{time homogeneous }}}Q^{(i,j)}(t_{2}-t_{1},0)=:Q^{(i,j)}(t_{2}-t_{1}).

(7)

We note that the stochastic matrices
(Q(t))_{t\geq 0}
satisfy the functional equation
Q(t_{1}+t_{2})=Q(t_{2})\cdot Q(t_{1})
, making it a semigroup:

\displaystyle\bigl[Q(t_{2})

\displaystyle Q(t_{1})\bigr]^{(i,j)}=\sum\limits_{k\in\Omega}\,\underbrace{Q^{(i,k)}(t_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}(X_{t_{2}}=i\,|\,X_{0}=k)}\;\;\;\underbrace{Q^{(k.j)}(t_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}(X_{t_{1}}=k\,|\,X_{0}=j)}

(8)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\text{Def }Q}}\sum\limits_{k\in\Omega}\underbrace{\mathcal{P}(X_{t_{2}}=i\,|\,X_{0}=k)}_{\mathcal{P}(X_{t_{1}+t_{2}}=i\,|\,X_{t_{1}}=k)}\,\mathcal{P}(X_{t_{1}}=k\,|\,X_{0}=j)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\text{time homogeneity + }}_{\text{Markov property}}}\sum\limits_{k\in\Omega}\,\underbrace{\Bigl(\mathcal{P}(X_{t_{1}+t_{2}}=i\,|\,X_{t_{1}}=k,X_{0}=j)\Bigr)}_{\frac{\mathcal{P}(X_{t_{1}+t_{2}}=i,X_{t_{1}}=k,X_{0}=j)}{\mathcal{P}(X_{t_{1}}=k,X_{0}=j)}}\;\;\;\underbrace{\Bigl(\mathcal{P}(X_{t_{1}}=k\,|\,X_{0}=j)\Bigr)}_{\frac{\mathcal{P}(X_{t_{1}}=k,X_{0}=j)}{\mathcal{P}(X_{0}=j)}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k\in\Omega}\,\underbrace{\left(\frac{\mathcal{P}(X_{t_{1}+t_{2}}=i,X_{t_{1}}=k,X_{0}=j)}{\mathcal{P}(X_{0}=j)}\right)}_{\mathcal{P}(X_{t_{1}+t_{2}}=i,X_{t_{1}}=k\,|\,X_{0}=j)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}(X_{t_{1}+t_{2}}=i\,|\,X_{0}=j)=[Q(t_{2}+t_{1})]^{(i,j)}.

When we additionally assume that the function

\displaystyle Q\;:\;\mathbb{R}_{\geq\,0}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{BL}\Bigl(l^{1}(\Omega)\Bigr)

(9)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto Q_{t}:=Q(t)

is continuously differentiable, then
(Q_{t})_{t\geq\,0}
is even a
uniformly continuous

C_{0}
-semigroup, with the bounded generator
\Gamma:=\dot{Q}(t=0)
, that is
Q_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}
, with
\|\Gamma\|_{1}^{\text{op}}<\infty
. We even have
Q(h)\xlongrightarrow[\|\cdot\|_{1}^{\text{op}}]{h\to 0+}\mathbb{1}
, not only pointwise, but also with respect to the operator norm
[
EN00
,
BFR17
]
.

When starting st state
j\in\Omega
, the probability for being at a different state
i\in\Omega\backslash\{j\}
after a small time
h
grows linearly with
h
, with the proportionality constant
\gamma_{j\to i}
called the
rate
. Since probabilities must sum up to one, we get:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\left(X_{h}=i\,|\,X_{0}=j\right)=\begin{cases}h\,\gamma_{j\to i}\hskip 8.53581pt+\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(h)&\text{ , if }i\neq j\\
1-h\,\gamma_{j\to}+\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(h)&\text{ , if }i=j.\end{cases}

This allows us to compute the entries of the generator matrix, as:

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(i,j)}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\gamma_{j\to i}&\text{ , for }i\neq j\\
-{\sum}\limits_{k\in\Omega}\gamma_{j\to k}&\text{ , for }i=j\,.\end{cases}

(generator)

Then the function

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}_{\geq\,0}

\displaystyle\to l^{1}(\Omega)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}

is the solution of the following differential equation, called the
mater equation
:

\displaystyle\dot{\boldsymbol{P}}(t)

\displaystyle=\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}(t)

(master eq.)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}(t=0)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{P}_{0}

Similar to the discrete-time case, we have an associated state transition network, where the states correspond to the nodes and the links (indicating transitions between the states) correspond to the edges. In contrast to the discrete-time case, the weights are only required to be non-negative (in contrast to lying in the interval
[0,1]
) and we explicitly exclude self-loops:

\displaystyle\gamma:\;\;\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq\,0}

(10)

\displaystyle(j,i)

\displaystyle\mapsto\gamma_{j\to i},\text{ with }\gamma_{i\to i}=0.

This is due to the fact that for the continuous-time Markov chains we have
transition rates
instead of
transition probabilities
, which means that the probability for remaining in some state is always strictly positive:
\mathcal{P}(X_{t}=i\,|\,X_{0}=i)>0
for all times
t\geq 0
and all states
i\in\Omega
.

The solution is given by
\boldsymbol{P}(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}):=\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}
, with the initial state
\boldsymbol{P}(t=0)=\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\in[0,1]^{\Omega}
and the
solution operator

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}:={\sum}\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{\Gamma^{k}\,t^{k}}{k!}=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\left(\mathbb{1}+\frac{t\,\Gamma}{n}\right)^{n}.

(11)

4
The solution of the infinite dimensional master equation

In the following section we study the solution of the infinite dimensional master equation, by first showing that the existence of a unique solution in the Banach space
l^{1}(\Omega)
, which is something that is not guaranteed for a infinite, countable state space
\Omega
. We continue by showing that the solution has the desired properties of a probability vector (namely being non-negative and normalized to one) for all times
t\geq 0
.

For
irreducible
networks the solution operator can be shown to be - element-wise - strictly positive, resulting in - at most - one stationary solution.

4.1
Existence and uniqueness of the solution

In this section we will prove that the master equation (
master eq.
) has a unique solution in
l^{1}(\Omega)
, by following the arguments of
[
Bel47
]
and
[
BFR17
]
. Let the generator matrix
\Gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{\Omega\times\Omega}
be as in equation (
generator
). The master equation itself can be written in two equivalent ways, namely a
differential
- and an
integral
form:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\boldsymbol{P}(t)

\displaystyle=\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}(t)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}(t=0)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{P}_{0}

\hskip-28.45274pt\iff

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}(t)=\boldsymbol{P}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}(\tau)\text{ d}\tau.

(master eq. integral form)

The
existence
and
uniqueness
of the solution is shown in details in lemma
43
. In the following, we outline the basic structure of the proof, using and adaptive version of the theorem of Picard-Lindelöf.

First, we define a sequence iteratively, namely

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}_{0}

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{P}(0)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}_{n+1}(t)

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{P}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}_{n}(\tau)\text{ d}\tau

We proceed by proving that this sequence is bounded (claim
44
), in particular

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{P}_{n+1}(t)\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\|_{1}\,\cdot\,\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\Gamma\|_{1}^{\text{(op)}}\text{ d}\tau\right),

and that we can estimate the norm of the difference of two consecutive members of the sequence (claim
45
):

\displaystyle\|\left(\boldsymbol{P}_{n}-\boldsymbol{P}_{n-1}\right)(t)\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\Gamma\|_{1}^{\text{(op)}}\text{ d}\tau\right)^{n}}{n!}

From this we can deduce that this sequence is a Cauchy sequence in
l^{1}(\Omega)
(claim
46
), that is

\displaystyle\|\left(\boldsymbol{P}_{n+m}-\boldsymbol{P}_{n}\right)(t)\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\sum\limits_{k=n+1}^{n+m}\frac{\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\Gamma\|_{1}^{\text{(op)}}\text{ d}\tau\right)^{k}}{k!}}_{<\,\infty}\xlongrightarrow{m,n\to\infty}0,

Due to completeness of the space
l^{1}(\Omega)
, the sequence converges with respect to the
\|\cdot\|_{1}
-norm to some function

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}\,:\,\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

\displaystyle\to l^{1}(\Omega)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\boldsymbol{P}(t)

This function
\boldsymbol{P}(t)
solves the master equation. To see this, we note that it suffices to show, that the sequence
\left(\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}_{n}(\tau)\text{ d}\tau\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq l^{1}(\Omega)
converges in
l^{1}(\Omega)
for
n\to\infty
to
\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}(\tau)\text{ d}\tau
, since then we have:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}_{n}(t)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{P}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}_{n}\hskip 14.22636pt\Bigl|\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}

\displaystyle\underbrace{\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}_{n}(t)}_{\boldsymbol{P}(t)}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{P}_{0}+\underbrace{\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}_{n}}_{\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}},

which is just the integral form of the master equation (
master eq. integral form
).

To show the convergence of
\left(\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}_{n}(\tau)\text{ d}\tau\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, we can compute the following:

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}_{n}(\tau)\text{ d}\tau-\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}(\tau)\text{ d}\tau\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\|\Gamma\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}_{n}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{P}(\tau)\bigr)\|_{1}\text{ d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq t\,\cdot\,\sup\limits_{\tau\in[0,t]}\|\Gamma\|_{1}^{\text{(op)}}\|\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}_{n}-\boldsymbol{P}\bigr)(\tau)\|_{1}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\text{some }\xi\in[0,t]}}t\,\cdot\,\|\Gamma\|_{1}^{\text{(op)}}\,\underbrace{\|\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}_{n}(\xi)-\boldsymbol{P}\bigr)(\xi)\|_{1}}_{\xlongrightarrow{n\to\infty}0}\xlongrightarrow{n\to\infty}0,

where we used the fact that a continuous function attains its supremum on a compact set.

In order to show uniqueness of the solution, we assume that there is another function

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{P}}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq\,0}

\displaystyle\to l^{1}(\Omega)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\tilde{\boldsymbol{P}}(t)

solving the master equation. Then we can make the following estimation:

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{P}(t)-\tilde{\boldsymbol{P}}(t)\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}(\tau)-\tilde{\boldsymbol{P}}(\tau)\bigr)\text{ d}\tau\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\underbrace{\|\Gamma\|_{1}^{\text{(op)}}}_{\geq\,0}\,\cdot\,\underbrace{\|\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}(\tau)-\tilde{\boldsymbol{P}}(\tau)\bigr)\|_{1}}_{\geq\,0}\text{ d}\tau.

By the integral version of Grönwall’s lemma
53
, this implies that
\|\boldsymbol{P}(t)-\tilde{\boldsymbol{P}}(t)\|_{1}=0
, which concludes the proof.

In the next section we will see, that the fact that
\Gamma
is a generator matrix - and therefore of the form described in equation (
generator
) - and
\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{PV}
a probability vector, guarantees that the solution
\boldsymbol{P}(t)
will also be a probability vector for all time
t>0
.

4.2
The solution remains a probability vector

When
\boldsymbol{P}_{0}
is a probability vector (that is
\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{PV}
), then the solution of the master equation
\boldsymbol{P}(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}):=\mathrm{e}^{\Gamma\,t}\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}
is also a probability vector for all times
t\geq\,0
, provided that
\|\Gamma\|_{1}^{\text{(op)}}<\infty
. We divide the proof into three parts:

Lemma 2

(finite probability flow)
.

The probability flow is finite for all times
t\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{n,m\in\Omega}P^{(n)}(t)\,\gamma_{n\to m}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\in\Omega}\,P^{(n)}(t)\,\underbrace{\sum\limits_{m\in\Omega}\,\gamma_{n\to m}}_{\gamma_{n\to}}=\sum\limits_{n\in\Omega}\,P^{(n)}(t)\,\gamma_{n\to}

\displaystyle\overset{{Hoelder}}{\leq}\underbrace{\left\|\left(P^{(n)}(t)\right)_{n\in\Omega}\right\|_{1}}_{1}\,\cdot\,\underbrace{\left\|\left(\gamma_{n\to}\right)_{n\in\Omega}\right\|_{\infty}}_{=\sup\{\left(\gamma_{n\to}\right)_{n\in\Omega}\}}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{}}{\leq}\frac{1}{2}\,\|\Gamma\|_{1}^{\text{(op)}}\,<\,\infty.

Lemma 3

(Boundedness of the solution)
.

Every entry of the solution of the master equation remains in the interval
[0,1]
, that is
\boldsymbol{P}(t)\in[0,1]^{\Omega}

Proof.

When for some state
i\in\Omega
and some time
t\geq 0
, the i-th entry of
\boldsymbol{P}(t)
equals one (zero), then the time derivative
\frac{\text{ d}}{\text{ d}t}P^{(i)}(t)
of that entry is negative (positive), since

\displaystyle P^{(i)}(t)=1\Longrightarrow\frac{\text{ d}}{\text{ d}t}P^{(i)}(t)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\eqref{Equation_MasterEquation_components}}}{\sum}\limits_{k\in\Omega\atop k\neq i}\;\big(\,\underbrace{P^{(k)}(t)}_{0}\,\gamma_{k\to i}-\underbrace{P^{(i)}(t)}_{1}\,\gamma_{i\to k}\,\big)=\,-\sum\limits_{k\in\Omega}\gamma_{i\to k}\in(-\infty,0]

(12)

\displaystyle P^{(j)}(t)=\,0\Longrightarrow\frac{\text{ d}}{\text{ d}t}P^{(j)}(t)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\eqref{Equation_MasterEquation_components}}}\,{\sum}\limits_{k\in\Omega\atop k\neq j}\;\big(\,P^{(k)}(t)\,\gamma_{k\to j}-\underbrace{P^{(j)}(t)}_{0}\,\gamma_{j\to k}\,\big)=\,\sum\limits_{k\in\Omega}P^{(k)}(t)\,\gamma_{k\to j}\overset{\text{lemma }\ref{Lemma_FiniteProbabilityFlow}}{\in}[0,\infty).

This ensures that every entry of
\boldsymbol{P}(t+\epsilon)
remains in the interval
[0,1]
:

\displaystyle P^{(i)}(t)=1

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}(t+\epsilon)\bigr)^{(j)}=\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}(t)+\epsilon\,\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}(t)+\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(\epsilon^{2})\bigr)^{(j)}

\displaystyle=\delta_{i,j}+\epsilon\,\left[(1-\delta_{i,j})\,\gamma_{i\to j}-\delta_{i,j}\,\gamma_{j\to}\right]+\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{j=i}}1-\epsilon\,\gamma_{i\to}+\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(\epsilon)\leq 1\text{ and }

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{j\neq i}}\epsilon\,\gamma_{i\to j}+\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(\epsilon)\geq 0.

\displaystyle P^{(i)}(t)=0

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow P^{(i)}(t+\epsilon)=\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}(t+\epsilon)\bigr)^{(i)}

\displaystyle=\epsilon\,\sum\limits_{k\in\Omega\atop k\neq i}P^{(k)}(t)\,\gamma_{k\to i}+\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(\epsilon^{2})\geq 0.

∎

Lemma 4

(Interchanging time derivative and index summation)
.

The time derivative of the total probability mass equals the sum of the time derivatives of the individual probabilities:

\displaystyle\frac{\text{ d}}{\text{ d}t}\sum\limits_{n\in\Omega}P^{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\in\Omega}\frac{\text{ d}}{\text{ d}t}P^{(n)}(t).

While this statement statement follows directly from the linearity of the derivative for a finite state space, it involves interchanging limits for the infinite dimensional case.

Proof.

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\sum\limits_{n\in\Omega}P^{(n)}(t+h)-\sum\limits_{n\in\Omega}P^{(n)}(t)}{h}-\sum\limits_{n\in\Omega}\underbrace{\frac{\text{ d}}{\text{ d}t}P^{(n)}(t)}_{(\Gamma\boldsymbol{P})^{(n)}(t)}\right|=\left|\frac{1}{h}\sum\limits_{n\in\Omega}\left(\underbrace{\left(P^{(n)}(t+h)-P^{(n)}(t)\right)}_{\int_{t}^{t+h}(\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P})^{(n)}(\tau)}-\underbrace{h\,(\Gamma\boldsymbol{P})^{(n)}(t)}_{\int_{t}^{t+h}\text{ d}\tau(\Gamma\boldsymbol{P})^{(n)}(t)}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{n\in\Omega}\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}\text{ d}\tau\left|\bigl(\Gamma\boldsymbol{P}(\tau)-\Gamma\boldsymbol{P}(t)\bigr)^{(n)}\right|{\mathrel{\to}^{\text{monotone}}_{\text{convergence}}}\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}\text{ d}\tau\underbrace{\sum\limits_{n\in\Omega}\left|\left(\Gamma\boldsymbol{P}(\tau)-\Gamma\boldsymbol{P}(t)\right)^{(n)}\right|}_{\|\Gamma\boldsymbol{P}(\tau)-\Gamma\boldsymbol{P}(t)\|_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Gamma\|_{1}^{\text{(op)}}\,\sup\limits_{\tau\in[t,t+h]}\|\boldsymbol{P}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{P}(t)\|_{1}\xlongrightarrow[\boldsymbol{P}\text{ continuous}]{h\to\infty}0.

∎

Lemma 5

(The solution remains normalized)
.

The norm of the solution of the master equation remains constant for all times, that is

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{P}(t)\|_{1}=\|\underbrace{\boldsymbol{P}(t=0)}_{\boldsymbol{P}_{0}}\|_{1}{\mathrel{\to}^{\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{PV}}}1.

Proof.

\displaystyle\frac{\text{ d}}{\text{ d}t}\|\boldsymbol{P}(t)\|_{1}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\text{lemma }\ref{Lemma_BoundednessOfTheSolution}}_{\boldsymbol{P}(t)\in\left(\mathbb{R}_{\geq\,0}\right)^{\Omega}}}\frac{\text{ d}}{\text{ d}t}\sum\limits_{n\in\Omega}P^{(n)}(t){\mathrel{\to}^{\text{lemma }\ref{Lemma_InterchangingTimeDerivativeAndIndexSummation}}}\sum\limits_{n\in\Omega}\underbrace{\frac{\text{ d}}{\text{ d}t}P^{(n)}(t)}_{\sum\limits_{m\in\Omega}P^{(m)}(t)\,\gamma_{m\to n}-P^{(n)}(t)\,\gamma_{n\to m}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n,m\in\Omega}\left(P^{(m)}(t)\,\gamma_{m\to n}-P^{(n)}(t)\,\gamma_{n\to m}\right)=\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{n,m\in\Omega}P^{(m)}(t)\,\gamma_{m\to n}\right)}_{<\,\infty}-\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{n,m\in\Omega}P^{(n)}(t)\,\gamma_{n\to m}\right)}_{<\,\infty}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\text{lemma }\ref{Lemma_FiniteProbabilityFlow}}}0.

∎

4.3
The positivity of the solution operator for a strongly connected network

Given a network
\mathcal{S}=(\Omega,\mathcal{E},\gamma)
with two states
i,j\in\Omega
such that state
i
is reachable from state
j
,
j\rightsquigarrow i
, then the
i
-
j
-th entry of the solution operator is strictly positive,
\left(\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\right)_{ij}>0
for all
t>0
.

In particular, for a strongly connected network
\mathcal{S}
, the solution operator has only strictly positive entries for all times, making it irreducible, that is
\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\in(\mathbb{R}_{>0})^{\Omega\times\Omega}
for all
t>0
.

Proof.

∎

Let
i,j\in\Omega
be states in the network, such that state
i
is reachable from state
j
,
j\rightsquigarrow i
. Since we know that the limit
\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}(\mathbb{1}+\frac{t\,\Gamma}{n})^{n}
exists in the
norm
topology, it also exists pointwise, which means, it suffices to show that that the sequence

\displaystyle\left(\left(\left(\mathbb{1}+\frac{t\,\Gamma}{n}\right)^{n}\right)^{(i,j)}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=\sum\limits_{k_{1}\in\Omega}\cdots\sum\limits_{k_{n-1}\in\Omega}\left(\mathbb{1}+\frac{\Gamma\,t}{n}\right)^{(i,\,k_{1})}\dotsc\;\left(\mathbb{1}+\frac{\Gamma\,t}{n}\right)^{(k_{n-1},\,j)}

(13)

has a strictly positive lower bound.

(a)

Original network
\mathcal{S}
1

2

3

4

{\gamma_{1\to 2}}
{\gamma_{1\to 4}}
{\gamma_{2\to 3}}
{\gamma_{3\to 4}}
{\gamma_{3\to 1}}

(b)

Network
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
associated to
\mathbb{1}+\frac{t\,\Gamma}{n}
1

2

3

4

{\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{1\to 2}}
{\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{1\to 4}}
{\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{2\to 3}}
{\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{3\to 4}}
{\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{3\to 1}}
{1-\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{1\to}}
{1-\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{2\to}}
{1-\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{3\to}}
{1-\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{4\to}}

Figure 4
:
Illustrating the difference between the original network
\mathcal{S}
and network
\tilde{S}_{n}
associated to the matrix
\mathbb{1}+\frac{t\,\Gamma}{n}
, with the modified link strength and additional self-loops.

The walk
(1,1,2,3,3,4)\in\mathcal{WP}(1\xlongrightarrow{5}4,\,\tilde{S}_{n})\subseteq\,\mathcal{W}(1\xlongrightarrow{5}4,\tilde{S}_{n})
, after removing the self-loops, becomes a
path
, where as the walk
(1,2,3,1,2,3,4)\in\mathcal{W}(1\xlongrightarrow{6}4,\tilde{S}_{n})\backslash\mathcal{WP}(1\xlongrightarrow{6}4,\tilde{S}_{n})
does not.

We call
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{n}:=(\Omega,\tilde{\mathcal{E}},\tilde{\gamma})
the network associated to the matrix
\mathbb{1}+\frac{t\,\Gamma}{n}
, provided that
n\in\mathbb{N}
is large enough, such that all entries are non-negative and
1-\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{i\to}
is positive for all
i\in\Omega
.
It can be recovered from the original network
\mathcal{S}
, by modifying the links between different states with a factor of
\frac{t}{n}
and adding a self-loop at every state with a weight of
1+\frac{t}{n}\,\Gamma^{(s,s)}
for all
s\in\Omega
.

We need the following definitions:

\displaystyle\gamma_{\to}:=

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{i\in\Omega}\gamma_{i\to}

(14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(j\xlongrightarrow{n}i,\,\mathcal{S}):=

\displaystyle\{\text{ all \emph{walks} $\omega\in\Omega^{n+1}$ of length $n\in\mathbb{N}$ from state $j\in\Omega$ }

\displaystyle\text{ to state $i\in\Omega$ in the network $\mathcal{S}$}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(j\xlongrightarrow{n}i,\,\mathcal{S}):=

\displaystyle\{\text{ all \emph{paths} $\omega\in\Omega^{n+1}$ of length $n\in\mathbb{N}$ from state $j\in\Omega$ }

\displaystyle\text{ to state $i\in\Omega$ in the network $\mathcal{S}$}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{WP}(j\xlongrightarrow{n}i,\,\mathcal{S}):=

\displaystyle\{\text{ all \emph{walks} $\omega\in\Omega^{n+1}$ of length $n\in\mathbb{N}$ from state $j\in\Omega$}

\displaystyle\text{ to state $i\in\Omega$ in the network $\mathcal{S}$, }

\displaystyle\text{ \emph{which become paths} after removing the self-loops}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(j\rightsquigarrow i,\,\mathcal{S}):=

\displaystyle\bigcup\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{P}(j\xlongrightarrow{n}i,\,\mathcal{S})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\text{ all paths of arbitrary length from state $j\in\Omega$}

\displaystyle\text{\hskip 14.22636pt to state $i\in\Omega$ in the network $\mathcal{S}$ }

Clearly, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(j\xlongrightarrow{n}i,\,\mathcal{S})\subseteq\mathcal{WP}(j\xlongrightarrow{n}i,\,\mathcal{S})\subseteq\mathcal{W}(j\xlongrightarrow{n}i,\,\mathcal{S}).

A non-zero summand in the right hand side of Equation (
13
) can be interpreted as the weight
\gamma_{\tilde{\omega}}
of a walk
\tilde{\omega}\in\,\mathcal{W}(j\xlongrightarrow{n}i,\,\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{n})
of length
|\tilde{\omega}|=n
from
j
to
i
.

When we look only at the weight of a special walk

\displaystyle\tilde{\omega}\in\mathcal{WP}(j\xlongrightarrow{|\tilde{\omega}|}i,\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{n})\subseteq\mathcal{W}(j\xlongrightarrow{|\tilde{\omega}|}i,\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{n}),

then this weight can be separated into the weight of the corresponding
path

\omega\in\mathcal{P}(j\xlongrightarrow{|\omega|}i,\,\mathcal{S})
times a scaling factor
\left(\frac{t}{n}\right)^{|\omega|}
times the weight of the self-loops:

\displaystyle\gamma_{\tilde{\omega}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\gamma_{\tilde{\omega}_{\alpha}\to\,\tilde{\omega}_{\alpha+1}}=\underbrace{\prod\limits_{k=1}^{|\omega|}\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{\omega_{k}\to\omega_{k+1}}}_{=\,\left(\frac{t}{n}\right)^{|\omega|}\,\gamma_{\omega}\,}\cdot\underbrace{\prod\limits_{s\in\{\text{self-loops}(\tilde{\omega})\}}\left(1+\frac{t\,\Gamma_{(s,s)}}{n}\right)}_{\geq\,\left(1-\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{\to}\right)^{|\tilde{\omega}|-|\omega|}}\geq

(15)

\displaystyle\geq\gamma_{\omega}\,\left(\frac{t}{n}\right)^{|\omega|}\,\left(1-\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{\to}\right)^{|\tilde{\omega}|-|\omega|},

where the product is taken over all self-loops of
\tilde{\omega}
.

(a)

The
walk

\color[rgb]{0.6523,0,0.5195}\tilde{\omega}=(1,1,2,3,3,4)\in\mathcal{WP}(1\xlongrightarrow{|\tilde{\omega}|=5}4,\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{n})

3

4

{\color[rgb]{0.4453,0.0625,0.5234}\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{1\to 2}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{1\to 4}}
{\color[rgb]{0.4453,0.0625,0.5234}\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{2\to 3}}
{\color[rgb]{0.4453,0.0625,0.5234}\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{3\to 4}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{3\to 1}}
{\color[rgb]{0.4453,0.0625,0.5234}1+\frac{t}{n}\,\Gamma^{(1,1)}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1+\frac{t}{n}\,\Gamma^{(2,2)}}
{\color[rgb]{0.4453,0.0625,0.5234}1+\frac{t}{n}\,\Gamma^{(3,3)}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1+\frac{t}{n}\,\Gamma^{(4,4)}}

(b)

The
path

\color[rgb]{0.9023,0,0.1016}{\omega}=(1,2,3,4)\in\mathcal{P}(1\xlongrightarrow{|\omega|=3}4,\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{n})

and

.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.6641}1+\frac{t}{n}\,\Gamma^{(3,3)}}

Figure 5
:
Illustrating the separation of the weight of a
walk

\tilde{\omega}\in\mathcal{WP}(1\xlongrightarrow{|\tilde{\omega}|=5}4,\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{n})
into the weight of a
path

\omega\in\mathcal{P}(1\xlongrightarrow{|\omega|=3}4,\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{n})
and a product of the weights of self-loops.

For a fixed path
\omega=(1,2,3,4)\in\mathcal{P}(1\xlongrightarrow{|\omega|=3}4,\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{n})
, there are
\binom{|\tilde{\omega}|=5}{|\omega|=3}=10
many
\tilde{\omega}\in\mathcal{WP}(1\xlongrightarrow{|\tilde{\omega}|=5}4,\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{n})
that include the original path, with only additional self-loops, namely

\tilde{\omega}_{1}=(1,1,1,2,3,4)
,

\tilde{\omega}_{2}=(1,1,2,2,3,4)
,

\tilde{\omega}_{3}=(1,1,2,3,3,4)
,

\tilde{\omega}_{4}=(1,1,2,3,4,4)
,

\tilde{\omega}_{5}=(1,2,2,2,3,4)
,

\tilde{\omega}_{6}=(1,2,2,3,3,4)
,

\tilde{\omega}_{7}=(1,2,2,3,4,4)
,

\tilde{\omega}_{8}=(1,2,3,3,3,4)
,

\tilde{\omega}_{9}=(1,2,3,3,4,4)
,

and

\tilde{\omega}_{10}=(1,2,3,4,4,4)
.

For a given path
\omega\in\mathcal{P}(j\xlongrightarrow{|\omega|}i)
and some fixed natural number
|\tilde{\omega}|
greater or equal to
|\omega|
,
|\tilde{\omega}|\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq\,|\omega|}
, there are
\binom{|\tilde{\omega}|}{|\omega|}
many walks
\tilde{\omega}\in\mathcal{WP}(j\xlongrightarrow{|\tilde{\omega}|}i)
, which include the original path, but have additional self-loops (out of
|\tilde{\omega}|
many transitions, choose the positions of
|\omega|
non-trivial ones).

Now take
n\in\mathbb{N}
large enough, such that there exists a path
\omega_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(j\rightsquigarrow i,\mathcal{S})
with
|\omega_{0}|\leq n
.
Since we have:

\displaystyle\frac{n!}{(n-|\omega_{0}|)!\cdot n^{|\omega_{0}|}}

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{k=1}^{|\omega_{0}|-1}\left(1-\frac{k}{n}\right)\xlongrightarrow{n\to\infty}1\text{ , hence }\frac{n!}{(n-|\omega_{0}|)!\cdot n^{|\omega_{0}|}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\text{ , for $n$ large enough }\text{ and }

(16)

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{\to}\right)^{n-|\omega_{0}|}

\displaystyle\xlongrightarrow{n\to\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t\,\gamma_{\to}}\text{ , hence }\left(1-\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{\to}\right)^{n-|\omega_{0}|}\leq\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-t\,\gamma_{\to}}}{2}\text{ , for $n$ large enough, }

we can make the following estimation:

\displaystyle\left(\Bigl(\mathbb{1}+\frac{t\,\Gamma}{n}\Bigr)^{n}\right)^{(i,j)}

\displaystyle\text{}\hskip 11.38109pt=\sum\limits_{k_{1}\in\Omega}\cdots\sum\limits_{k_{n-1}\in\Omega}\left(\mathbb{1}+\frac{\Gamma\,t}{n}\right)^{(i,\,k_{1})}\cdot\dotsc\cdot\;\left(\mathbb{1}+\frac{\Gamma\,t}{n}\right)^{(k_{n-1},\,j)}

(17)

\displaystyle\text{}\hskip 11.38109pt=\sum\limits_{\tilde{\omega}\in\,\mathcal{W}\left(j\,\xlongrightarrow{n}\,i,\,\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{n}\right)}\,\gamma_{\tilde{\omega}}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathcal{W}\,\supseteq\,\mathcal{WP}}{\geq}\sum\limits_{\tilde{\omega}\in\,\mathcal{WP}\left(j\,\xlongrightarrow{n}\,i,\,\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{n}\right)}\,\underbrace{\hskip 28.45274pt\gamma_{\tilde{\omega}}\hskip 28.45274pt}_{\hskip 11.38109pt\geq\,\gamma_{\omega}\,\left(\frac{t}{n}\right)^{|\omega|}\,\left(1-\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{\to}\right)^{n-|\omega|}}

\displaystyle\text{}\hskip 5.69054pt\overset{\eqref{Estimation_gamma_omega_tilde}}{\geq}\sum\limits_{{\omega\in\,\mathcal{P}\left(j\,\rightsquigarrow\,i,\,\mathcal{S}\right)}\atop{|\omega|\leq n}}\,\binom{n}{|\omega|}\,\gamma_{\omega}\,\left(\frac{t}{n}\right)^{|\omega|}\,\left(1-\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{\to}\right)^{n-|\omega|}

\displaystyle\text{}\hskip 11.38109pt{\mathrel{\to}^{n\geq|\omega_{0}|}}\,\binom{n}{|\omega_{0}|}\,\gamma_{\omega_{0}}\,\left(\frac{t}{n}\right)^{|\omega_{0}|}\,\left(1-\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{\to}\right)^{n-|\omega_{0}|}

\displaystyle\text{}\hskip 11.38109pt{\mathrel{\to}}\underbrace{\frac{n!}{(n-|\omega_{0}|)!\,n^{|\omega_{0}|}}}_{\geq\,\frac{1}{2}}\,\,\frac{\gamma_{\omega_{0}}\,t^{|\omega_{0}|}}{|\omega_{0}|!}\,\underbrace{\left(1-\frac{t}{n}\,\gamma_{\to}\right)^{n-|\omega_{0}|}}_{\geq\,\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{e}^{-t\,\gamma_{\to}}}

\displaystyle\text{}\hskip 5.69054pt\overset{\eqref{Eq_SideCalculation_1}}{\geq}\,\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-t\,\gamma_{\to}}}{4}\,\frac{\gamma_{\omega_{0}}\,t^{|\omega_{0}|}}{|\omega_{0}|!}

\displaystyle\text{}\hskip 11.38109pt>\,0.

In the fourth step, we used both the estimate of Equation (
15
) and the fact that every ‘walk-path’ can be separated into a path and self-loops.

This concludes the proof.

4.4
Uniqueness and strict positivity of stationary solutions for irreducible networks

Lemma 6

(The kernel of the generator of an irreducible network)
.

Let
\Gamma
be as in equation (
generator
),
\|\Gamma\|_{1}^{\text{(op)}}<\infty
and let the semigroup
(\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma})_{t\geq 0}
be irreducible. Then the dimension of the kernel of the generator is at most one-dimensional, that is
\text{dim}(\text{kern }(\Gamma))\leq 1
. If the kernel is exactly one-dimensional, it is spanned by an - element-wise - strictly positive eigenvector, that is
\text{kern }(\Gamma)=\text{span }(\boldsymbol{X})
with
\boldsymbol{X}\in(\mathbb{R}_{>0})^{\Omega}
.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{X}\in l^{1}(\Omega)\cap\text{kern }(\Gamma-\lambda\,\mathbb{1})
be an eigenvector of
\Gamma
to an eigenvalue
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, that is
\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{X}=\lambda\,\boldsymbol{X}
. Then we have

\displaystyle|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda\,t}|\,\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda\,t}\boldsymbol{X}\|_{1}=\|\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\boldsymbol{X}\|_{1}=\sum\limits_{i\in\Omega}\left|\;\sum\limits_{j\in\Omega}\left(\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\right)^{(i,j)}\,X^{(j)}\;\right|\leq

(18)

\displaystyle\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,1}(*)}}{\leq}\sum\limits_{i,\,j\in\Omega}\left|\,\underbrace{\left(\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\right)^{(i,j)}}_{\geq\,0}\,\right|\cdot|X^{(j)}|=\sum\limits_{j\in\Omega}|X^{(j)}|\;\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{i\in\Omega}\left(\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\right)^{(i,j)}\right)}_{=\,1}=\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{1}.

If
\lambda=0
we have equality in (
18
), but on the other hand, we have equality in step
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,1}(*)
, if and only if for all
i\in\Omega

\displaystyle\Bigl(\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\bigr)^{(i,j)}\,X^{(j)}\Bigr)_{j\in\Omega}

\displaystyle\in\bigl(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\bigr)^{\Omega}\cup\bigl(\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\bigr)^{\Omega}

(19)

For an irreducible system, every element of the solution operator
\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}
is strictly positive, so we can deduce from equation (
19
) that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}

\displaystyle=(X^{(j)})_{j\in\Omega}\in\bigl(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\bigr)^{\Omega}\cup\bigl(\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\bigr)^{\Omega},

(20)

hence
\text{kern }(\Gamma)\subseteq\bigl(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\bigr)^{\Omega}\cup\bigl(\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\bigr)^{\Omega}
.
Since
\left(\bigl(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\bigr)^{\Omega}\cup\bigl(\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\bigr)^{\Omega}\right)\backslash\{\boldsymbol{0}\}
is not path-connected, we can deduce that the dimension of
\text{kern }(\Gamma)
must be (at most) one.

What is left to show, is that every element of
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}\in\text{kern }(\Gamma)\cap\mathcal{PV}
is
strictly positive
, so we have to rule out the case that there are vanishing entries. As we will see, this violates the assumption that the network is irreducible:

Let us define
S:=\{n\in\Omega\,:\,P_{*}^{(n)}=0\}\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{P}}(\Omega)
. Then we have for all
s\in S

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P})^{(s)}=\sum\limits_{j\in\Omega}P^{(j)}\,\gamma_{j\to s}-\underbrace{P^{(s)}}_{0}\,\gamma_{s\to j}

(21)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j\in\Omega\backslash S}\underbrace{P^{(j)}}_{>\,0}\,\gamma_{j\to s}

This means that for all
s\in S
and for all
j\in\Omega\backslash S
we have
\gamma_{j\to s}=0
. This makes the linear subspace associated to
S^{C}
an invariant subspace, since the generator can - after a suitable permutation - be brought into a triangular matrix. This implies
\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\in S^{C}
and hence
\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\in S^{C}
whenever
\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\in S^{C}
.

S
S^{C}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{n}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}*&*\\
\boldsymbol{0}&*\end{pmatrix},\,\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}=\begin{pmatrix}*&*\\
\boldsymbol{0}&*\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}\left(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}*\\
\boldsymbol{0}\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}}_{\begin{pmatrix}*&*\\
\boldsymbol{0}&*\end{pmatrix}}\,\underbrace{\boldsymbol{P}_{0}}_{\begin{pmatrix}*\\
\boldsymbol{0}\end{pmatrix}}=\begin{pmatrix}*\\
\boldsymbol{0}\end{pmatrix}.

Figure 6
:
The network
\Omega=S\dot{\cup}S^{C}
can be decomposed into two disjoint parts
S
and
S^{C}
, where there is no link from
S^{C}
to
S
. This results in a block-triangular form of both the generator
\Gamma
, as well as the solution operator
\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}
, making the network reducible.

∎

5
The thermodynamic limit for master equations

5.1
How to approximate infinite dimensional probability vectors by finite dimensional ones

For a given infinite dimensional probability vector
\boldsymbol{P}=\bigl(P^{(1)},P^{(2)},P^{(3)},\dots\bigr)\in\mathcal{PV}
, the problem is how to define a sequence
(\boldsymbol{p}_{n})_{n\in\Omega}\in\mathcal{PV}_{n}
such that
\boldsymbol{p}_{n}\xlongrightarrow{n\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}
, given that they are not even elements of the same vector space ?

A first idea could be, to ‘fill the sequence with zeros’, as depicted below.

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}_{2}

\displaystyle=\bigl(p_{2}^{(1)},p_{2}^{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}_{3}

\displaystyle=\bigl(p_{3}^{(1)},p_{3}^{(2)},p_{3}^{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}_{4}

\displaystyle=\bigl(p_{4}^{(1)},p_{4}^{(2)},p_{4}^{(3)},p_{4}^{(4)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\text{ with }\boldsymbol{p}_{n}

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{PV}_{n}:=\bigl(\mathbb{R}_{\geq\,0}\bigr)^{n}\cap B_{r=1}^{\|\cdot\|_{1}}(0)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}_{2}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{p}_{2}\times\{0\}^{\Omega}=\bigl(p_{2}^{(1)},p_{2}^{(2)},0,\dots\bigr)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}_{3}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{p}_{3}\times\{0\}^{\Omega}=\bigl(p_{3}^{(1)},p_{3}^{(2)},p_{3}^{(3)},0,\dots\bigr)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}_{4}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{p}_{4}\times\{0\}^{\Omega}=\bigl(p_{4}^{(1)},p_{4}^{(2)},p_{4}^{(3)},p_{4}^{(4)},0,\dots\bigr)

\displaystyle\text{ with }\boldsymbol{P}_{n}

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{PV}_{0}:=\bigl(\mathbb{R}_{\geq\,0}\bigr)^{n}\cap B_{r=1}^{\|\cdot\|_{1}}(0)

This solves the problem of different vector spaces, but as it turns out,
nets
are better suited than
sequences
for describing the transition from finite- to infinite dimensions.

Therefore, we define the set of all finite subsets of
\Omega
as
\text{Fin}(\Omega):=\{F\subseteq\Omega\,:\,|F|<\infty\}
. For some fixed
F\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)
the probability sequence
\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}\in\mathcal{PV}_{0}
could look as follows:

F

\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}

\{1,2,3\}

\bigl(P^{(1)},P^{(2)},P^{(3)},0,0,,0,\dots\bigr)

\{1,4,5\}

\bigl(P^{(1)},0,0,P^{(4)},P^{(5)},0,\dots\bigr)

This definition makes
\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}
a probability sequence, that is effectively finite dimensional.

We note that the element-wise definition
\left(P^{[F]}\right)^{(n)}:=\frac{P^{(n)}}{\sum\limits_{f\in F}P^{(f)}}
requires that the denominator does not vanish.

We therefore define for some fixed probability sequence
\boldsymbol{P}\in\mathcal{PV}
the set of null sets of the corresponding measure, that is

Definition 7

(
the set of null sets
)
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{P}}

\displaystyle:=\{N\subseteq\Omega\,:\,\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}\bigr)(N):=\sum\limits_{n\in N}P^{(n)}=0\}.

Then we can define
\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}
as follows:

Definition 8

(
the probability net
\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}

)
.

Then for any
F\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)\backslash\,\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{P}}
we can define
\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}
entry-wise as

\displaystyle\left(P^{[F]}\right)^{(n)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{P^{(n)}}{\sum\limits_{f\in F}P^{(f)}}

(ProbSeq-FinSubNet)

However, since the solution
\boldsymbol{P}^{F}\bigl(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\bigr)=\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma^{[F]}}\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}
depends on the generator, we need a good definition for
\Gamma^{[F]}
.

5.2
Definition of finite subnetworks and their corresponding generators

Following the arguments made above, it makes sense to define
\Gamma^{[F]}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Omega\times\Omega}
as a double sequence, where all but finitely many entries vanish:

\displaystyle\Gamma^{[F]}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Omega\times\Omega}\text{, with }\left(\Gamma^{[F]}\right)^{(i,\,j)}\neq 0\Longrightarrow i,j\in F.

(22)

This ensures that the states of the original, infinite dimensional network are always aligned with the component of the finite subnetwork.

The first idea on how to define
\Gamma^{[F]}
would be to introduce a sharp cutoff, namely

\displaystyle\left(\widetilde{\Gamma^{[F]}}\right)^{(i,\,j)}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(\Gamma)^{(i,\,j)}&\text{ , if }i,j\in F\\
0&\text{ , else}.\end{cases}

(23)

However, this could make
\widetilde{\Gamma^{[F]}}
loose the property of being the generator of a continuous-time Markov chain (in particular, the column-sum would not necessarily equal zero). In the following, we need the definition of
subnetworks
:

Definition 9

(
Subnetworks
)
.

We call a network
\mathcal{S}_{F}=(\Omega_{F},\mathcal{E}_{F})
a
subnetwork
of
\mathcal{S}=(\Omega,\mathcal{E})
if it contains
some
of its states and
all
the original links between those states, that is:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{F}\subseteq\mathcal{S}\iff\Omega_{F}

\displaystyle\subseteq\Omega\text{ and }

(24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{F}

\displaystyle:=\{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}\,:\,i,j\in\Omega_{F}\}.

(a)

1
2
3
\gamma_{1\to 2}
\gamma_{2\to 1}
\gamma_{1\to 3}
\gamma_{3\to 2}

(b)

1
2
\gamma_{1\to 2}
\gamma_{2\to 1}

(c)

1
2
3
\gamma_{1\to 3}
\gamma_{3\to 2}

Figure 7
:
Illustrating the concept of subnetworks: With Figure
7(a)
being the original network, Figure
7(b)
is a subnetwork, according to definition
9
, while Figure
7(c)
is not.

For a finite number of state
F\subseteq\Omega
, we define
\Gamma^{[F]}
as the generator of the corresponding
subnetwork

\mathcal{S}_{F}
, that is :

\displaystyle\left(\Gamma^{[F]}\right)^{(i,\,j)}

\displaystyle:=\left(\Gamma^{[\mathcal{S}_{F}]}\right)^{(i,\,j)}=\begin{cases}(\Gamma)^{(i,\,j)}&\text{ , if }i\neq j,\,i,j\in F\\
-\sum\limits_{f\in F}\Gamma^{(f,j)}&\text{ , if }i=j,\,i,j\in F\\
0&\text{ , else}\\
\end{cases}

(GenFinSubNet)

\displaystyle=(1-\delta_{i,j})\,\Gamma^{(i,j)}\,1_{F}(i)\,1_{F}(j)-\delta_{i,j}\,\sum\limits_{f\in F}\Gamma^{(f,j)},

which ensure that the generator property is being preserved (compare equation (
master eq.
)):

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-\gamma_{1\to 2}-\gamma_{1\to 3}&\gamma_{2\to 1}&0\\
\gamma_{1\to 2}&-\gamma_{2\to 1}&\gamma_{3\to 2}\\
\gamma_{1\to 3}&0&-\gamma_{3\to 2}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\text{ $\dots$ generator of the network in figure \ref{Illustratin_SubNetworks_a}},

(25)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma^{[\{1,2\}]}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-\gamma_{1\to 2}-\gamma_{1\to 3}&\gamma_{2\to 1}&0\\
\gamma_{1\to 2}&-\gamma_{2\to 1}&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\text{ $\dots$ is NOT a generator matrix }

\displaystyle\Gamma^{[\{1,2\}]}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-\gamma_{1\to 2}&\gamma_{2\to 1}&0\\
\gamma_{1\to 2}&-\gamma_{2\to 1}&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\text{ $\dots$ is a generator matrix }

Given an initial probability sequence
\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{PV}
and a finite subset
F\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)
with
\boldsymbol{P}_{0}(F)>0
, we define the initial state
\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}
- like in equation (
ProbSeq-FinSubNet
) - as follows:

\displaystyle\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\bigr)^{(i)}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\frac{P_{0}^{(i)}}{\sum\limits_{f\in F}P^{(f)}_{0}}&\text{ , if }i\in F\\
0,&\text{ else}.\end{cases}

(InitialStateFinSubNet)

The probability sequence
\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0})\in\left(\mathbb{R}_{\geq\,0}\right)^{\Omega}
for
F\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)\backslash\,\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{P}_{0}}
is then defined as the solution of the following initial value problem:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t)

\displaystyle=\Gamma^{[F]}\,\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t=0)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}.

\iff

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}^{F}\left(t\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\right)=\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma^{[F]}}\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}

(MEqFinSubNet)

Since only finitely many entries are unequal to zero, the problem is effectively finite dimensional.

The following table gives some examples of truncations of generators and probability vectors of the original network, given in figure

.

5.3
Approximating the system with finite subnetworks

Two finite subsets
A,B\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)
with
A\cancel{\subseteq}B
and
B\cancel{\subseteq}A
cannot directly be compared, due to the fact that the set of all finite subsets has no
total order
but only a
half order
, the set of probability sequences
\left(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}\right)_{F\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)}
is no sequence, but a
net
in the topological sense and the thermodynamic limit is a convergence of nets.

We say that the probability vector
\boldsymbol{P}^{F}\left(t\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\right)
converges in the thermodynamic limit to the probability vector probability vector
\boldsymbol{P}\left(t\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\right)
of the infinite dimensional system
\Omega
, if for all
\epsilon
larger than zero, there exists a finite subset
F_{\epsilon}\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)
such that for all finite subsets
F\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)
larger than
F_{\epsilon}
, the corresponding probability vector
\boldsymbol{P}^{F}\left(t\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\right)
for the finite subsystem differs from the probability vector of the whole system only by a number
\epsilon
with respect to the
1
-norm:

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}^{F}\left(t\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\right):=\lim\limits_{F\in\text{Fin}({\Omega})}\boldsymbol{P}^{F}\left(t\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\right)=\boldsymbol{P}\left(t\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\right)\overset{\text{def}}{\iff}

(ThermodLimit)

\displaystyle\forall\epsilon>0\;\;\exists\,F_{\epsilon}\in\text{Fin}({\Omega})\,:\,\forall F\in\text{Fin}({\Omega}),\,F\supseteq F_{\epsilon}\;\;\;\left\|\boldsymbol{P}\left(t\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\right)-\boldsymbol{P}^{F}\left(t\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\right)\right\|_{1}<\epsilon

For better readability we write “
\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}
” instead of “
\lim\limits_{F\in\text{Fin}({\Omega})}
”, but emphasize that is a convergence of nets.

Theorem 10

(Thermodynamic Limit for master equations)
.

For bounded generators
(\|\Gamma\|_{1,1}^{\text{(op)}}<\infty)
the solution of the master equation (
master eq.
) for a countable, infinite dimensional system
\boldsymbol{P}\left(t\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\right)
at a given time
t>0
can be approximated by finite subsystems in the thermodynamic limit of definition (
ThermodLimit
).

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t)

\displaystyle=\Gamma^{[F]}\,\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t=0)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}.

\iff

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}^{F}\left(t\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\right)=\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\underbrace{\frac{d}{d\tau}\,\boldsymbol{P}^{F}\left(\tau\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\right)}_{\Gamma^{[F]}\,\boldsymbol{P}^{F}\left(\tau\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\right)}\text{ d}\tau

For two, large enough, finite subnetworks
F_{1},\,F_{2}\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)
, we have:

\displaystyle u(t)

\displaystyle:=\Bigl\|\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{1}}\left(t\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{1}]}\right)-\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{2}}\left(t\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{2}]}\right)\Bigr\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{1}]}-\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{2}]}+\mathop{\text{\Large$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\kern-1.63885pt0$}}^{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle t$}}$}}\nolimits\text{ d}\tau\left(\Gamma^{[F_{1}]}\,\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{1}}\left(\tau\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{1}]}\right)\right)-\left(\Gamma^{[F_{2}]}\,\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{2}}\left(\tau\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{2}]}\right)\right)\pm\left(\Gamma^{[F_{1}]}\,\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{2}}\left(\tau\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{2}]}\right)\right)\right\|_{1}\leq

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{1}]}-\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{2}]}\right\|_{1}+\mathop{\text{\Large$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\kern-1.63885pt0$}}^{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle t$}}$}}\nolimits\left(\underbrace{\left\|\left(\Gamma^{[F_{1}]}-\Gamma^{[F_{2}]}\right)\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{2}}\left(\tau\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{2}]}\right)\right\|_{1}}_{\leq\,\left\|\Gamma^{[F_{1}]}-\Gamma^{[F_{2}]}\right\|_{1,1}^{\text{(op)}}\,\left\|\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{2}}\left(\tau\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{2}]}\right)\right\|_{1}}\right)\text{ d}\tau

\displaystyle+\mathop{\text{\Large$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\kern-1.63885pt0$}}^{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle t$}}$}}\nolimits\left(\underbrace{\left\|\Gamma^{[F_{1}]}\,\left(\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{1}}\left(\tau\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{1}]}\right)-\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{2}}\left(\tau\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{2}]}\right)\right)\right\|_{1}}_{\leq\,\left\|\Gamma^{[F_{1}]}\right\|_{1,1}^{\text{(op)}}\,\left\|\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{1}}\left(\tau\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{1}]}\right)-\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{2}}\left(\tau\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{2}]}\right)\right\|_{1}}\right)\text{ d}\tau\leq

\displaystyle\overset{\text{lemma }\ref{Lemma_Comparing_1NormOfMatrices_with_Vector1NormOfMatrices}}{\leq}\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{1}]}-\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{2}]}\right\|_{1}+\mathop{\text{\Large$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\kern-1.63885pt0$}}^{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle t$}}$}}\nolimits\left\|\Gamma^{[F_{1}]}-\Gamma^{[F_{2}]}\right\|_{1,1}^{\text{(op)}}\,\underbrace{\left\|\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{2}}\left(\tau\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{2}]}\right)\right\|_{1}}_{1}\text{ d}\tau

\displaystyle+\mathop{\text{\Large$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\kern-1.63885pt0$}}^{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle t$}}$}}\nolimits\left\|\Gamma^{[F_{1}]}\right\|_{1,1}^{\text{(op)}}\,\left\|\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{1}}\left(\tau\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{1}]}\right)-\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{2}}\left(\tau\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{2}]}\right)\right\|_{1}\text{ d}\tau

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{1}]}-\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{2}]}\right\|_{1}+t\,\left\|\Gamma^{[F_{1}]}-\Gamma^{[F_{2}]}\right\|_{1,1}^{\text{(op)}}}_{=:\alpha(t)}+\mathop{\text{\Large$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\kern-1.63885pt0$}}^{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle t$}}$}}\nolimits\underbrace{\left\|\Gamma^{[F_{1}]}\right\|_{1,1}^{\text{(op)}}}_{\beta}\,\underbrace{\left\|\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{1}}\left(\tau\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{1}]}\right)-\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{2}}\left(\tau\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{2}]}\right)\right\|_{1}\text{ d}\tau}_{u(\tau)}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\text{lemma \ref{Lemma_Groenwall_IntegralVersion}}}_{\text{Groenwall}}}

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{1}}\left(t\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{1}]}\right)-\boldsymbol{P}^{F_{2}}\left(t\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{2}]}\right)\Bigr\|_{1}=u(t)\leq\alpha\;\mathrm{e}^{\int_{0}^{t}\beta}=

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\,\underbrace{\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{1}]}-\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F_{2}]}\right\|_{1}}_{\,<\,\epsilon}+t\,\underbrace{\left\|\Gamma^{[F_{1}]}-\Gamma^{[F_{2}]}\right\|_{1,1}^{\text{(op)}}\,}_{\,<\,\epsilon}\Bigr)\,\cdot\,\underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{t\,\left\|\Gamma^{[F_{1}]}\right\|_{1,1}^{\text{(op)}}}}_{\leq\,\mathrm{e}^{t\,\left\|\Gamma\right\|_{1,1}^{\text{(op)}}}}

\displaystyle\leq\,\epsilon\,(1+t)\,\mathrm{e}^{t\,\left\|\Gamma\right\|_{1,1}^{\text{(op)}}}.

We note that this convergence is not uniform in
t
.

∎

Remark 11
.

We note that, while
\|\Gamma\|_{1,1}^{\text{(op)}}<\infty
is a sufficient criterion, it suffices that
\gamma_{n\to}\xlongrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
.

5.4
The appropriate norm for the generator

A suitable norm
\|\cdot\|_{\text{gen}}
for the generator matrix
\Gamma
must satisfy two requirements:

i)

The norm “
\|\cdot\|_{\text{gen}}
” must be compatible with the
1
-norm of
\mathbb{R}^{\Omega}
, since that property was used in theorem
10
.

ii)

The generators of finite subnetworks
\Gamma^{[F]}
must be able to approximate the full generator
\Gamma
with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{\text{gen}}
.

Since the
\|\cdot\|_{1,1}^{\text{(op)}}
-norm fulfills both properties (compare lemma
48
and lemma
49
), we additionally require in the definition of a master equation (
master eq.
) that

\displaystyle\infty

\displaystyle>\|\Gamma\|_{1,1}^{\text{(op)}}:=\sum\limits_{i,j\in\Omega}|\Gamma^{(i,j)}|=2\,\sum\limits_{j\in\Omega}\gamma_{j\to}=2\,\|(\gamma_{j\to})_{j\in\Omega}\|_{1}.

(26)

This makes
\Gamma
both a continuous operator on
l^{1}(\Omega)
, as well as the generator of the uniformly continuous semigroup
\left(\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\right)_{t\geq 0}
, which is defined via its power series:

\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{(t\,\Gamma)^{k}}{k!}.

(27)

5.5
Comparison with other truncation methods

In the following section we will briefly discuss two other truncation methods, as opposed to our method of finite subsystems, presented in section
5.2
.

The essential - and perhaps philosophical - question, is, what is meant by the term ‘approximating an unknown object by a sequence of known objects’. The way we understand it, is that the sequence of known objects have properties that are eventually similar to that of the unknown object, not only in the limit case.

We briefly show why two alternative truncation methods fall short of these requirements.

5.5.1
The truncation method ‘sharp cutoff ’

As briefly mentioned above, a natural method for approximating the solution of linear differential equations on Banach spaces, is introducing a sharp cutoff of the generator, namely

\displaystyle\left(\Gamma_{\text{sharp cutoff}}^{[\mathbb{N}_{\leq N}]}\right)^{(i,\,j)}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}(\Gamma)^{(i,\,j)}&\text{ , if }i,j\in F\\
0&\text{ , else}.\end{cases}

(28)

This procedure can result in generators, which do not describe master equation of a finite state space, as shown in equation (
25
).

In case of a sharp cutoff, we have an outflow of probability of the system, as the following calculation shows:

\displaystyle\frac{\text{ d}}{\text{ d}t}\sum\limits_{i\in F}p^{(i)}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in F}\underbrace{\frac{\text{ d}}{\text{ d}t}p^{(i)}(t)}_{\sum\limits_{a\in F}p^{(a)}(t)\,\gamma_{a\to i}-\sum\limits_{j\in\Omega}p^{(i)}(t)\,\gamma_{i\to j}}

(29)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{a\in F}\sum\limits_{i\in F}p^{(a)}(t)\,\gamma_{a\to i}-\sum\limits_{i\in F}p^{(i)}(t)\,\gamma_{i\to}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{a\in F}p^{(a)}(t)\,\gamma_{a\to}-\sum\limits_{\alpha\in F}p^{(\alpha)}(t)\,\gamma_{\alpha\to}\right)}_{0}-\sum\limits_{\beta\in\Omega\backslash F}p^{(\beta)}(t)\,\gamma_{\beta\to}

\displaystyle\leq 0.

\displaystyle\Gamma=\begin{pmatrix}-\gamma_{1\to 2}-\gamma_{1\to 3}&\gamma_{2\to 1}&0\\
\gamma_{1\to 2}&-\gamma_{2\to 1}&\gamma_{3\to 2}\\
\gamma_{1\to 3}&0&-\gamma_{3\to 2}\end{pmatrix}

1
2
3
\gamma_{1\to 2}
\gamma_{2\to 1}
\gamma_{1\to 3}
\gamma_{3\to 2}

(a)

Generator matrix with its corresponding network
\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle\Gamma=\begin{pmatrix}-\gamma_{1\to 2}{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0,0.1172}-\gamma_{1\to 3}}&\gamma_{2\to 1}&0\\
\gamma_{1\to 2}&-\gamma_{2\to 1}&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}

1
2
\gamma_{1\to 2}
\gamma_{2\to 1}
\gamma_{1\to 3}

(b)

The generator matrix
after
a ‘sharp cutoff’ looses the properties of a generator and does no longer correspond to a meaningful network

Figure 9
:
Illustrating the fact that a sharp cutoff of the generator results in a matrix, which can not be interpreted as a generator for a network

This means that we can non longer interpret the entries
P^{(i)}
as probabilities, which results in qualitative different behavior between the infinite system and finite cutoffs, no matter how large.

5.5.2
The truncation method ‘condense to some state’

Another possible truncation method is to consider a finite set
F\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)
and to ‘condense’ the remaining - infinitely many - states in to a single state ‘remainder’:

\displaystyle\gamma_{a\to\text{rem}}

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{\beta\in F^{C}}\gamma_{a\to\beta}

(30)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\text{rem}\to a}

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{\beta\in F^{C}}\gamma_{\beta\to a}

While this is feasible and in some cases - as the linear chain on the natural numbers, compare figure
10
- equivalent to our truncation method, it can also create totally different topologies, as depicted in figure
11
.

(a)

0
1
2
\dots
N-1
\;\;\;N\;\;\;
N+1
\dots
\gamma_{0\to 1}
\gamma_{1\to 2}
\gamma_{N-1\to N}
\gamma_{N\to N+1}
\gamma_{2\to 1}
\gamma_{1\to 0}
\gamma_{N+1\to N}
\gamma_{N\to N-1}

(b)

0
1

\dots
N-1
\;\;\;N\;\;\;
remainder
\gamma_{0\to 1}
\gamma_{1\to 2}
\gamma_{N-1\to N}
\gamma_{N\to\text{rem}}
\gamma_{2\to 1}
\gamma_{1\to 0}
\gamma_{\text{rem}\to N}
\gamma_{N\to N-1}

Figure 10
:
Condensing the remainder of a network into a single state, can lead to the same result as the method of finite subsystems

(a)

(b)
