\gamma_{-N+1\to-N}

\gamma_{-N\to\text{rem}}

Figure 11
:
The truncation method ‘condensing the remainder into a single state’ can also lead to a totally different topology compared to the original network, as the linear chain on the integers shows

Since different topologies naturally result in different dynamics - and hence different - long-term behavior, we cannot hope to capture the essential dynamics of the infinite system by a finite, truncated system. The truncation method of condensing the remainder part of a network into a single state seems unfeasible.

6
Markov chains: Existence and uniqueness of stationary solutions

In the following section we look at stationary solutions for both discrete- and continuous-time Markov chains. While an irreducible network guarantees the
uniquenes
of a stationary solution,
positive recurrence
- on an irreducible network - is equivalent to its existence. This is done by writing the
expected visiting numbers
for each state in a vector and showing that this vector is - after normalization - the only candidate for a stationary solution.

6.1
Discrete-time Markov chains

Definition 12

(Return time for DTMC (DTMC))
.

For a discrete-time Markov chain (
DTMC
)
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
starting from a state
X_{0}=\omega_{0}\in\Omega
(that is
\mathcal{P}(X_{0}=\omega_{0})=1
), we define the
return time

T_{R}\in\mathbb{N}
as follows:

\displaystyle T_{R}

\displaystyle:=T_{R}^{[DTMC]}\bigl(\omega_{0}\bigr)

\displaystyle:=\inf\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\{X_{k}=\omega_{0}=X_{0}\}

(return time, DTMC)

with
\inf(\emptyset):=\infty
. Apparently we have
X_{0}=\omega_{0}=X_{T_{R}}
.

Definition 13

((Positive) recurrence for DTMC )
.

A DTMC is called
recurrent
, if it almost surely returns to the initial state, that is

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}(T_{R}<\infty)=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{P}\left(T_{R}=n\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right)

(31)

A DTMC is called
positive recurrent
, if it is recurrent and its expectation value is finite:

\displaystyle\infty

\displaystyle>E[T_{R},|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}]=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}n\,\cdot\,\mathcal{P}\left(T_{R}=n\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right)=\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{P}(T_{R}\geq n)

(32)

Definition 14

(Expected visiting number for DTMC)
.

Further, we define for a DTMC starting at state
\omega_{0}\in\Omega
the
expected visiting number

N^{(\omega)}
to state
\omega\in\Omega
, before the return time
T_{R}
, namely

\displaystyle N^{(\omega)}

\displaystyle:=E\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{T_{R}-1}1_{\{X_{k}=\omega\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\}}\right]=E\left[\sum\limits_{k=1}^{T_{R}}1_{\{X_{k}=\omega\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\}}\right]=E\left[\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}1_{\{X_{k}=\omega,k\leq T_{R}\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\}}\right]

(expected visiting number)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\text{monotone}}_{\text{convergence}}}\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\,\underbrace{E\left[1_{\{X_{k}=\omega,\,T_{R}\geq k\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\}}\right]}_{\mathcal{P}\left(X_{k}=\omega,\,T_{R}\geq k\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right)}=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\,\mathcal{P}\left(X_{k}=\omega,\,T_{R}\geq k\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right)\geq 0.

We point out that for the initial state
\omega_{0}
we have
N^{(\omega_{0})}=1
.

The reason for these two definitions is that they appear in the definition of the expected return time:

Lemma 15

(Expected return time and expected visiting number)
.

The expected return time equals the sum over all states of the expected visiting number of that state, that is
E\left[T_{R}\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right]=\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}\,N^{(\omega)}
.

Proof.

\displaystyle E\left[T_{R}\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\,\underbrace{\mathcal{P}\left(T_{R}\geq n\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right)}_{\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}\mathcal{P}\left(T_{R}\geq n,\,X_{n}=\omega\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right)}=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\,\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}}_{\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}\,\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}\,\underbrace{\mathcal{P}\left(T_{R}\geq n,\,X_{n}=\omega\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right)}_{E\left[1_{\{T_{R}\geq n,\,X_{n}=\omega\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\}}\right]}

(expected return time)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\text{monotone}}_{\text{convergence}}}\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}\,E\left[\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}1_{\{T_{R}\geq n,X_{n}=\omega\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\}}\right]=\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}\,\underbrace{E\left[\sum\limits_{n=1}^{T_{R}}1_{\{X_{n}=\omega\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\}}\right]}_{N^{(\omega)}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}\,N^{(\omega)}=\left\|\underbrace{\left(N^{(\omega)}\right)_{\omega\in\Omega}}_{\boldsymbol{N}}\right\|_{1}=\left\|\boldsymbol{N}\right\|_{1}.

∎

Theorem 16

(Vector of expected visiting numbers is candidate for stationary solution of DTMC )
.

For an irreducible DTMC, every entry of
\boldsymbol{N}
is strictly positive and the eigenspace of the transition matrix
Q
to the eigenvalue
\lambda=1
is spanned by
\frac{\boldsymbol{N}}{\|\boldsymbol{N}\|_{1}}
. This means that
\frac{\boldsymbol{N}}{\|\boldsymbol{N}\|_{1}}
is the only possible probability vector that is a stationary solution of the Markov chain, that is

\displaystyle\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q)

\displaystyle=\text{span }\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{N}}{\|\boldsymbol{N}\|_{1}}\right)\text{and }\boldsymbol{N}\in(\mathbb{R}_{>\,0})^{\Omega}\text{ , that is }

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{\boldsymbol{N}}{\left\|\boldsymbol{N}\right\|_{1}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q)\cap\mathcal{PV}\cap(\mathbb{R}_{>\,0})^{\Omega}&\text{, if }\|\boldsymbol{N}\|_{1}<\infty\text{ OR}\\
\{\boldsymbol{0}\}&\text{, if }\|\boldsymbol{N}\|_{1}=\infty\end{cases}

Proof.

We know from section
4.4
that the dimension of the kernel of
Q-1\,\cdot\mathbb{1}
is at most one dimensional. On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\left(Q\,\boldsymbol{N}\right)^{(\beta)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\Omega}\underbrace{Q^{(\beta,\alpha)}}_{q_{\alpha\to\beta}}\,N^{(\alpha)}=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\Omega}\underbrace{N^{(\alpha)}}_{\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\,\mathcal{P}\left(X_{k}=\alpha,\,T_{R}>k\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right)}\,q_{\alpha\to\beta}=

(33)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\Omega}\,\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\,}_{\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\,\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\Omega}\,,}\mathcal{P}\left(X_{k}=\alpha,\,T_{R}>k\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right)\;\underbrace{\overbrace{\mathcal{P}\left(X_{k+1}=\beta\,|\,X_{k}=\alpha\right)}^{q_{\alpha\to\beta}}}_{\mathcal{P}\left(X_{k+1}=\beta\,|\,X_{k}=\alpha,\,X_{0}=\omega_{0},\,T_{R}>k\right)}=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\text{Markov}}_{\text{chain}}}\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\,\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\Omega}\,\underbrace{\mathcal{P}\left(X_{k}=\alpha,\,T_{R}>k\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right)\,\mathcal{P}\left(X_{k+1}=\beta\,|\,X_{k}=\alpha,\,X_{0}=\omega_{0},\,T_{R}>k\right)}_{\mathcal{P}\left(X_{k}=\alpha,\,X_{k+1}=\beta,\,T_{R}>k\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right)}=

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\,\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\Omega}\,\mathcal{P}\left(X_{k}=\alpha,\,X_{k+1}=\beta,\,T_{R}>k\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right)}_{\mathcal{P}\left(X_{k+1}=\beta,\,\underbrace{T_{R}>k}_{T_{R}\geq k+1}\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right)}=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\text{index shift}}_{j=k+1}}\sum\limits_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\,\mathcal{P}\left(X_{j}=\beta,\,T_{R}\geq j\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right)=

\displaystyle=N^{(\beta)}\text{, that is }

\displaystyle Q\,\boldsymbol{N}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{N}.

Since the Markov chain is irreducible, there exist two natural numbers
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\left(Q^{m}\right)^{(\alpha,\omega_{0})}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}\left(X_{m}=\alpha\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right)>0,

(34)

\displaystyle\left(Q^{k}\right)^{(\omega_{0},\alpha)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}\left(X_{k}=\omega_{0}\,|\,X_{0}=\alpha\right)>0

(35)

Since
Q\,\boldsymbol{N}=\boldsymbol{N}
implies
Q^{k}\,\boldsymbol{N}=\boldsymbol{N}
, we have:

\displaystyle N^{(\alpha)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}\left(Q^{m}\right)^{(\alpha,\omega)}\,N^{(\omega)}\geq\underbrace{\left(Q^{m}\right)^{(\alpha,\omega_{0})}}_{>0}\,\underbrace{N^{(\omega_{0})}}_{1}\;\overset{\eqref{Q_m_alpha_omega0_0}}{>}\;0\text{ and }

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=N^{(\omega_{0})}=\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}\,\left(Q^{k}\right)^{(\omega_{0},\omega)}\,N^{(\omega)}\geq\underbrace{\left(Q^{k}\right)^{(\omega_{0},\alpha)}}_{>\,0}\,N^{(\alpha)}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow N^{(\alpha)}

\displaystyle\overset{\bigg\downarrow}{\leq}\frac{1}{\left(Q^{k}\right)^{(\omega_{0},\alpha)}}\;\overset{\eqref{Q_m_omega0_alpha_0}}{<}\;\infty.

Uniqueness follow from the fact that for irreducible Markov chains we have
\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q)\leq 1
(compare section
4.4
).

∎

Lemma 17
.

For an irreducible, DTMC the following are equivalent:

(i)

All
states in
\Omega
are
positive recurrent

(ii)

There exists a
single
state
\omega_{0}\in\Omega
which is positive recurrent

(iii)

There exists a
stationary solution

Proof.

clear

By assumption we have:

\displaystyle\infty

\displaystyle>E[T_{R}\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}]{\mathrel{\to}^{\eqref{Eq_DTMC_ExpectedReturnTime}}}\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}N^{(\omega)}=\|\boldsymbol{N}\|_{1}

(36)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\left(\frac{N^{(\omega)}}{\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\Omega}N^{(\alpha)}}\right)_{\omega\in\Omega}=\frac{\boldsymbol{N}}{\left\|\boldsymbol{N}\right\|_{1}}\text{is a stationary probability vector by lemma \ref{Thm_VectorOfExpectedVisitingNumbersIsCandidateForStationarySolutionOfDTMC} }

If there exists an invariant probability distribution, by lemma
16
it must be of the form
\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{N}}{\left\|\boldsymbol{N}\right\|_{1}}\right)_{\omega\in\Omega}
. Hence, we have for all states
\omega_{0}\in\Omega

\displaystyle\infty>\left\|\boldsymbol{N}\right\|_{1}=\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}N^{(\omega)}=E\left[T_{R}\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right].

(37)

∎

6.2
Continuous-time Markov chains

Definition 18

(Return time for CTMC)
.

For a continuous-time Markov chain (
CTMC
)
\left(X_{t}\right)_{t\geq 0}
starting from a state
X_{0}=\omega_{0}
(that is
\mathcal{P}(X_{0}=\omega_{0})=1
), we define the
return time

t_{R}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
as follows:

\displaystyle t_{R}

\displaystyle:=t_{R}^{[CTMC]}\bigl(\omega_{0}\bigr)

\displaystyle:=\inf\limits_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\{X_{t}=\omega_{0}=X_{0}\,:\,\exists\tau\in(0,t)\,:\,X_{\tau}\neq\omega_{0}\}.

(return time, CTMC)

Again we have
X_{0}=\omega_{0}=X_{t_{R}}
.

In analogy to the discrete-time case, we define recurrence and positive recurrence for a CTMC.

Definition 19

((Positive) recurrence for CTMC )
.

A CTMC is called
recurrent
, if it almost surely returns to the initial state, that is

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}(t_{R}<\infty)=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\rho_{t_{R}}(\tau)\,\text{ d}\tau.

(38)

A CTMC is called
positive recurrent
, if it is recurrent and its expectation value is finite:

\displaystyle\infty

\displaystyle>E[t_{R}\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}]=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\tau\,\rho_{t_{R}}(\tau)\,\text{ d}\tau=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{P}(t_{R}\geq\tau)\,\text{ d}\tau.

(39)

Definition 20

(Expected visiting time for CTMC)
.

In analogy to the
expected visiting number
in DTMC, we define the
expected visiting time
for CTMC as

\displaystyle T^{(\omega)}

\displaystyle:=E\left[\int\limits_{0}^{t_{R}}1_{\{X_{t}=\omega\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\}}\text{ d}\,t\right].

(expected visiting time)

As in the case of (DTMC), the expected visiting number appears in the expected return time:

\displaystyle E\left[t_{R}\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\,\underbrace{\mathcal{P}\left(t_{R}>t\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right)}_{\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}\mathcal{P}\left(t_{R}>t,\,X_{t}=\omega\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right)}\text{ d}\,t=\underbrace{\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\,\text{ d}t\,\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}}_{\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}\,\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\,\text{ d}t\,}\,\underbrace{\mathcal{P}\left(t_{R}>t,\,X_{t}=\omega\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right)}_{E\left[1_{\{t_{R}>t,\,X_{t}=\omega\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\}}\right]}

(40)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}\,\underbrace{E\left[\int\limits_{0}^{t_{R}}1_{\{X_{n}=\omega\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\}}\right]}_{T^{(\omega)}}=\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}\,T^{(\omega)}=\left\|\underbrace{\left(T^{(\omega)}\right)_{\omega\in\Omega}}_{\boldsymbol{T}}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\|\boldsymbol{T}\right\|_{1}.

Lemma 21

(Expected visiting
time
of CTMC and expected visiting
number
of DTMC)
.

Let
T^{(\omega)}
be the expected waiting
time
of the CTMC and
N^{(\omega)}
the expected visiting
number
of the embedded, DTMC. Then we have:

\displaystyle T^{(\omega)}

\displaystyle=N^{(\omega)}\,\cdot\,\frac{1}{\gamma_{\omega\to}}.

(41)

Proof.

\displaystyle T^{(\omega)}

\displaystyle:=E\left[\underbrace{\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}1_{\{X_{t}=\omega,t_{R}\geq t\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\}}\text{ d}\,t}_{\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}1_{\{X_{t_{k}}=\omega,T_{R}\geq k\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\}}\,\cdot\,(t_{k+1}-t_{k})}\right]=

(42)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\text{monotone}}_{\text{convergence}}}\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\,\underbrace{E\left[1_{\{X_{t_{k}}=\omega,T_{R}\geq k\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\}}\,\cdot\,(t_{k+1}-t_{k})\right]}_{E\left[1_{\{X_{t_{k}}=\omega,T_{R}\geq k\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\}}\right]\,\cdot\,E[\tau_{\omega}]}

\displaystyle\overset{(*)}{=}\underbrace{\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\,E\left[1_{\{X_{t_{k}}=\omega,T_{R}\geq k\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\}}\right]}_{N^{(\omega)}}\,\cdot\,\underbrace{E[\tau_{\omega}]}_{\frac{1}{\gamma_{\omega\to}}}

\displaystyle=N^{(\omega)}\,\cdot\,\frac{1}{\gamma_{\omega\to}}.

In the first step, we used the fact that the return time
t_{R}
to the initial state
\omega_{0}
of the CTMC is in the interval
[t_{k},t_{k+1})
, if and only if the return time
T_{R}
of the embedded, DTMC equals
k
(
t_{R}\in[t_{k},t_{k+1})\iff T_{R}=k
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
) and in step (*) we made use of the fact that the waiting time distribution
t_{k+1}-t_{k}
in a certain state
\omega
depends only on that state and is independent on
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. The expectation value of the waiting time is given by
E[\tau]=\frac{1}{\gamma_{\omega\to}}
.

∎

Lemma 22

(Vector of expected visiting
time
is candidate for stationary solution of CTMC )
.

For an irreducible CTMC, every entry of
\boldsymbol{T}
is strictly positive and the eigenspace of the generator matrix
\Gamma
to the eigenvalue
\lambda=0
is spanned by
\frac{\boldsymbol{T}}{\|\boldsymbol{T}\|_{1}}
. This means that
\frac{\boldsymbol{T}}{\|\boldsymbol{T}\|_{1}}
is the only possible probability vector that is a stationary solution of the Markov chain:

\displaystyle\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{T}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{0}\text{ and }\boldsymbol{T}\in(\mathbb{R}_{>\,0})^{\Omega}\text{ , that is }

\displaystyle\frac{\boldsymbol{T}}{\left\|\boldsymbol{T}\right\|_{1}}

\displaystyle\in\begin{cases}\text{kern }(\Gamma)\cap\mathcal{PV}\cap(\mathbb{R}_{>\,0})^{\Omega}&\text{, if }\|\boldsymbol{T}\|_{1}<\infty\text{ OR}\\
\{\boldsymbol{0}\}&\text{, if }\|\boldsymbol{T}\|_{1}=\infty\end{cases}

Proof.

We know from section
4.4
that the dimension of the kernel of
\Gamma
is at most one dimensional. On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\left(\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{T}\right)^{(\alpha)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\beta\in\Omega}\Gamma^{(\alpha,\beta)}\,\underbrace{T^{(\beta)}}_{\frac{N^{(\beta)}}{\gamma_{\beta\to}}}=\sum\limits_{\beta\in\Omega\atop\beta\neq\alpha}N^{(\beta)}\,\underbrace{\left(\frac{\Gamma^{(\alpha,\beta)}}{\gamma_{\beta\to}}\right)}_{q_{\beta\to\alpha}}\,+\,\frac{N^{(\alpha)}}{\gamma_{\alpha\to}}\,\underbrace{\Gamma^{(\alpha,\alpha)}}_{-\gamma_{\alpha\to}}

(43)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{\beta\in\Omega\atop\beta\neq\alpha}N^{(\beta)}\,q_{\beta\to\alpha}\right)}_{N^{(\alpha)}}\,-\,N^{(\alpha)}

\displaystyle=0.

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{T}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{0}.

Since we have
\boldsymbol{N}\in(\mathbb{R}_{>0})^{\Omega}
and
\left(\frac{1}{\gamma_{\omega\to}}\right)_{\omega\in\Omega}\in(\mathbb{R}_{>0})^{\Omega}
, we can conclude that

\displaystyle T^{(\omega)}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{N^{(\omega)}}_{\in(0,\infty)}\,\cdot\,\underbrace{\frac{1}{\gamma_{\omega\to}}}_{\in(0,\infty)}\in(0,\infty).

(44)

Since we assumed that the Markov chain was irreducible, and we know from section
4.4
that an irreducible Markov chain has at most one stationary solution, the kernel of the generator must be spanned by
\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{T}}{\|\boldsymbol{T}\|_{1}}\right)
.

∎

Lemma 23

(Positive recurrence for CTMC)
.

For an irreducible, CTMC the following are equivalent:

(i)

All
states in
\Omega
are positive recurrent

(ii)

There exists a
single
state
\omega_{0}\in\Omega
which is positive recurrent

(iii)

There exists a
stationary solution

Proof.

(i)\Rightarrow(ii)

clear

(ii)\Rightarrow(iii)

By assumption we have:

\displaystyle\infty

\displaystyle>E[t_{R}\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}]{\mathrel{\to}^{\eqref{Eq_CTMC_ExpectedReturnTime}}}\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}T^{(\omega)}

(45)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\frac{\boldsymbol{T}}{\left\|\boldsymbol{T}\right\|_{1}}=\left(\frac{T^{(\omega)}}{\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\Omega}T^{(\alpha)}}\right)_{\omega\in\Omega}\text{is a stationary probability vector by lemma \ref{Lemma_VectorOfExpectedVisitingTimeIsCandidateForStationarySolutionOfCTMC}}

(iii)\Rightarrow(i)

If there exists an invariant probability distribution, it must be of the form
\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{T}}{\left\|\boldsymbol{T}\right\|_{1}}\right)_{\omega\in\Omega}
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle\infty>\left\|\boldsymbol{T}\right\|_{1}=\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}T^{(\omega)}=E\left[T_{R}\,|\,X_{0}=\omega_{0}\right]

(46)

∎

7
The long-term behavior of an infinite-dimensional master equation

Computing the long-term behaviour
t\to\infty
of the master equation is interesting, because this determines, where a system eventually ‘is’. For finite dimensional system, this follows directly by applying Perron–Frobenius theorem Gershgorin’s circle theorem to the generator matrix.
However, this theorem is not applicable in the infinite dimensional case. Moreover, we have to specify that we mean by the time limit the convergence in the so-called ‘strong operator topology’, that is
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\|\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\boldsymbol{P}_{0}-\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}\|_{1}
for all initial probability states
\boldsymbol{P}_{0}
. We emphasize that this is a strictly weaker version than the convergence in the norm-topology
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\|\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}-\mathrm{e}^{\infty\,\Gamma}\|_{1}^{\text{(op)}}
, where we denoted by ‘
\mathrm{e}^{\infty\,\Gamma}
’ the transition operator at time ‘
t=\infty
’.

We make use of the fact that
(\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma})_{t\geq 0}
is a
positive
semigroup, meaning that for all
\boldsymbol{X}\in l^{1}(\Omega)
,
\boldsymbol{X}\geq 0
we have
\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\,\boldsymbol{X}\geq\boldsymbol{0}
(with ‘
\boldsymbol{X}\geq\boldsymbol{0}
’ meaning that
\boldsymbol{X}\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq\,0})^{\Omega}\backslash\{\boldsymbol{0}\}
).

Closely related to the time limit
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}
for master equation (i.e. CTMC) is
Cesaro average
(also called the
mean ergodic limit
)
\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\frac{1}{M}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{M-1}Q^{i}\boldsymbol{P}_{0}
for DTMC.

The so-called
mean ergodic space
, where both these limits exists, can be shown to be the direct sum of the set of stationary states and the closure of the identity operator minus the transition matrix, with respect to the operator-one-norm. While this is in general a proper subspace of
l^{1}(\Omega)
, it coincides with the whole space in the case where the system is both
irreducible
and
positive recurrent
. This means that the minimal assumptions (namely the existence of a stationary solution) are also sufficient to guarantee convergence in the strong operator topology.

For
positive
,
irreducible
semigroups, we know that there exists a rank one projection
\mathrm{e}^{\infty\,\Gamma}=\boldsymbol{P}_{*}\otimes\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}
such that
\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\xlongrightarrow[\|\cdot\|_{1}]{t\to\infty}\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}_{*}\otimes\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}\bigr)\cdot\boldsymbol{P}_{0}=\boldsymbol{P}_{*}
(see lemma
55
for details).

While this theorem is stated in
[
AG20
]
as Corollary 3.7, and some parts can be found in the literature such as
[
BFR17
,
Are+86
]
, we are still lacking a direct proof that is accessible to physicists, without diving deep into the mathematical literature. This section is meant to close that gap.

7.1
The mean ergodic space of
l^{1}(\Omega)

We start by defining the finite Cesaro mean, as well as the mean ergodic subspace
l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)
for a given state space and a corresponding transition matrix of a DTMC.

Definition 24

(The finite Cesaro mean and the
mean ergodic subspace
)
.

Let
\Omega
be a countable, infinite state space and
Q\in[0,1]^{\Omega\times\Omega}
a countable, stochastic matrix, that is
\sum\limits_{i\in\Omega}Q^{(i,j)}=1
. The
finite Cesaro mean

\mathcal{C}_{M}
of the operator
Q
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{C}_{M}^{Q}(\boldsymbol{X}):=\frac{1}{M}\,\sum\limits_{i=0}^{M-1}(Q)^{i}(\boldsymbol{X})

\displaystyle\text{ for $M\in\mathbb{N}$}

We further define the mean ergodic subspace of
l^{1}(\Omega)
with respect to
Q
as

\displaystyle l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)

\displaystyle:=\{\boldsymbol{X}\in l^{1}(\Omega)\,:\,\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})\text{ exists with respect to }\|\cdot\|_{1}\},

(47)

where “m.e” stands for “mean ergodic´´.

Let us now study the mean ergodic space, as well as the corresponding mean ergodic averages. As it turns out, the Cesaro mean projects the mean ergodic subspace onto the set of fixed points of the transition matrix.

Lemma 25
.

The mean ergodic subspace is closed in
l^{1}(\Omega)
and the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\,:\,l^{1}_{m.e.}

\displaystyle(\Omega,Q)\to l^{1}(\Omega)

(48)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}\hskip 17.07164pt\mapsto\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})

is a projection onto
\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q)
, which is the set of fixed points of
Q
.

Proof.

First, we note that since
Q
is stochastic, the operator norm of
Q
,
\mathcal{C}_{M}
and
\mathcal{C}
are bounded by one:

\displaystyle\|Q\|_{1}^{\text{(op)}}

\displaystyle:=\sup\limits_{\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{1}=1}\underbrace{\|Q\,\boldsymbol{X}\|_{1}}_{\sum\limits_{i\in\Omega}|(Q\,\boldsymbol{X})^{(i)}|}=\sup\limits_{\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{1}=1}\sum\limits_{i\in\Omega}\,\Bigl|\sum\limits_{j\in\Omega}Q^{(i,j)}\,X^{(j)}\Bigr|\leq\sup\limits_{\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{1}=1}\,\sum\limits_{j\in\Omega}|X^{(j)}|\;\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{i\in\Omega}Q^{(i,j)}\right)}_{1}

(49)

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{1}=1}\underbrace{\sum\limits_{j\in\Omega}|X^{(j)}|}_{\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{1}}=1,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{C}_{M}\|_{1}^{\text{(op)}}

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{1}=1}\left\|\frac{1}{M}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{M-1}Q^{i}\boldsymbol{X}\right\|\leq\sup\limits_{\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{1}=1}\frac{1}{M}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{M-1}\underbrace{\|Q^{i}\boldsymbol{X}\|}_{\leq\|Q\|^{i}\,\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{1}}\leq 1,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{C}\|_{1}^{\text{(op)}}

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{1}=1}\|\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})\|_{1}=\sup\limits_{\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{1}=1}\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\underbrace{\|\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})\|_{1}^{\text{(op)}}}_{\leq\,\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{1}}\leq 1.

Further, we note that
Q
- and therefore
\mathcal{C}_{M}
- are a positive operators (meaning
\boldsymbol{X}\geq\boldsymbol{0}\Rightarrow Q\,\boldsymbol{X}\geq\boldsymbol{0}
and
\mathcal{C}_{M}\,\boldsymbol{X}\geq\boldsymbol{0}
) and that the norm for positive vectors is preserved (
\boldsymbol{X}\geq\boldsymbol{0}\Rightarrow\|\mathcal{C}_{M}\,\boldsymbol{X}\|=\|Q\,\boldsymbol{X}\|=\|\boldsymbol{X}\|
).

Now let
\boldsymbol{X}\in\overline{l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)}
, then we know that there exists a sequence
(\boldsymbol{X}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)
converging to
\boldsymbol{X}
and a sequence
(\boldsymbol{X}_{n,*})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X}_{n})=\boldsymbol{X}_{n,*}
.

First, we show that the sequence
(\boldsymbol{X}_{n,*})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is Cauchy. We can choose the number
m
and
n
large enough, such that

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{X}_{n,*}-\boldsymbol{X}_{m,*}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\|\boldsymbol{X}_{n,*}\pm\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X}_{n})\pm\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X}_{m})-\boldsymbol{X}_{m,*}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\|\boldsymbol{X}_{n,*}-\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X}_{n})\|_{1}}_{<\,\epsilon}+\underbrace{\|\mathcal{C}_{M}\|_{1}\,}_{1}\,\underbrace{\|\boldsymbol{X}_{n}-\boldsymbol{X}_{m}\|_{1}}_{<\,\epsilon}+\underbrace{\|\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X}_{m})-\boldsymbol{X}_{m,*}\|_{1}}_{<\,\epsilon}

\displaystyle<3\,\epsilon,

hence the limit
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}:=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\boldsymbol{X}_{n,*}
exists in
l^{1}(\Omega)
. Further, we have:

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})-\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})\pm\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X}_{n})\pm\boldsymbol{X}_{n,*}-\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\|\mathcal{C}_{M}\|_{1}}_{\leq\,1}\,\cdot\,\underbrace{\|\boldsymbol{X}-\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\|_{1}}_{<\,\epsilon}+\underbrace{\|\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X}_{n})-\boldsymbol{X}_{n,*}\|_{1}}_{<\,\epsilon}+\underbrace{\|\boldsymbol{X}_{n,*}-\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}}_{<\,\epsilon}

\displaystyle<3\,\epsilon,

that is
\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})\xlongrightarrow[\|\cdot\|_{1}]{M\to\infty}\boldsymbol{X}_{*}
and hence
\boldsymbol{X}\in l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)
. This shows that
l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)
is closed in
l^{1}(\Omega)
.

Now, let us determine the image of
\mathcal{C}
. Since
Q
is a continuous operator, it ”commutes” with the Cesaro operator
\mathcal{C}
on
l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)
:

\displaystyle Q\,\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{X})

\displaystyle=Q\,\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\frac{1}{M}\,\sum\limits_{i=0}^{M-1}(Q)^{i}(\boldsymbol{X}){\mathrel{\to}^{\text{$Q$ is }}_{\text{continuous}}}\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\frac{1}{M}\,\sum\limits_{i=0}^{M-1}(Q)^{i+1}(\boldsymbol{X})=\mathcal{C}\left(Q\,\boldsymbol{X}\right),

(50)

for all
\boldsymbol{X}\in l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)
. What is more, is that equation (
50
) tells us that
l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)
is
Q
-invariant, meaning
Q\,l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)\subseteq l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)
.

Let us now look at the effect of a finite Cesaro operator acting on an element of the image of
\mathbb{1}-Q
: For
\boldsymbol{X}\in l^{1}(\Omega)
we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{M}\,\bigl((\mathbb{1}-Q)\,\boldsymbol{X}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M}\,\underbrace{\sum\limits_{i=0}^{M-1}Q^{i}(\mathbb{1}-Q)}_{Q^{0}-Q^{M}}\,\boldsymbol{X}=\frac{\boldsymbol{X}-Q^{M}\,\boldsymbol{X}}{M}\xlongrightarrow{M\to\infty}\boldsymbol{0},

(51)

which mean that the mean ergodic subspace of
Q
contains the image of
\mathbb{1}-Q
, that is
\text{image }(\mathbb{1}-Q)\subseteq l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)
.

On the other hand, we have for all
\boldsymbol{X}\in l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\eqref{Eq_CMeanActingOnIdMinusQ_X_1}}}\underbrace{\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\mathcal{C}_{M}}_{\mathcal{C}}\,\bigl((\mathbb{1}-Q)\,\boldsymbol{X}\bigr)=\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{X})-\underbrace{\mathcal{C}(Q\,\boldsymbol{X})}_{Q\,\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{X})}{\mathrel{\to}^{\eqref{Eq_QCommutesWithCMean}}}(\mathbb{1}-Q)\,\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{X}),

(52)

which means that the Cesaro average
\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{X})
is a fixed point of the operator
Q
, that is
\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{X})\in\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q)
.

It now remains to show that the Cesaro operator is indeed a projection. This can be shown by applying the Cesaro operator twice, and keeping in mind that it is linear, bounded and that its image in
Q
-invariant:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{2}\,(\boldsymbol{X})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}\left(\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\frac{1}{M}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{M-1}Q^{i}\,\boldsymbol{X}\right){\mathrel{\to}^{\text{C is }}_{\text{continuous}}}\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\underbrace{\frac{1}{M}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{M-1}\overbrace{\mathcal{C}\left(Q^{i}\,\boldsymbol{X}\right)}^{\mathcal{C}\left(\boldsymbol{X}\right)}}_{\mathcal{C}\left(\boldsymbol{X}\right)}{\mathrel{\to}^{\eqref{Eq_CMeanActingOnIdMinusQ_X_2}}}\mathcal{C}\left(\boldsymbol{X}\right).

So we can say
l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)
is a closed,
Q
- invariant subspace, where the Ceasro average is a projection onto the kernel of
\mathbb{1}-Q
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\,:\,l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)

\displaystyle\to\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}

\displaystyle\mapsto\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})

∎

We are now in a position to characterize the mean ergodic subspace: While it clearly contains the set of fixed points of
Q
, the previous calculations have shown, that the mean ergodic average vanishes for every vector contained in the image of the identity minus the transition matrix. As it turns, these subspaces uniquely determine the mean ergodic space.

Theorem 26
.

The mean ergodic space of a stochastic operator
Q
in
l^{1}(\Omega)
equals the direct sum of the kernel of
\mathbb{1}-Q
and the closure of the image of
\mathbb{1}-Q
, that is

\displaystyle l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle=\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q)\oplus\overline{\text{image }(\mathbb{1}-Q)}.

(53)

Proof.

”
\supseteq
”

This part was indirectly shown in lemma
25
:

The mean ergodic space clearly contains every fixed point of
Q
(that is
\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q)\subseteq l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)
), while equation (
52
) tells us that it also contains the image of
\mathbb{1}-Q
(
\text{image }(\mathbb{1}-Q)\subseteq l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)
). Finally, since
l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)
is closed in
l^{1}(\Omega)
, the claim follows.

”
\subseteq
”

Let
\boldsymbol{X}\in l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)
, that is
\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})\xlongrightarrow[\|\cdot\|_{1}]{M\to\infty}\boldsymbol{X}_{*}
for some
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\in\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q)
.

Then we can separate
\boldsymbol{X}
into three parts: One belonging to
\text{image }(\mathbb{1}-Q)
, one belonging to
\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q)
and one vanishing:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}=\underbrace{\boldsymbol{X}-\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})}_{\in\,\text{image }(\mathbb{1}-Q)}+\underbrace{\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})-\boldsymbol{X}_{*}}_{\xlongrightarrow{M\to\infty}\,\boldsymbol{0}}+\underbrace{\boldsymbol{X}_{*}}_{\in\,\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q)},

(54)

which implies
\boldsymbol{X}\in\overline{\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q)+\text{image }(\mathbb{1}-Q)}
.

What remains to show is the first under brace, claiming that the image of
\mathbb{1}-\mathcal{C}_{M}
is contained in the image of
\mathbb{1}-Q
. We do this, by consider the following calculation:

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathbb{1}-Q\bigr)

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=0}^{M-2}\,\left(\frac{M-1-i}{M}\right)\,Q^{i}=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{i=0}^{M-2}\,\left(\frac{M-1-i}{M}\right)\,Q^{i}}_{\sum\limits_{j=0}^{M-2}\left(1-\frac{j+1}{M}\right)\,Q^{j}}-\underbrace{\sum\limits_{i=0}^{M-2}\frac{M-1-i}{M}\,Q^{i+1}}_{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{M-1}\left(1-\frac{j}{M}\right)\,Q^{j}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(1-\frac{1}{M}\right)\,Q^{0}}_{\mathbb{1}-\frac{1}{M}\,Q^{0}}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{M-2}\underbrace{\left[\left(1-\frac{1+j}{M}\right)-\left(1-\frac{j}{M}\right)\right]\,Q^{j}}_{\frac{-1}{M}\,Q^{j}}-\underbrace{\left(1-\frac{M-1}{M}\right)\,Q^{M-1}}_{-\frac{1}{M}\,Q^{M-1}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{1}-\frac{1}{M}\,\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{M-1}\,Q^{i}\right)}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}}=\mathbb{1}-\mathcal{C}_{M}\text{ , which implies }

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}-\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{X})

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbb{1}-Q\right)\,\sum\limits_{i=0}^{M-2}\,\left(\frac{M-1-i}{M}\right)\,Q^{i}\boldsymbol{X}\in\text{image }(\mathbb{1}-Q).

What remains to show, is that the sum of the two vector spaces
\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q)
and
\overline{\text{image }(\mathbb{1}-Q)}
in indeed direct. Let
\boldsymbol{X}
be an element of their intersection, that is

\boldsymbol{X}\in\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q)\cap\overline{\text{image }(\mathbb{1}-Q)}
. We now proceed by showing that the norm of
\boldsymbol{X}
is arbitrary small.

Since
\boldsymbol{X}\in\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q)
, we have
\boldsymbol{X}=Q\,\boldsymbol{X}=\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})
. On the other hand,
\boldsymbol{X}\in\overline{\text{image }(\mathbb{1}-Q)}
implies that for every
\epsilon>0
there exists an element
\boldsymbol{Y}_{\epsilon}\in l^{1}(\Omega)
such that
\|\boldsymbol{X}-(\mathbb{1}-Q)\boldsymbol{Y}_{\epsilon}\|_{1}<\epsilon
.

Now - for a fixed
\epsilon>0
- choose a natural number
M=M_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\|\mathcal{C}_{M}(\mathbb{1}-Q)\boldsymbol{Y}_{\epsilon}\|<\epsilon
, which is possible by equation (
51
). Then we can estimate the norm of
\boldsymbol{X}
to

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})\|_{1}=\|\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})\pm\mathcal{C}_{M}\,(\mathbb{1}-Q)\,\boldsymbol{Y}_{\epsilon}\|_{1}\leq\underbrace{\|\mathcal{C}_{M}\left[\boldsymbol{X}-(\mathbb{1}-Q)\,\boldsymbol{Y}_{\epsilon}\right]\|_{1}}_{\leq\|\mathcal{C}_{M}\|_{1}^{\text{(op)}}\,\|\boldsymbol{X}-(\mathbb{1}-Q)\,\boldsymbol{Y}_{\epsilon}\|_{1}}+\|\mathcal{C}_{M}\,(\mathbb{1}-Q)\,\boldsymbol{Y}_{\epsilon}\|_{1}

(55)

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\|\mathcal{C}_{M}\|_{1}^{\text{(op)}}}_{1}\,\underbrace{\|\boldsymbol{X}-(\mathbb{1}-Q)\,\boldsymbol{Y}_{\epsilon}\|_{1}}_{<\,\epsilon}+\underbrace{\|\mathcal{C}_{M}\,(\mathbb{1}-Q)\,\boldsymbol{Y}_{\epsilon}\|_{1}}_{<\,\epsilon}<2\,\epsilon.

Since
\epsilon>0
was arbitrary, this mean that the norm of
\boldsymbol{X}
must be arbitrary small, hence
\boldsymbol{X}
must vanish. This concludes the proof.

∎

7.2
The ergodic mean of the transition matrix of an irreducible, positive recurrent Markov chain

So far we characterized the mean ergodic space for arbitrary DTMC, but eventually we are interested in the long-term behavior, for which we need the existence of stationary solutions. Stationary solutions, on the other hand, require - as we have seen in section
6
- positive recurrence on each strongly connected component.

It turns out that for networks which are both strongly connected and positive recurrent, all initial states are mean ergodic.

Theorem 27

(For irreducible and positive recurrent networks all states are mean ergodic)
.

If
Q
is the stochastic matrix of an
irreducible
,
positive recurrent
Markov chain, then the whole space
l^{1}(\Omega)
is mean ergodic with respect to
Q
, that is
l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,Q)=l^{1}(\Omega)
, or, in other words: The Cesaro mean (i.e. the ergodic mean) of a countable stochastic matrix in
l^{1}(\Omega)
converges, if the underlying Markov chain is both irreducible and positive recurrent.

basic idea

It is possible - similar to equation (
54
) - to write every unit vector
\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega}
as a sum, consisting of a summand
\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega}-\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})
contained in the image of
\mathbb{1}-Q
, a summand
\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})-\boldsymbol{P}_{*}
converging to zero and third summand
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}
contained in the set of stationary states:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega}-\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})}_{\in\,\text{image }(\mathbb{1}-Q)}+\underbrace{\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})-\boldsymbol{P}_{*}}_{\xlongrightarrow[\|\cdot\|_{1}]{???}\,\boldsymbol{0}}+\underbrace{\boldsymbol{P}_{*}}_{\in\,\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q)}

The problem is now, that in order to show that the whole space
l^{1}(\Omega)
is a mean ergodic, we have to assume that the Cesaro mean
\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})
of every unit vector
\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega}
converges to
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}
, which would be a circular reasoning.

This can be circumvented, by showing that for all
\omega\in\Omega
the sequence
\left(\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})\right)_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq l^{1}(\Omega)
has a convergent
subsequence

\left(\mathcal{C}_{M_{n}}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
l^{1}(\Omega)
. This limit must coincide with
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}
, since:

(i)

It lies in the kernel of
Id-Q
:

\displaystyle(\mathbb{1}-Q)\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\underbrace{\mathcal{C}_{M_{n}}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})}_{\frac{1}{M_{n}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{M_{n}-1}Q^{k}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})}{\mathrel{\to}^{\text{Q continuous}}}\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\underbrace{(\mathbb{1}-Q)\,\sum\limits_{k=0}^{M_{n}-1}Q^{k}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})}_{\frac{\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega}-Q^{M_{n}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega}}{M_{n}}}=\boldsymbol{0}

(ii)

The norm is conserved by the limit
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{C}_{M_{n}}
:

\displaystyle\|\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\,\mathcal{C}_{M_{n}}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})\|_{1}{\mathrel{\to}^{\|\cdot\|_{1}\text{ continuous}}}\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\,\underbrace{\|\mathcal{C}_{M_{n}}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})\|_{1}}_{1}=1.

Since
(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})_{\omega\in\Omega}
forms a Schauder basis of
l^{1}(\Omega)
, we can conclude that the vector space
l^{1}(\Omega)
of summable entries of
\Omega
equals the sum of the closure of the image- and the kernel of the operator
\mathbb{1}-Q
, that is:

l^{1}(\Omega)=\overline{\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q)+\text{image }(\mathbb{1}-Q)}
.

Proof.

Since the Markov chain is irreducible, the dimension of the fixed points of
Q
(that is the dimension of the kernel of
\mathbb{1}-Q
) is at most one (compare lemma
6
) and due to positive recurrence, there exists a strictly positive probability vector
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}\in\mathcal{PV}\cap(\mathbb{R}_{>\,0})^{\Omega}
such that
\mathbb{1}-Q=\text{span }(\boldsymbol{P}_{*})
(compare lemma
23
).

In order to show the existence of a converging subsequence of
\left(\mathcal{C}_{M}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})\right)_{M\in\mathbb{N}}
, we make use of the fact that the element of the kernel of the generator has strictly positive entries:
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}\in\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q)\cap(\mathbb{R}_{>0})^{\Omega}
. This allows us to define the vector
\left(\frac{P_{*}^{(\alpha)}}{P_{*}^{(\omega)}}\right)_{\alpha\in\Omega}
, which at the same time, is an element-wise upper bound for
\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega}
, that is we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}

\displaystyle\leq\;\;\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\boldsymbol{P}_{*}}{P_{*}^{(\omega)}}

\displaystyle\text{ element-wise, that is }

(56)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\;\;E_{\omega}^{(\alpha)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{P_{*}^{(\alpha)}}{P_{*}^{(\omega)}}

\displaystyle\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Omega.

We note that equation (
56
) reads the following:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\;\;\underbrace{E_{\alpha}^{(\alpha)}}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\frac{P_{*}^{(\alpha)}}{P_{*}^{(\alpha)}}}_{1}\text{ for $\alpha=\omega$ and }

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\;\;\underbrace{E_{\omega}^{(\alpha)}}_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\frac{P_{*}^{(\alpha)}}{P_{*}^{(\omega)}}}_{>\,0}\text{ for $\alpha\neq\omega$. }

Since
Q
is a positive, linear operator (and hence
(Q)^{i}
is positive, for all natural numbers
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
), we conclude:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}\overset{\eqref{Eq_CapturingErgodicMean}}{\leq}\left(Q\right)^{i}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})\overset{\eqref{Eq_CapturingErgodicMean}}{\leq}\left(Q\right)^{i}\,\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{P}_{*}}{P_{*}^{(\omega)}}\right){\mathrel{\to}^{\text{$\boldsymbol{P}_{*}$ is a fixed}}_{\text{point of $Q$}}}\frac{\boldsymbol{P}_{*}}{P_{*}^{(\omega)}}.

By summing over
i
from
0
to
M-1
, we can make the same estimation for the corresponding Cesaro-mean:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}\leq\mathcal{C}_{M}\left(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega}\right)=\frac{1}{M}\,\sum\limits_{i=0}^{M-1}\,\left(Q\right)^{i}\,(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})\leq\frac{\boldsymbol{P}_{*}}{P_{*}^{(\omega)}}.

Since every
\alpha\in\Omega
the sequence
\left(\left(\mathcal{C}_{M}\left(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega}\right)\right)^{(\alpha)}\right)_{M\in\mathbb{N}}
is contained in the compact interval
\left[0,\frac{P_{*}^{(\alpha)}}{P_{*}^{(\omega)}}\right]
, we can construct - using a ‘diagonal argument’ - a pointwise converging subsequence of
\left(\mathcal{C}_{M_{n}}\left(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega}\right)\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

An alternative way to argue would be to consider the set
\prod\limits_{\alpha\in\Omega}\left[0,\frac{P_{*}^{(\alpha)}}{P_{*}^{(\omega)}}\right]
, which by Tychonoff’s theorem, is compact (in the product topology) as a product of compact spaces. Since every sequence in a compact space contains a convergent subsequence, we get a - pointwise - converging subsequence
\left(\mathcal{C}_{M_{n}}\left(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega}\right)\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
to
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}\in\mathcal{PV}\cap\prod\limits_{\alpha\in\Omega}\left[0,\frac{P_{*}^{(\alpha)}}{P_{*}^{(\omega)}}\right]
.

So far, we have shown the existence of a
pointwise
converging subsequence, that is for all states
\alpha\in\Omega
and all small numbers
\epsilon>0
, we know of the existence of a natural number
N_{\alpha,\epsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all larger numbers
n\geq N_{\alpha,\epsilon}
we have:

\displaystyle\Bigl|\bigl(\mathcal{C}_{M_{n}}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})\bigr)^{(\alpha)}-P_{*}^{(\alpha)}\Bigr|<\epsilon.

What remains of the proof, is to show is that this convergence is not only
pointwise
, but also with respect to the
\|\cdot\|_{1}
-norm (i.e. that the number
N_{\alpha,\epsilon}
can be chosen independent of
\alpha
). Therefore, we fix
\epsilon>0
and let
F_{\epsilon}\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)
be a finite set, such that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\Omega\backslash F}P_{*}^{(\alpha)}<\epsilon\,P_{*}^{(\omega)},

(57)

which can be done, since
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}
is a probability vector.

Since
F_{\epsilon}
is a
finite
set, we can choose
N_{\epsilon}:=\max\limits_{f\in F_{\epsilon}}\{N_{f,\epsilon}\}
, such that for all
n\geq N_{\epsilon}
for all
f\in F_{\epsilon}
we have:

\displaystyle\Bigl|\left(\mathcal{C}_{M_{n}}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})\right)^{(f)}-P_{*}^{(f)}\Bigr|<\frac{\epsilon}{|F_{\epsilon}|}.

(58)

Since both
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}
and
\left(\mathcal{C}_{M_{n}}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are contained in
{\prod}\limits_{\alpha\in\Omega}\left[0,\frac{P_{*}^{(\alpha)}}{P_{*}^{(\omega)}}\right]
, then so is the vector of the element-wise absolute value of their difference:

\displaystyle\left(\Bigl|P_{*}^{(\alpha)}-\bigl(\mathcal{C}_{M_{n}}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})\bigr)^{(\alpha)}\Bigr|\right)_{\alpha\in\Omega}

\displaystyle\in{\prod}\limits_{\alpha\in\Omega}\left[0,\frac{P_{*}^{(\alpha)}}{P_{*}^{(\omega)}}\right]

(59)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\Bigl|P_{*}^{(\alpha)}-\bigl(\mathcal{C}_{M_{n}}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})\bigr)^{(\alpha)}\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{P_{*}^{(\alpha)}}{P_{*}^{(\omega)}}.

Then we can make the following estimation:

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{C}_{M_{n}}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})-\boldsymbol{P}_{*}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{f\in F_{\epsilon}}\underbrace{\left|\mathcal{C}_{M_{n}}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})^{(f)}-P_{*}^{(f)}\right|}_{<\frac{\epsilon}{|F_{\epsilon}|}}+\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\Omega\backslash F_{\epsilon}}\underbrace{\left|P_{*}^{(\alpha)}-\mathcal{C}_{M_{n}}(\boldsymbol{E}_{\omega})^{(\alpha)}\right|}_{<\frac{P_{*}^{(\alpha)}}{P_{*}^{(\omega)}}}

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{Eq_CapturingDifference}}{\overset{\eqref{Eq_Estimating_FinitePart}}{<}}\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{f\in F}\frac{\epsilon}{|F|}\right)}_{\epsilon}+\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\Omega\backslash F}\frac{P_{*}^{(\alpha)}}{P_{*}^{(\omega)}}\right)}_{<\,\epsilon}\overset{\eqref{Eq_Estimating_Tail}}{<}2\,\epsilon,

which concludes the proof.

∎

7.3
The long-term behavior of an irreducible, positive recurrent master equation

Similar to the discrete-time case we define the subspace where the long-term behavior is well defined. The time limit
\xlongrightarrow[\|\cdot\|_{1}]{t\to\infty}
- in analogy to the Cesaro mean - is then again a projection onto the stationary states.

Lemma 28
.

Let
l^{1}_{\text{l.t.b.}}(\Omega,\Gamma):=\{\boldsymbol{X}\in l^{1}(\Omega)\,:\,\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\boldsymbol{X}\text{ exists with respect to }\|\cdot\|_{1}\}
. Then
l^{1}_{\text{l.t.b.}}(\Omega,\Gamma)
is closed in
l^{1}(\Omega)
and the mapping

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\,:\,l^{1}_{\text{l.t.b.}}(\Omega,\Gamma)

\displaystyle\to l^{1}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}

\displaystyle\mapsto\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\,\boldsymbol{X}

is a projection onto
\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma})=\text{kern }(\Gamma)
.

Proof.

The closedness of
l^{1}_{\text{l.t.b.}}(\Omega,\Gamma)
can be shown in analogy to the proof in lemma
25
, by replacing ‘
\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{X})
’ by ‘
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}
’ (see lemma
62
in the appendix for details).

The fact that the image of
\mathcal{L}
is contained in the fixed space of
\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}
can be seen from the following calculation: For
\boldsymbol{X}\in l^{1}_{\text{l.t.b.}}(\Omega,\Gamma)
and
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}:=\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{X})
, we have

\displaystyle\|\left(\mathbb{1}-\mathrm{e}^{\tau\,\Gamma}\right)\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{X})\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\|\underbrace{\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{X})}_{\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\,\boldsymbol{X}}-\mathrm{e}^{\tau\,\Gamma}\underbrace{\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{X})}_{\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\,\boldsymbol{X}}\pm\,\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}\leq\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\left(\|\mathrm{e}^{(t+\tau)\,\Gamma}\,\boldsymbol{X}-\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}+\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}-\mathrm{e}^{\tau\,\Gamma}\,\boldsymbol{X}\|_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=0=\|\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{X})\left(\mathbb{1}-\mathrm{e}^{\tau\,\Gamma}\right)\|_{1}.

hence
\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{X})\in\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-\mathrm{e}^{\tau\,\Gamma})=\text{kern }(\Gamma)
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{e}^{\tau\,\Gamma}\,\boldsymbol{X})=\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{X})=\mathrm{e}^{\tau\,\Gamma}\,\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{X})
.

The fact that
\mathcal{L}
is a projection, can be seen as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{2}(\boldsymbol{X})

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{L}}\,(\boldsymbol{X})=\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{X}).

∎

Just as we have seen in the discrete-time case, that the two properties of irreducibility and positive recurrence make sure that all states are mean ergodic, they guarantee in the continuous-time case that the time limit exists for all initial states.

Theorem 29

(The time limit for countable, infinite dimensional master equations exists for all networks which are irreducible and positive recurrent)
.

If the master equation is both irreducible and positive recurrent, then the limit
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\boldsymbol{X}_{0}
exists for all
\boldsymbol{X}_{0}\in l^{1}(\Omega)
, that is
l^{1}_{\text{l.t.b.}}(\Omega,\Gamma)=l^{1}(\Omega)
.

Proof.

For every
t>0
, the solution operator
Q_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}
is a stochastic matrix, which is both irreducible and positive recurrent, if and only if the master equation is both irreducible and positive recurrent.

After applying the theorems
26
and
27
, we get:

\displaystyle l^{1}(\Omega)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\text{thm }\ref{Thm_Irreducible_PositiveRecurrent_l1IsMeanErgodic}}}\underbrace{\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q_{t})}_{\text{kern }(\Gamma)}\oplus\overline{\text{image }(\mathbb{1}-Q_{t})}{\mathrel{\to}^{\text{thm }\ref{Thm_ExplicitFormOfTheMeanErgodicSubspaceInl1}}}l^{1}_{m.e.}(\Omega,Q_{t})

Since
l^{1}_{\text{l.t.b.}}(\Omega,\Gamma)
is closed in
l^{1}(\Omega)
and clearly contains the set of fixed points
\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-Q_{t})=\text{kern }(\Gamma)
, it suffices to show convergence on
\text{image }(\mathbb{1}-Q_{t})
.

Now fix
\tau>0
and write
t=n\,\tau+\tilde{\tau}
. This helps us to make the following estimation:

\displaystyle\|\underbrace{Q_{t}\,\left(\mathbb{1}-Q_{\tau}\right)}_{Q_{t}-Q_{t+\tau}}\boldsymbol{X}\|

\displaystyle=\|\left(Q_{n\,\tau+\tilde{\tau}}-Q_{(n+1)\,\tau+\tilde{\tau}}\right)\boldsymbol{X}\|

\displaystyle=\|\left(Q_{\tau}^{n}-Q_{\tau}^{n+1}\right)\,Q_{\tilde{\tau}}\,\boldsymbol{X}\|\leq\|Q_{\tau}^{n}-Q_{\tau}^{n+1}\|\,\|Q_{\tilde{\tau}}\,\boldsymbol{X}\|

In order to show that
\|Q_{\tau}^{n}-Q_{\tau}^{n+1}\|
converges for
n\to\infty
(and thus for
t\to\infty
), it suffices - by lemma
60
- to show that the spectrum of
Q_{\tau}
intersects with the unit circle at the point
1
, that is
\sigma(Q_{\tau})\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,:\,|z|=1\}\subseteq\{1\}
. This can be done by invoking a version of the spectral mapping theorem (compare
56
), the fact that the boundary spectrum of the generator of the master equation contains only its spectral bound (lemma
59
), as well as the fact that this spectral bound vanishes (see lemma
57
):

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma})

\displaystyle\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,:\,|z|=1\}=\underbrace{\left(\sigma(\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma})\backslash\{0\}\right)}_{\mathrm{e}^{t\,\sigma(\Gamma)}}\cap\,\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,:\,|z|=1\}{\mathrel{\to}^{\text{lemma }\ref{Lemma_VersionOfTheSpectralMappingThm}}}\mathrm{e}^{t\,\sigma(\Gamma)}\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,:\,|z|=1\}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\text{lemma \ref{Lemma_SpectralMappingAppliedToBoundaryOfTheUnitCircle}}}}\mathrm{e}^{t\,\sigma(\Gamma)\cap i\,\mathbb{R}}{\mathrel{\to}^{i\,t\,\mathbb{R}=i\,\mathbb{R}}}\mathrm{e}^{t\,(\sigma(\Gamma)\cap i\,\mathbb{R})}{\mathrel{\to}^{\text{lemma }\ref{Lemma_GrowthBoundAndSpectralBoundOfPositiveSemigroups}}_{s(\Gamma)=0}}\mathrm{e}^{t\,\overbrace{\bigl(\sigma(\Gamma)\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,:\,\text{Re }[z]=s(\Gamma)\}\bigr)}^{\sigma_{b}(\Gamma)}}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}}\mathrm{e}^{t\,\overbrace{\sigma_{b}(\Gamma)}^{\{0\}}}{\mathrel{\to}^{\text{lemma }\ref{Lemma_BoundarySpectrumContainsOnlyTheSpectralBound}}}\mathrm{e}^{\{0\}}=\{1\}.

This concludes the proof.

∎

8
The thermodynamic limit of the stationary solutions of the master equation

8.1
The (uniform) convergence of the time limit with respect to the system size

The solution of the master equation of a finite system size approaches a steady state, which - for non irreducible networks - can depend on the initial condition (compare
[
FD22
]
).

Let us consider a generator
\Gamma
of an infinite dimensional system, fix an arbitrary initial state
\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{PV}
and a finite set
F\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)
.

In the following, we estimate the difference between the solution of the master equation
\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0})
and the steady state
\boldsymbol{P}^{F}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0})
. When keeping in mind, that the stationary state is an eigenvector of the generator to the eigenvalue
\lambda=0
, we get:

\displaystyle\left\|\underbrace{\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0})}_{\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma^{[F]}}\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}}-\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{\infty}\left(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\right)\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\sigma\left(\Gamma^{[F]}\right)\backslash\{0\}}\,\underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{t\,\lambda}}_{\leq\,\mathrm{e}^{-t\,S_{g}\left(\Gamma^{[F]}\right)}}\,\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{d=1}^{g_{\lambda}(\Gamma^{[F]})}\;\;\sum\limits_{r=1}^{j_{\lambda,d}(\Gamma^{[F]})}\mu_{\lambda,d,r}\,(\Gamma^{[F]})\,\sum\limits_{k=0}^{r-1}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}\right)}_{\leq\,c\,t^{j(\lambda)}}

\displaystyle\leq C\,\mathrm{e}^{-t\,S_{g}\left(\Gamma^{[F]}\right)}\,t^{J\left(\Gamma^{[F]}\right)}.

where
\sigma
denotes the spectrum of a matrix,
g_{\lambda}(\Gamma^{[F]})
the geometric multiplicity,
j_{\lambda,d}(\Gamma^{[F]})\in\mathbb{N}_{\leq\,g_{\lambda}(\Gamma^{[F]})}
the size of Jordan block number
d
to the eigenvalue
\lambda
, and
\mu
arbitrary coefficients.

The expression in the parenthesis
\bigl(\cdots\bigr)
can be estimated as some constants times
t
to the power of the size of the largest Jordan block
j(\lambda)
, whereas the exponential term
\mathrm{e}^{t\,\lambda}
is less or equal to
\mathrm{e}^{-t\,S_{g}(\Gamma^{[F]})}
, where
S_{g}(\Gamma^{[F]}):=\text{dist}\bigl(\text{Re }(\sigma(\Gamma^{[F]})\backslash\{0\}),0\bigr):=\max\{\text{Re }(\lambda)\,:\,\lambda\in\sigma(\Gamma^{[F]})\backslash\{0\}\}
is called the
spectral gap
, the minimal distance from the eigenvalues of the generator and the imaginary axis.

The sum over the eigenvalue of the generator can again be estimated to a constant times the time
t
to the power of the
largest
Jordan block
J(\Gamma^{[F]})
times the exponential function of
t
times the spectral gap.

While this expression does converge to zero for
t\to\infty
, it does not do so uniformly in the system size
|F|
.

A sufficient conditions for uniform convergence in the system size would be the following two properties:

(i)

The size of the Jordan blocks is bounded from above, that is
J\left(\Gamma^{[F]}\right)\leq N
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
AND

(ii)

The spectral gap is bounded from below and does therefore not vanish in the thermodynamic limit, that is
S_{g}\left(\Gamma^{[F]}\right)\geq\epsilon_{0}
for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
.

With these two requirements met, it is straightforward to see that we can achieve uniform convergence, since for fixed
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon>0
, we can choose a time
T_{\epsilon}
such that from thereon out (
t\geq T_{\epsilon}
) we have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0})-\boldsymbol{P}^{F}_{\infty}\left(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\right)\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C\,\mathrm{e}^{-t\,S_{g}\left(\Gamma^{[F]}\right)}\,t^{J\left(\Gamma^{[F]}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq C\,\mathrm{e}^{-t\,\epsilon_{0}}\,t^{N}<\epsilon.

But since we lack theorems which guarantee these requirements, we can only prove the existence of the iterated limit

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{{t\to\infty}\atop{|F|\to\infty}}\,\boldsymbol{P}^{F}\left(t\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F]}\right)

in very specific examples.

8.2
Sufficient conditions for the convergence of stationary solutions for finite systems in the thermodynamic limit

We start by showing that if the thermodynamic limit for stationary solutions of finite systems exist, then this limit vector is a
stationary
solution for the infinite dimensional system.

Theorem 30

(thermodynamic limit of stationary states)
.

Let
\Gamma
be the generator of an irreducible master equation with
\|\Gamma\|^{\text{(op)}}_{1,1}<\infty
and let the limiting states
\left(\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F}\right)_{F\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)}:=\left(\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}^{F}\left(\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[F]}\right)\right)_{F\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)}\in\mathcal{PV}\cap\text{kern }(\Gamma^{[F]})
of every finite subsystem
F\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)
converge for all initial state
\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{PV}
to some probability vector
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}
. Then the master equation is positive recurrent with the stationary and limiting solution
\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}=\boldsymbol{P}_{*}=\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F}
.

Proof.

First, we note that
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}
has non-negative entries and is normalized, since both the dual unit vector
\boldsymbol{E}_{i}^{\prime}
, as well as the norm
\|\cdot\|_{1}
are continuous:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}_{i}^{\prime}\left(\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F}\right)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\boldsymbol{E}_{i}^{\prime}\text{ continuous}}}\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\underbrace{\boldsymbol{E}_{i}^{\prime}\left(\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F}\right)}_{\geq\,0}\geq 0\text{ and }

\displaystyle\left\|\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\|\cdot\|_{1}\text{ cont}}}\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\,\underbrace{\|\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F}\|_{1}}_{1}=1.

To see, that
\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F}
indeed lies in the kernel of
\Gamma
, we consider

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\underbrace{\Gamma\,\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F}}_{\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F}}\Bigr\|_{1}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\Gamma\text{ continuous}}}\underbrace{\left\|\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F}\right\|_{1}}_{\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\left\|\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F}\right\|_{1}}{\mathrel{\to}^{\|\cdot\|_{1}\text{ continuous}}}

(60)

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\left\|\underbrace{\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F}-\overbrace{\Gamma^{[F]}\,\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F}}^{\boldsymbol{0}}}_{(\Gamma-\Gamma^{[F]})\,\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F}}\right\|_{1}=\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\underbrace{\left\|(\Gamma-\Gamma^{[F]})\,\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F}\right\|_{1}}_{\leq\,\left\|(\Gamma-\Gamma^{[F]})\right\|_{1}\,\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F}\right\|_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\left\|(\Gamma-\Gamma^{[F]})\right\|_{1}\,\underbrace{\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F}\right\|_{1}}_{1}{\mathrel{\to}^{\text{lemma}}_{\ref{Lemma_ApproximatingTheFullGeneratorWithGeneratorsOfFiniteSubnetworks}}}0.

Since the network was assumed to be irreducible, by lemma
6
, the stationary solution must be unique, hence the limit
\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F}
does not depend on the initial state
\boldsymbol{P}_{0}
.

∎

Remark 31
.

Let
(F_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\text{Fin}(\Omega)
be an increasing sequence of finite sets (that is
F_{n+1}\subseteq F_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
) such that
\Omega=\bigcup\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}F_{n}
. It is sufficient to check, whether
\left(\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}^{F_{n}}(\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{F_{n}})\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges, in order to make the same conclusions as in theorem
30
.

Lemma 32
.

[A Sufficient condition for the approximation of the long-term behavior of probability states]

Let
\mathcal{S}
be an irreducible network with only bidirectional links (that is
\gamma_{\alpha\to\beta}>0\iff\gamma_{\beta\to\alpha}>0
),
\boldsymbol{P}_{0},\boldsymbol{P}_{*}\in\mathcal{PV}
be probability vectors and
M_{0}\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)\backslash(\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{P}_{0}}\cup\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{P}_{*}})
a strongly connected, finite subnetwork, such that for all strongly connected, finite subnetworks containing
M_{0}
(
M\in\text{Fin}(\Omega),\,M\supseteq M_{0}
,
\mathcal{S}_{M}
strongly connected) we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}^{M}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{P}^{[M]}_{0})=\boldsymbol{P}^{[M]}_{*},

(61)

then
\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}^{M}_{\infty}\bigr)(\boldsymbol{P}^{[M]}_{0})\xlongrightarrow{M\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)\backslash\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{P}_{0}}}\boldsymbol{P}_{*}
.

Proof.

First, we note that if equation (
61
) were to hold for all finite sets (instead of only on those finite sets, whose associated network is strongly connected), then the statement would follow from lemma
51
. What real message from lemma
32
is therefore, that it is enough to check the fulfillment of equation (
61
) for strongly connected subnetworks.

Fix
\epsilon\in\bigl(0,\frac{1}{2}\bigr)
.

(i)

choose
F_{\epsilon}^{(1)}\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)
such that for all
F\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)\backslash\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{P}_{0}},\,F\supseteq F_{\epsilon}^{(1)}
we have
\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\bigr)(F)>1-\epsilon
and
\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}_{*}\bigr)\bigl(F\bigr)>0

(ii)

choose
F_{\epsilon}^{(2)}\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)
such that for all
A\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)\backslash\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{P}_{0}},\,A\supseteq F_{\epsilon}^{(2)}
we have
\|\boldsymbol{P}_{*}-\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{*}\|_{1}<\epsilon
(this is possible due to lemma
51
)

(iii)

choose
F_{\epsilon}:=F_{\epsilon}^{(1)}\cup F_{\epsilon}^{(2)}
and fix
F\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)
,
F\supseteq F_{\epsilon}
. Since there are only bidirectional links, the set
F
is a disjoint union of minimal absorbing set, that is
A=\bigcup\limits_{i=0}^{n}M_{i}
with
A_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}_{A}}
. We call
M_{0}
the minimal absorbing set containing
F_{\epsilon}
, that is
M_{0}\supseteq F_{\epsilon}
.

We also have

(iv)

\boldsymbol{P}^{F}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\in M_{0})=\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}^{[M_{0}]}(\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[M_{0}]})
and

(v)

\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\bigr)\,\bigl(\Omega\backslash M_{0}\bigr)=\frac{1-(\boldsymbol{P}_{0})\,\bigl(M_{0}\bigr)}{(\boldsymbol{P}_{0})\,\bigl(A\bigr)}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}\overset{\epsilon<1/2}{<}2\,\epsilon
.

Then we get:

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\boldsymbol{P}_{*}-\underbrace{\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0})}_{\sum\limits_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}_{A}}}\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}(M)\bigr)\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\in M)}\Bigr\|_{1}\overset{M_{0}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}_{A}}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl\|\boldsymbol{P}_{*}-\overbrace{\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\bigr)(M_{0})}^{1-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\bigr)\bigl(\Omega\backslash M_{0}\bigr)}}\,\overbrace{\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{\infty}\bigr)(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\in M_{0})}^{\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}^{[M_{0}]}(\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[M_{0}]})}\Bigr\|_{1}+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}_{A}}\backslash\{M_{0}\}}\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\bigr)(M)\overbrace{\|\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\in M)\|_{1}}^{1}}_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,1}\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\bigr)(\Omega\backslash M_{0})}=

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl\|\boldsymbol{P}_{*}-\underbrace{\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}^{[M_{0}]}(\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[M_{0}]})}_{\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}_{*}\bigr)^{[M_{0}]}}\Bigr\|_{1}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\bigr)\bigl(\Omega\backslash M_{0}\bigr)}\underbrace{\|\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}^{[M_{0}]}(\boldsymbol{P}_{0}^{[M_{0}]})\|_{1}}_{1}+{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,1}\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\bigr)\bigl(\Omega\backslash M_{0}\bigr)}{\mathrel{\to}^{\text{assumption (iii)}}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left\|\boldsymbol{P}_{*}-\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}_{*}\bigr)^{[M_{0}]}\right\|_{1}}_{\overset{(\text{ii})}{<}\epsilon}+2\,\underbrace{\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}\bigr)\bigl(\Omega\backslash M_{0}\bigr)}_{\leq\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}\,\overset{(\text{v})}{<}2\,\epsilon}

\displaystyle<5\,\epsilon.

{A_{\epsilon}=}\atop{A_{\epsilon}^{(1)}\cup A_{\epsilon}^{(2)}}

A_{0}

A_{1}

A_{2}

\dots

A_{n}

Figure 12
:
Illustrating the relationships between the sets
F_{\epsilon}\subseteq A_{0}
,
\dots
,
A_{n}
and
A=\bigcup\limits_{i=0}^{n}
.

∎

8.3
Explicit expression for the stationary solution in case of detailed balance

In the following section we want to show, that in case of detailed balance, it is possible to compute the stationary solution
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}=\frac{\boldsymbol{T}}{\|\boldsymbol{T}\|_{1}}
in terms of transition rates.

Definition 33

(Detailed balance)
.

A probability vector
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}
is called
stationary
, if it lies in the kernel of the generator matrix
\Gamma
, that is
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}\in\text{kern }(\Gamma)
, or component-wise:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j\in\Omega\backslash\{i\}}\left(P_{*}^{(j)}\gamma_{j\to i}-P_{*}^{(i)}\gamma_{i\to j}\right)

(62)

When, in addition to being stationary, each summand
\left(\cdot\right)
in equation (
62
) vanishes, then we say, the stationary solution
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}
exhibits
detailed balance
.
Since we often encounter so called
‘non-normalizable’ stationary states
(that is vectors
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\in(\mathbb{R}_{>\,0})^{\Omega}
with
\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{X}_{*}=\boldsymbol{0}
, but
\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}=\infty
) we relax the definition to some extend, in order to better suit our requirements:

A master equation is said to satisfy
detailed balance
, if there exists a strictly positive vector
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\in(\mathbb{R}_{>\,0})^{\Omega}
, such that for all
j\in\Omega\backslash\{i\}
we have

\displaystyle X_{*}^{(j)}\gamma_{j\to i}

\displaystyle=X_{*}^{(i)}\gamma_{i\to j},

(gen. det. bal. )

independent of whether
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}
is normalizable or not.

An equivalent way to characterize detailed balance for Markov chains, is the so called
Kolmogorov criterion
(see
[
Kel11
]
)

Lemma 34

(Kolmogorov criterion)
.

A stationary solution of an irreducible, positive recurrent Markov chain exhibits the general detailed balance condition of equation (
gen. det. bal.
), if and only if, the
weight
of a closed walk does not depend on its direction, or in other words, there are nor ‘circular flows’. To be more precise, let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq\,2}
and
\boldsymbol{w}:=(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{n})\in\Omega^{n}
be a path in
\mathcal{S}
.

Then the system exhibits detailed balance, if and only if

\displaystyle\gamma_{\boldsymbol{w}_{\circlearrowleft}}

\displaystyle:=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n-1}\gamma_{\omega_{i}\to\omega_{i+1}}\cdot\gamma_{\omega_{n}\to\omega_{1}}=\gamma_{\omega_{1}\to\omega_{n}}\cdot\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n-1}\gamma_{\omega_{i+1}\to\omega_{i}}=:\gamma_{\boldsymbol{w}_{\circlearrowright}}.

(63)

Lemma 35

(The fraction of the weight of a path and its reverse path is path-independent)
.

For an irreducible Markov chain which exhibits detailed balance, then the product of the weight of a path, divided by the weight of its inverse path does not depend on the choice of the path, but only on its start- and end-point.

Proof.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega
be two different states and let
\boldsymbol{w}_{1}^{\to}(\alpha\rightsquigarrow\beta)
and
\boldsymbol{w}_{2}^{\to}(\alpha\rightsquigarrow\beta)
be two different path from state
\alpha
to state
\beta
.

By Kolmogorov’s criterion, we have:

\displaystyle\gamma_{\boldsymbol{w}_{1}^{\to}}(\alpha\rightsquigarrow\beta)\,\cdot\,\gamma_{\boldsymbol{w}_{2}^{\leftarrow}}(\beta\rightsquigarrow\alpha)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\text{Kolmogorov}}}\gamma_{\boldsymbol{w}_{2}^{\to}}(\alpha\rightsquigarrow\beta)\,\cdot\,\gamma_{\boldsymbol{w}_{1}^{\leftarrow}}(\beta\rightsquigarrow\alpha)

(64)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\frac{\gamma_{\boldsymbol{w}_{1}^{\to}}(\alpha\rightsquigarrow\beta)}{\gamma_{\boldsymbol{w}_{1}^{\leftarrow}}(\alpha\leftlsquigarrow\beta)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{\boldsymbol{w}_{2}^{\to}}(\alpha\rightsquigarrow\beta)}{\gamma_{\boldsymbol{w}_{2}^{\leftarrow}}(\alpha\leftlsquigarrow\beta)}

Figure
13
es meant to illustrate this result.

\boldsymbol{\beta}

\boldsymbol{\alpha}

\frac{\gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0,0.1172}\boldsymbol{w}_{1}}^{\to}}(\alpha\rightsquigarrow\beta)}{\gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0,0.1172}\boldsymbol{w}_{1}}^{\leftarrow}}(\alpha\leftlsquigarrow\beta)}=\frac{\hbox to37.2pt{\vbox to11.84pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.3711pt\lower-4.3711pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
