\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}=\frac{\gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3555,0.707}\boldsymbol{w}_{2}}^{\to}}(\alpha\rightsquigarrow\beta)}{\gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3555,0.707}\boldsymbol{w}_{2}}^{\leftarrow}}(\alpha\leftlsquigarrow\beta)}

Figure 13
:
Illustrating Kolmogorov’s criterion: The term
\frac{\gamma_{\boldsymbol{w}^{\to}}(\alpha\rightsquigarrow\beta)}{\gamma_{\boldsymbol{w}^{\leftarrow}}(\alpha\leftlsquigarrow\beta)}
is independent of the specific paths
{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0,0.1172}\boldsymbol{w}_{1}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3555,0.707}\boldsymbol{w}_{2}}
.

Hence, the term
\frac{\gamma_{\boldsymbol{w}^{\to}}(\alpha\rightsquigarrow\beta)}{\gamma_{\boldsymbol{w}^{\leftarrow}}(\alpha\leftlsquigarrow\beta)}
is independent of the path
\boldsymbol{w}
, which enables us to write
\frac{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\rightsquigarrow\omega}}{\gamma_{\omega\leftlsquigarrow\omega_{0}}}
for any path
\omega_{0}\rightsquigarrow\omega:=(\omega_{0}\to\dots\to\omega)
, where we set
\gamma_{\omega_{0}\rightsquigarrow\omega_{0}}:=1
.

∎

Lemma 36

(Candidate for stationary solution of irreducible networks with detailed balance)
.

For an irreducible Markov chain, the only candidate for a stationary solution is proportional to

\boldsymbol{X}_{*}:=\left(\frac{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\rightsquigarrow\omega}}{\gamma_{\omega\leftlsquigarrow\omega_{0}}}\right)_{\omega\in\Omega}
, in the sense that
\Gamma\boldsymbol{X}_{*}=\boldsymbol{0}
and
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\in(\mathbb{R}_{>\,0})^{\Omega}
. This implies that

\displaystyle\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{*}}{\left\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\right\|_{1}}

\displaystyle\in\begin{cases}\text{kern }(\Gamma)\cap\mathcal{PV}\cap(\mathbb{R}_{>\,0})^{\Omega}&\text{, if }\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}<\infty\text{ OR}\\
\{\boldsymbol{0}\}&\text{, if }\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}=\infty,\end{cases}

that is, Markov chain is
positive recurrent
, if and only if
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\in l^{1}(\Omega)
.

Proof.

Since the Markov chain is assumed to be irreducible, every state is reachable from any other state, that is
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\in(\mathbb{R}_{>\,0})^{\Omega}
. Let
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(i)
denote the set of
nearest neighbors
of state
i
. Then we have:

\displaystyle\left(\Gamma\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\right)^{(i)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j\in\Omega\backslash\{i\}}X_{*}^{(j)}\gamma_{j\to i}-X_{*}^{(i)}\gamma_{i\to j}

(65)

\displaystyle=\Bigl[\sum\limits_{j\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(i)}\underbrace{\left(\frac{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\rightsquigarrow j}}{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\leftlsquigarrow j}}\cdot\frac{\gamma_{j\to i}}{\gamma_{j\leftarrow i}}\right)}_{\frac{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\rightsquigarrow i}}{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\leftlsquigarrow i}}=X_{*}^{(i)}}\,\cdot\,\gamma_{i\to j}\Bigr]-X_{*}^{(i)}\,\underbrace{\sum\limits_{j\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(i)}\gamma_{i\to j}}_{\gamma_{i\to}}

\displaystyle=X_{*}^{(i)}\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{j\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}(i)}\gamma_{i\to j}\right)}_{\gamma_{i\to}}-X_{*}^{(i)}\gamma_{i\to}=0.

∎

Theorem 37

(Thermodynamic limit for irreducible, positive recurrent systems with detailed balance)
.

If an infinite dimensional, irreducible, positive recurrent master equation satisfies the (generalized) detailed balance condition of equation (
gen. det. bal.
), then the limiting solutions of the finite master equation converge in the thermodynamic limit to the limiting solution of the infinite dimensional master equations, that is
\boldsymbol{P}^{F}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0})\xlongrightarrow[\|\cdot\|_{1}]{|F|\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}_{*}
.

Proof.

Since the master equation is irreducible and positive recurrent, there exists a unique stationary solution
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}\in\mathcal{PV}\cap\,\text{kern }(\Gamma)\cap(\mathbb{R}_{>\,0})^{\Omega}
such that
\boldsymbol{P}\left(t\,\bigl|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\right)\xlongrightarrow[\|\cdot\|]{t\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{P}_{0})=\boldsymbol{P}_{*}
.

Since the master equation satisfies the detailed balance condition of equation (
gen. det. bal.
), we know by theorem
36
the exact form of this stationary solution, namely

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}_{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{*}}{\left\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\right\|_{1}}

(66)

\displaystyle\text{ with }\boldsymbol{X}_{*}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\rightsquigarrow\omega}}{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\leftlsquigarrow\omega}}\right)_{\omega\in\Omega}\in l^{1}(\Omega).

Since the detailed balance condition holds also for finite subsystems, we have:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}^{F}(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\frac{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\rightsquigarrow f}}{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\leftlsquigarrow f}}\right)_{f\in\Omega}}{\left\|\left(\frac{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\rightsquigarrow f}}{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\leftlsquigarrow f}}\right)_{f\in\Omega}\right\|_{1}}=\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{*}\xlongrightarrow[\|\cdot\|_{1}]{|F|\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}_{*}.

∎

Lemma 38

(Sufficient condition for detailed balance and positive recurrence)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a network such that for every link, the reverse link is also present, that is
\gamma_{\alpha\to\beta}>0\iff\gamma_{\beta\to\alpha}>0
. Moreover, let the fraction of the link strength between a link and its reverse link, be given by

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{\alpha\to\beta}}{\gamma_{\alpha\leftarrow\beta}}

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{\boldsymbol{w}_{\beta}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}}}{q^{\boldsymbol{w}_{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}}}=q^{(\boldsymbol{w}_{\beta}-\boldsymbol{w}_{\alpha})\cdot\boldsymbol{c}},

(suff.cond.det.bal.)

for some positive number
q>0
, some non-negative vector
\boldsymbol{c}\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq\,0})^{\mathbb{N}}
and
w:=d\circ b
. Then the network fulfills the - generalized - detailed balance condition of equation (
gen. det. bal.
).

Here,
d\,:\,\mathbb{N}_{0}\to\{0,1\}^{\infty}
is the dual representation of natural numbers (that is, for
\boldsymbol{d}_{n}:=d(n)
we have
n=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}d^{(i)}\,2^{i}=\boldsymbol{d}\cdot 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
) and
b\,:\,\Omega\to\mathbb{N}_{0}
is any bijection.

If, in addition,
\sum\limits_{N\in\mathbb{N}}q^{c^{(N)}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{N-1}(1+q^{c^{(i)}})<\infty
, the network is positive recurrent.

Proof.

Let
(\alpha=\omega_{0}\to\dots,\dots\omega_{n}=\beta)
be any path in
\mathcal{S}
from state
\alpha
to state
\beta
. Then the weight of a path, divided by the weight of its corresponding reverse path, depends only on the start - and endpoint:

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{\alpha\rightsquigarrow\beta}}{\gamma_{\alpha\leftlsquigarrow\beta}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\to\dots\to\omega_{n}}}{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\leftarrow\dots\leftarrow\omega_{n}}}=\prod\limits_{i=0}^{n-1}\underbrace{\frac{\gamma_{\omega_{i}\to\omega_{i+1}}}{\gamma_{\omega_{i}\leftarrow\omega_{i+1}}}}_{\frac{q^{\boldsymbol{c}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{\omega_{i+1}}}}{q^{\boldsymbol{c}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{\omega_{i}}}}}=\frac{\prod\limits_{i=i}^{n}q^{\boldsymbol{c}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{\omega_{i}}}}{\prod\limits_{i=0}^{n-1}q^{\boldsymbol{c}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{\omega_{i}}}}=\frac{q^{\boldsymbol{c}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{\beta}}}{q^{\boldsymbol{c}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{\alpha}}}

With
\omega_{0}:=b^{-1}(0)
and
\frac{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\rightsquigarrow\omega}}{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\leftlsquigarrow\omega}}=\frac{q^{\boldsymbol{c}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{\omega}}}{q^{\boldsymbol{c}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{\omega_{0}}}}{\mathrel{\to}^{\boldsymbol{w}_{\omega_{0}}=\boldsymbol{w}_{\boldsymbol{0}}=\boldsymbol{0}}}\frac{q^{\boldsymbol{c}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{\omega}}}{q^{\boldsymbol{c}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{\boldsymbol{0}}}}=q^{\boldsymbol{c}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{\omega}}
, we get the following expression for
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}_{*}

\displaystyle=\left(\underbrace{\frac{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\rightsquigarrow\omega}}{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\leftlsquigarrow\omega}}}_{q^{\boldsymbol{c}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{\omega}}}\right)_{\omega\in\Omega}

(67)

\displaystyle=\left(\bigl(q^{0},q^{c^{(1)}}\bigr),\underbrace{q^{c^{(2)}},q^{c^{(2)}+c^{(1)}}}_{q^{c^{(2)}}\,\bigl(q^{0},q^{c^{(1)}}\bigr)},\underbrace{q^{c^{(3)}},q^{c^{(3)}+c^{(1)}},q^{c^{(3)}+c^{(2)}},q^{c^{(3)}+c^{(2)}+c^{(1)}}}_{q^{c^{(3)}}\,\bigl(q^{0},q^{c^{(1)}},q^{c^{(2)}},q^{c^{(2)}+c^{(1)}}\bigr)},\dots\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\underbrace{\underbrace{\bigl(q^{0},q^{c^{(1)}}\bigr),q^{c^{(2)}}\,\bigl(q^{0},q^{c^{(1)}}\bigr)}_{(1,q^{c^{(2)}})\otimes(1,q^{c^{(1)}})},q^{c^{(3)}}\,\underbrace{\bigl(q^{0},q^{c^{(1)}},q^{c^{(2)}},q^{c^{(2)}+c^{(1)}}\bigr)}_{(1,q^{c^{(2)}})\otimes(1,q^{c^{(1)}})}}_{(1,q^{c^{(3)}})\otimes 1,q^{c^{(2)}})\otimes(1,q^{c^{(1)}})},\dots\right)

\displaystyle=\bigotimes\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\,(1,q^{c^{(n)}}):=\otimes\dots\otimes(1,q^{c^{(3)}})\otimes(1,q^{c^{(2)}})\otimes(1,q^{c^{(1)}}).

This will be discussed in more details in the section about the infinite dimensional hypercube
11
.

The network is positive recurrent, if and only if
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}=\left(\frac{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\rightsquigarrow\omega}}{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\leftlsquigarrow\omega}}\right)_{\omega\in\Omega}\in l^{1}(\Omega)
. We can compute the norm of
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}
to

\displaystyle\infty

\displaystyle\overset{!}{>}\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}=\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}X^{(\omega)}=\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}\frac{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\rightsquigarrow\omega}}{\gamma_{\omega_{0}\leftlsquigarrow\omega}}=\sum\limits_{\boldsymbol{w}\in\{0,1\}^{\infty}}q^{\boldsymbol{w}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}}=\sum\limits_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sum\limits_{\boldsymbol{w}_{N}\in\{0,1\}^{N-1}\times\{1\}}q^{\boldsymbol{w}_{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{c}}

(suff.cond.pos.rec.)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{N\in\mathbb{N}}q^{c^{(N)}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{N-1}(1+q^{c^{(i)}}).

Clearly, this does not converge, if
\boldsymbol{c}
is a constant vector, that is
\boldsymbol{c}=\text{const}\cdot(1,1,\dots)
, since
\sum\limits_{N\in\mathbb{N}}q(1+q)^{N-1}=\infty
.

It does converge, however, for
\boldsymbol{c}\in\{(n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(n^{2})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(2^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\}
, as can be seen, by e.g. applying the ratio test (see fact
63
).

∎

9
A first non-trivial example: the linear chain with one open end

In the following section we focus on the following generator

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-\gamma_{0\to 1}&\gamma_{1\to 0}&0&0&\dots&\dots\\
\gamma_{0\to 1}&-\gamma_{1\to}&\gamma_{2\to 1}&&\dots&\dots\\
0&\gamma_{1\to 2}&-\gamma_{2\to}&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&\gamma_{2\to 3}&\ddots&\gamma_{n\to n-1}&\dots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&-\gamma_{n\to}&\dots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\gamma_{n\to n+1}&\dots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\end{pmatrix},

(68)

with
\gamma_{n\to}:=\sum\limits_{i\in\Omega}\gamma_{n\to i}
, or component-wise

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(m,n)}

\displaystyle=\delta_{n,0}\cdot\left(-\gamma_{0\to 1}\delta_{m,0}+\gamma_{0\to 1}\delta_{m,1}\right)

(69)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(1-\delta_{n,0})\cdot\Bigl(\delta_{m,n-1}\,\gamma_{n\to n-1}-\delta_{m,n}\,(\gamma_{n\to n-1}+\gamma_{n\to n+1})+\delta_{m,n+1}\,\gamma_{n\to n+1}\Bigr),

which corresponds to an infinite long chain with one open end:

0
1
2
\dots
n-1
\;\;\;n\;\;\;
n+1
\dots
\gamma_{0\to 1}
\gamma_{1\to 2}
\gamma_{n-1\to n}
\gamma_{n\to n+1}
\gamma_{2\to 1}
\gamma_{1\to 0}
\gamma_{n+1\to n}
\gamma_{n\to n-1}

Figure 14
:
The linear chain with one open end

9.1
The thermodynamic limit for the linear chain with one open end

The master equation for the linear chain with one open end satisfies the (generalized) detailed balance condition of equation (
gen. det. bal.
) for

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}_{*}

\displaystyle:=\left(\frac{\gamma_{0\rightsquigarrow n}}{\gamma_{0\leftlsquigarrow n}}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}=\prod\limits_{i=0}^{n-1}\frac{\gamma_{i\to i+1}}{\gamma_{i\leftarrow i+1}},

(70)

since we have for
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
:

\displaystyle\underbrace{X_{*}^{(n)}}_{\frac{\gamma_{0\rightsquigarrow n}}{\gamma_{0\leftlsquigarrow n}}}\,\gamma_{n\to n\pm 1}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\prod\limits_{i=0}^{n-1}\frac{\gamma_{i\to i+1}}{\gamma_{i\leftarrow i+1}}\right)\,\frac{\gamma_{n\to n\pm 1}}{\gamma_{n\leftarrow n\pm 1}}}_{\prod\limits_{i=0}^{n\pm 1}\frac{\gamma_{i\to i+1}}{\gamma_{i\leftarrow i+1}}}\,\gamma_{n\pm 1\to n}=\underbrace{\left(\prod\limits_{i=0}^{n\pm 1-1}\frac{\gamma_{i\to i+1}}{\gamma_{i\leftarrow i+1}}\right)}_{X_{*}^{(n\pm 1)}}\,\gamma_{n\pm 1\to n}=X_{*}^{(n\pm 1)}\,\gamma_{n\pm 1\to n}.

An alternative way to see the (generalized) detailed balance condition is to notice, that since there is exactly one path from the state
0
to any other state
n
, so clearly
\frac{\gamma_{0\rightsquigarrow n}}{\gamma_{0\leftlsquigarrow n}}
does not depend on the path
0\rightsquigarrow n
. The following figure is meant to illustrate this:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}^{F}_{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}^{F}}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{T_{\to\boldsymbol{0}}}\\
\\
\gamma_{T_{\to 1}}\\
\\
\vdots\\
\\
\gamma_{T_{\to f}}\\
\\
\vdots\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}^{F}}\begin{pmatrix}\underbrace{\hbox to130.48pt{\vbox to18.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.62872pt\lower-9.04298pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

Figure 15
:
Illustrating the fact, that the stationary solution for a finite linear chain can be expressed not only in terms of in-trees
\boldsymbol{P}^{F}_{*}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}^{F}}\left(\gamma_{T_{\to f}}\right)_{f\in F}
, but also in terms the paths from- and to zero:
\boldsymbol{P}^{F}_{*}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}^{F}}\left(\frac{\gamma_{0\rightsquigarrow f}}{\gamma_{0\leftlsquigarrow f}}\right)_{f\in F}

By theorem
36
, the only candidate for a stationary solution is
\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{*}}{\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}}
. When
\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}=\infty
, no stationary solution exists, when
\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}<\infty
, then the master equation of the linear chain with one open end is
positive recurrent
(theorem
36
) and its stationary solution can be approximated by corresponding finite subsystems in the thermodynamic limit (theorem
37
).

When combining these results, we can conclude that for the linear chain with one open end we have:

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\,\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0})=\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0})=

(71)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\text{irreducible + }}_{\text{positive recurrent}}}\boldsymbol{P}_{*}{\mathrel{\to}^{\text{ thm }\ref{Theorem_DetailedBalanceApproximatingStationarySolutionByFiniteSubsystems}}_{\text{detailed balance}}}\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\,\underbrace{\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{*}}_{\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}^{F}(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0})}

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\,\underbrace{\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}^{F}(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0})}_{\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\,\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0})}=\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\,\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\,\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0})

Claim 39

(A simplified version for specific rates)
.

If for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{n\to n+1}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{n+1}\,x\text{ and }

\displaystyle\gamma_{n\leftarrow n+1}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{n}\,(1-x)

for some
x\in(0,1)
some sequence
(\lambda_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in l^{1}(N_{0},\mathbb{R}_{>\,0})
with
\lambda:=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\sqrt[n]{\lambda_{n}}
, then
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}=\left(\frac{\lambda_{n}}{\lambda_{0}}\,\left(\frac{x}{1-x}\right)^{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
and we have
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\in l^{1}(\mathbb{N}_{0})
, if and only if
x\in\left(0,\frac{1}{1+\lambda}\right)
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle X_{*}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{0\rightsquigarrow n}}{\gamma_{0\leftlsquigarrow n}}=\prod\limits_{i=0}^{n-1}\underbrace{\frac{\gamma_{i\to i+1}}{\gamma_{i\leftarrow i+1}}}_{\frac{\lambda_{i+1}\,q}{\lambda_{i}\,q}}=\frac{\lambda_{n}}{\lambda_{0}}\,\left(\frac{x}{1-x}\right)^{n}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\infty

\displaystyle>\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}X_{*}^{(n)}=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{\lambda_{n}}{\lambda_{0}}\,\left(\frac{x}{1-x}\right)^{n}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\to}^{\text{root}}_{\text{test}}}1

\displaystyle>\limsup\limits_{n\to\infty}\sqrt[n]{\frac{\lambda_{n}}{\lambda_{0}}\,\left(\frac{x}{1-x}\right)^{n}}=\lambda\,\left(\frac{x}{1-x}\right)

\displaystyle\iff x

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{1+\lambda}

∎

9.2
Computing the kernel of the generator of the linear chain on
\mathbb{N}_{0}
‘by foot’

Since the linear chain satisfies the detailed balance condition (
gen. det. bal.
), we are able to directly write down the only candidate for the stationary solution, as we did in equation (
70
).

With the help of the two lemmata
6
and
30
we can validate this guess, without computing the kernel of
\Gamma
directly.

For illustration purposes, we do this now ‘by foot’.

Claim 40
.

Let
\widetilde{\text{kern }(\Gamma)}:=\{\boldsymbol{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}\,:\,\Gamma\boldsymbol{X}\text{ exists and }\Gamma\boldsymbol{X}=\boldsymbol{0}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}\}
(note that

\text{kern }(\Gamma)=\widetilde{\text{kern }(\Gamma)}\cap l^{1}(\mathbb{N}_{0})
), then
\widetilde{\text{kern }(\Gamma)}=\text{span }(\boldsymbol{X}_{*})
, where
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}
is given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}_{*}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\gamma_{0\rightsquigarrow n}}{\gamma_{0\leftlsquigarrow n}}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=\left(1,\left(\prod\limits_{i=0}^{n-1}\frac{\gamma_{i\to i+1}}{\gamma_{i\leftarrow i+1}}\right)_{n\geq\,1}\right)

(72)

\displaystyle=\left(1,\frac{\gamma_{0\to 1}}{\gamma_{1\to 0}},\frac{\gamma_{0\to 1}}{\gamma_{2\to 1}}\frac{\gamma_{1\to 2}}{\gamma_{1\to 0}},\frac{\gamma_{0\to 1}}{\gamma_{3\to 2}}\,\frac{\gamma_{1\to 2}\,\gamma_{2\to 3}}{\gamma_{2\to 1}\,\gamma_{1\to 0}},\dots\right).=\left(1,\frac{\gamma_{0\to 1}}{\gamma_{0\leftarrow 1}},\frac{\gamma_{0\to 1}\,\gamma_{1\to 2}}{\gamma_{0\leftarrow 1}\,\gamma_{1\leftarrow 2}},\frac{\gamma_{0\to 1}\,\gamma_{1\to 2}\,\gamma_{2\to 3}}{\gamma_{0\leftarrow 1}\,\gamma_{1\leftarrow 2}\,\gamma_{2\leftarrow 3}}\right)

where
(a,(b_{n})_{n\geq 1}):=(a,b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},\dots)
.

proof of ”
\text{span }(\boldsymbol{X}_{*})=\widetilde{\text{kern }(\Gamma)}
”.

Note: we have

\displaystyle X_{*}^{(m)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{0\to 1}}{\gamma_{m\to m-1}}\,\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m-2}\frac{\gamma_{i\to i+1}}{\gamma_{i\to i-1}}\frac{\gamma_{m-1\to m}}{\gamma_{m-1\to m-2}}=\underbrace{\left(\frac{\gamma_{0\to 1}}{\gamma_{m-1\to m-2}}\,\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m-2}\frac{\gamma_{i\to i+1}}{\gamma_{i\to i-1}}\right)}_{X_{*}^{(m-1)}}\frac{\gamma_{m-1\to m}}{\gamma_{m\to m-1}}

”
\subseteq
”:

\displaystyle(\Gamma\boldsymbol{X}_{*})^{(m)}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{m=0}}-\underbrace{X_{*}^{(0)}}_{1}\,\gamma_{0\to 1}+\underbrace{X_{*}^{(1)}}_{\frac{\gamma_{0\to 1}}{\gamma_{1\to 0}}}\,\gamma_{1\to 0}=0

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{m\geq 1}}X_{*}^{(m-1)}\,\gamma_{m-1\to m}-\underbrace{X_{*}^{(m)}}_{X_{*}^{(m-1)}\,\frac{\gamma_{{m-1}\to m}}{\gamma_{m\to m-1}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\gamma_{m\to}}+\underbrace{X_{*}^{(m+1)}}_{X_{*}^{(m-1)}\,\frac{\gamma_{m-1\to m}}{\gamma_{m\to m-1}}\,\frac{\gamma_{m}\to m+1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\gamma_{m+1\to m}}}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\gamma_{m+1\to m}}

\displaystyle=X_{*}^{(m-1)}\,\gamma_{{m-1}\to m}\underbrace{\left(1-\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\gamma_{m\to m-1}+\gamma_{m\to m+1}}}{\gamma_{m\to m-1}}+\frac{\gamma_{m\to m+1}}{\gamma_{m\to m-1}}\right)}_{0}=0.

”
\supseteq
”:

Let
\boldsymbol{X}\in\widetilde{\text{kern }(\Gamma)}
, that is
\Gamma\boldsymbol{X}=\boldsymbol{0}
. It suffices to show that
X^{(n+1)}=X^{(n)}\cdot\frac{\gamma_{n\to n+1}}{\gamma_{n+1\to n}}
, which we will prove by induction:

i-start:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{!}}(\Gamma\boldsymbol{X})^{(0)}=-X^{(0)}\,\gamma_{0\to 1}+X^{(1)}\,\gamma_{1\to 0}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow X^{(1)}

\displaystyle=X^{(0)}\cdot\frac{\gamma_{0\to 1}}{\gamma_{1\to 0}}

i-step:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{!}}(\Gamma\boldsymbol{X})^{(m)}=X^{(m-1)}\,\gamma_{m-1\to m}-\underbrace{X^{(m)}}_{X^{(m-1)}\,\frac{\gamma_{m-1\to m}}{\gamma_{m\to m-1}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\gamma_{m\to}+X^{(m+1)}\,\gamma_{m+1\to m}=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\text{i-hypothesis}}}X^{(m-1)}\,\gamma_{m-1\to m}\underbrace{\left(1-\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\gamma_{m\to m-1}+\gamma_{m\to m+1}}{\gamma_{m\to m-1}}\right)}_{-\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\gamma_{m\to m+1}}{\gamma_{m\to m-1}}}+X^{(m+1)}\gamma_{m+1\to m}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow X^{(m+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\gamma_{m+1\to m}}\underbrace{\left(X^{(m-1)}\frac{\gamma_{m-1\to m}}{\gamma_{m\to m-1}}\right)}_{X^{(m)}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\gamma_{m\to m+1}}=

\displaystyle=X^{(m)}\,\frac{\gamma_{m\to m+1}}{\gamma_{m+1\to m}}.

∎

This means, that we have

\displaystyle\text{kern }(\Gamma)=\underbrace{\widetilde{\text{kern }(\Gamma)}}_{\text{span }(\boldsymbol{X}_{*})}\,\cap\,l^{1}(\mathbb{N}_{0})=\text{span }\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{*}}{\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}}\right)=\begin{cases}\text{span }(\boldsymbol{X}_{*})&\text{, if }\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|<\infty\\
\{\boldsymbol{0}\}&\text{, if }\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|=\infty.\end{cases}

(KernOfTheGenOnN)

10
The linear chain with two open ends

In the following section we focus on the following generator

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\ddots&0&0&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\ldots&\gamma_{-n\to-n-1}&0&0&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\ldots&-\gamma_{-n\to}&\ddots&0&0&0&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\ldots&\gamma_{-n\to-n+1}&\ddots&\gamma_{-1\to-2}&0&0&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\ldots&0&\ddots&-\gamma_{-1\to}&\gamma_{0\to-1}&0&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\vdots&0&0&\gamma_{-1\to 0}&-\gamma_{0\to}&\gamma_{1\to 0}&\dots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\vdots&0&0&\gamma_{0\to 1}&-\gamma_{1\to}&\dots&0&\vdots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&0&0&\gamma_{1\to 2}&\ddots&0&\vdots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&0&0&\ddots&\gamma_{n\to n-1}&\dots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&0&\ddots&-\gamma_{n\to}&\dots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\gamma_{n\to n+1}&\dots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\end{pmatrix}

(73)

\displaystyle\text{or component-wise }

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(m,n)}

\displaystyle=\delta_{m,n-1}\,\gamma_{n\to n-1}-\delta_{m,n}\,(\gamma_{n\to n-1}+\gamma_{n\to n+1})+\delta_{m,n+1}\,\gamma_{n\to n+1}

\displaystyle\text{for }m,n\in\mathbb{Z},

\displaystyle\text{which corresponds to an infinite long chain with \emph{two} open ends: }

10.1
The thermodynamic limit for the linear chain on
\mathbb{Z}

The master equation for the linear chain with
two
open ends satisfies - similarly two the previous section - the (generalized) detailed balance condition of equation (
gen. det. bal.
) for

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}_{*}:=\left(\left(\prod\limits_{i\in\{0,\dots,z+1\}}\frac{\gamma_{i\to i-1}}{\gamma_{i\leftarrow i-1}}\right)_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{<\,0}},1,\left(\prod\limits_{i\in\{0,\dots,z-1\}}\frac{\gamma_{i\to i+1}}{\gamma_{i\leftarrow i+1}}\right)_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{>\,0}}\right)=\begin{cases}\prod\limits_{i\in\{0,\dots,z-1\}}\frac{\gamma_{i\to i+1}}{\gamma_{i\leftarrow i+1}}&\text{ , if $z>0$}\\
\hskip 19.91692pt1&\text{ , if $z=0$}\\
\prod\limits_{i\in\{0,\dots,z+1\}}\frac{\gamma_{i\to i-1}}{\gamma_{i\leftarrow i-1}}&\text{ , if $z<0$}\end{cases}

(74)

since we have:

\displaystyle\underbrace{X_{*}^{(z)}}_{\prod\limits_{i\in\{0,\dots,z{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mp}1\}}\frac{\gamma_{i\to i+1}}{\gamma_{i\leftarrow i+1}}}\,\gamma_{z\to z\pm 1}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\prod\limits_{i\in\{0,\dots,z-1\}}\frac{\gamma_{i\to i+1}}{\gamma_{i\leftarrow i+1}}\right)\,\frac{\gamma_{z\to z\pm 1}}{\gamma_{z\leftarrow z\pm 1}}}_{\prod\limits_{i\in\{0,\dots,z\pm 1{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mp}1\}}\frac{\gamma_{i\to i+1}}{\gamma_{i\leftarrow i+1}}}\,\gamma_{z\pm 1\to z}=\underbrace{\left(\prod\limits_{i\in\{0,\dots,z\pm 1{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mp}1\}}\frac{\gamma_{i\to i+1}}{\gamma_{i\leftarrow i+1}}\right)}_{X_{*}^{(z\pm 1)}}\,\gamma_{z\pm 1\to z}

\displaystyle=X_{*}^{(z\pm 1)}\,\gamma_{z\pm 1\to z}\text{ for }z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{>\,0}\atop{<\,0}}

Thus, the linear chain with
two
open ends behaves analogously to the linear chain with
one
open end:

By theorem
36
, the only candidate for a stationary solution is
\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{*}}{\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}}
. When
\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}=\infty
, no stationary solution exists, when
\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}<\infty
, then the master equation of the linear chain with one open end is
positive recurrent
(theorem
36
) and its stationary solution can be approximated by corresponding finite subsystems in the thermodynamic limit (theorem
37
).

Similarly to the linear chain with one open end, we can conclude:

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\,\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0})=\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0})=

(75)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\text{irreducible + }}_{\text{positive recurrent}}}\boldsymbol{P}_{*}{\mathrel{\to}^{\text{ thm }\ref{Theorem_DetailedBalanceApproximatingStationarySolutionByFiniteSubsystems}}_{\text{detailed balance}}}\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\,\underbrace{\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{*}}_{\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}^{F}(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0})}

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\,\underbrace{\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}^{F}(\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0})}_{\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\,\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0})}=\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\,\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\,\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0}).

Claim 41

(A simplified version for specific rates)
.

If for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{n\to n+1}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{n+1}\,x

\displaystyle\gamma_{n\leftarrow n+1}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{n}\,(1-x)

and

\displaystyle\gamma_{-n\to-n-1}

\displaystyle=\mu_{n+1}\,y

\displaystyle\gamma_{-n\leftarrow-n-1}

\displaystyle=\mu_{n}\,(1-y)

for some
x,y>0
, some sequences
(\lambda_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in l^{1}(N_{0},\mathbb{R}_{>\,0})
with
\lambda:=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\sqrt[n]{\lambda_{n}}
and
\mu:=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\sqrt[n]{\mu_{n}}
, respectively, then
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}=\left(\left(\frac{\mu_{n}}{\mu}\,\left(\frac{y}{1-y}\right)^{-z}\right)_{z\in Z_{<\,0}},1,\left(\frac{\lambda_{n}}{\lambda_{0}}\,\left(\frac{x}{1-x}\right)^{z}\right)_{z\in Z_{>\,0}}\right)
and we have
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\in l^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
, if and only if
(x,y)\in\left(0,\frac{1}{1+\lambda}\right)\times\left(0,\frac{1}{1+\mu}\right)
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle X_{*}^{(z)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{0\rightsquigarrow z}}{\gamma_{0\leftlsquigarrow z}}=\begin{cases}\prod\limits_{i=0}^{z-1}\overbrace{\frac{\gamma_{i\to i+1}}{\gamma_{i\leftarrow i-1}}}^{\frac{\lambda_{i+1}\,x}{\lambda_{i}\,x}}=\frac{\lambda_{z}}{\lambda_{0}}\,\left(\frac{q}{1-q}\right)^{z}&\text{ , if $z>0$, }\\
\hskip 19.91692pt1&\text{ , if $z=0$ }\\
\prod\limits_{i=0}^{z+1}\underbrace{\frac{\gamma_{i\to i-1}}{\gamma_{i\leftarrow i-1}}}_{\frac{\mu_{i+1}\,y}{\mu_{i}\,y}}=\frac{\mu_{z}}{\mu_{0}}\,\left(\frac{y}{1-y}\right)^{z}&\text{ , if $z<0$, }\end{cases}

and therefore

\displaystyle\infty

\displaystyle>\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}=\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{<\,0}}X_{*}^{(z)}+1+\sum\limits_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{>\,0}}X_{*}^{(z)}=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{\mu_{n}}{\mu_{0}}\,\left(\frac{y}{1-y}\right)^{n}+1+\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{\lambda_{n}}{\lambda_{0}}\,\left(\frac{x}{1-x}\right)^{n}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{\text{root}}_{\text{test}}}\begin{cases}1>\limsup\limits_{n\to\infty}\sqrt[n]{\frac{\lambda_{n}}{\lambda_{0}}\,\left(\frac{x}{1-x}\right)^{n}}=\lambda\,\left(\frac{x}{1-x}\right)\text{ and }\\
1>\limsup\limits_{n\to\infty}\sqrt[n]{\frac{\mu_{n}}{\mu_{0}}\,\left(\frac{y}{1-y}\right)^{n}}=\mu\,\left(\frac{y}{1-y}\right)\end{cases}

\displaystyle\iff(x,y)\in\left(0,\frac{1}{1+\lambda}\right)\times\left(0,\frac{1}{1+\mu}\right)

∎

10.2
Computing the kernel of the generator of the linear chain on
\mathbb{Z}
‘by foot’

Claim 42
.

Let
\widetilde{\text{kern }(\Gamma)}:=\{\boldsymbol{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}}\,:\,\Gamma\boldsymbol{X}\text{ exists and }\Gamma\boldsymbol{X}=\boldsymbol{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}\}
be defined as above. Then
\widetilde{\text{kern }(\Gamma)}=\text{span }(\boldsymbol{X}_{*},\boldsymbol{Y}_{*})
, where
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}
and
\boldsymbol{Y}_{*}
are given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}_{*}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod\limits_{\alpha\leq z-1}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}\,\cdot\,\prod\limits_{\beta\geq z+1}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}\right)_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}

(76)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Y}_{*}

\displaystyle=\left(\left((-1)\sum\limits_{j=1}^{-m}\frac{\prod\limits_{\beta=m+1}^{-j}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}{\prod\limits_{\alpha=m}^{-j}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}\right)_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{<0}},0,\left(\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\prod\limits_{\alpha=j}^{n-1}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}{\prod\limits_{\beta=j}^{n}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\right)=

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\frac{(-1)}{\gamma_{m\to m+1}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{-m}\prod\limits_{\epsilon=m+1}^{-j}\frac{\gamma_{\epsilon\to\epsilon-1}}{\gamma_{\epsilon\to\epsilon+1}}\right)_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{<0}},0,\left(\frac{1}{\gamma_{n\to n-1}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\prod\limits_{\epsilon=j}^{n-1}\frac{\gamma_{\epsilon\to\epsilon+1}}{\gamma_{\epsilon\to\epsilon-1}}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\right)

Proof.

In order to prove this, we consider the following:

\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\in\widetilde{\text{kern }(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle X_{*}^{(z-1)}\gamma_{z-1\to z}+X_{*}^{(z+1)}\gamma_{z+1\to z}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod\limits_{\alpha\leq z-2}{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0,0.1016}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}\prod\limits_{\beta\geq z}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}\right){\color[rgb]{0.9023,0,0.1016}\gamma_{z-1\to z}}+\left(\prod\limits_{\alpha\leq z}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}\prod\limits_{\beta\geq z+2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}\right){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\gamma_{z+1\to z}}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod\limits_{\alpha\leq z-1}{\color[rgb]{0.9023,0,0.1016}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}\prod\limits_{\beta\geq z}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}\right)+\left(\prod\limits_{\alpha\leq z}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}\prod\limits_{\beta\geq z+1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\prod\limits_{\alpha\leq z-1}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}\prod\limits_{\beta\geq z+1}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}\right)}_{X_{*}^{(z)}}\,\cdot\,\left(\gamma_{z\to z+1}+\gamma_{z\to z-1}\right)

The most direct way to argue would be the following: If
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\in l^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
, then
\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{*}}{\left\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\right\|_{1}}\in\text{kern }(\Gamma)
and since we know from lemma
4.4
that the dimension of the kernel of an irreducible system is at most one dimensional, we can conclude that the kernel of the generator is spanned by

\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{*}}{\left\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\right\|_{1}}
, or in short:

\displaystyle\left.\begin{array}[]{c c}\left.\begin{array}[]{c }\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\in\widetilde{\text{kern }(\Gamma)}\\
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\in l^{1}(\mathbb{Z})\end{array}\right\}\Longrightarrow&\boldsymbol{0}\neq\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{*}}{\left\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\right\|_{1}}\in\text{kern }(\Gamma)\\
&\text{dim}(\text{kern }(\Gamma))=1\end{array}\right\}\Longrightarrow\text{kern }(\Gamma)=\text{span }\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{*}}{\left\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\right\|_{1}}\right)

For illustration purposes, we will continue to show that while
\boldsymbol{Y}_{*}\in\widetilde{\Gamma}
, it is not normalizable (
\|\boldsymbol{Y}_{*}\|_{1}=\infty
),

\boldsymbol{Y}_{*}\in\widetilde{\text{kern }(\Gamma)}

Since
\boldsymbol{Y}_{*}
looks different on the negative numbers than it does on the positive ones, we divide the proof in to three steps:

(
n>0
)

\displaystyle Y_{*}^{(n-1)}\gamma_{n-1\to n}+Y_{*}^{(n+1)}\gamma_{n+1\to n}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n-1}\left(\underbrace{\frac{\prod\limits_{\alpha=j}^{n-2}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}{\prod\limits_{\beta=j}^{n-1}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}\gamma_{n-1\to n}}_{\frac{\prod\limits_{\alpha=j}^{n-1}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}{\prod\limits_{\beta=j}^{n-1}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}}\right)+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n+1}\Bigl(\underbrace{\frac{\prod\limits_{\alpha=j}^{n}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}{\prod\limits_{\beta=j}^{n+1}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}\gamma_{n+1\to n}}_{\frac{\prod\limits_{\alpha=j}^{n}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}{\prod\limits_{\beta=j}^{n}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n-1}\frac{\prod\limits_{\alpha=j}^{n-1}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}{\prod\limits_{\beta=j}^{n-1}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}\underbrace{\left[1+\frac{\gamma_{n\to n+1}}{\gamma_{n\to n-1}}\right]}_{\frac{\gamma_{n\to n+1}+\gamma_{n\to n-1}}{\gamma_{n\to n-1}}}+\underbrace{\left(\frac{\gamma_{n\to n+1}}{\gamma_{n\to n-1}}+1\right)}_{\frac{\gamma_{n\to n+1}+\gamma_{n\to n-1}}{\gamma_{n\to n-1}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\gamma_{n\to n+1}+\gamma_{n\to n-1}\right)\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\prod\limits_{\alpha=j}^{n-1}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}{\prod\limits_{\beta=j}^{n}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}\right)}_{Y_{*}^{(n)}}

(n=0)

\displaystyle\underbrace{Y_{*}^{(-1)}}_{\frac{-1}{\gamma_{-1\to 0}}}\,\gamma_{-1\to 0}+\underbrace{Y_{*}^{(1)}}_{\frac{1}{\gamma_{1\to 0}}}\,\gamma_{1\to 0}

\displaystyle=0=Y_{*}^{(0)}\,\gamma_{0\to}.

(
m<0
)

\displaystyle Y_{*}^{(m-1)}\gamma_{m-1\to m}+Y_{*}^{(m+1)}\gamma_{m+1\to n}

\displaystyle=(-1)\sum\limits_{j=1}^{-m-1}\underbrace{\frac{\prod\limits_{\beta=m}^{-j}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}{\prod\limits_{\alpha=m-1}^{-j}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}\gamma_{m-1\to m}}_{\frac{\prod\limits_{\beta=m}^{-j}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}{\prod\limits_{\alpha=m}^{-j}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}}-\sum\limits_{j=1}^{-m+1}\underbrace{\frac{\prod\limits_{\beta=m+2}^{-j}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}{\prod\limits_{\alpha=m+1}^{-j}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}\gamma_{m+1\to m}}_{\frac{\prod\limits_{\beta=m+1}^{-j}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}{\prod\limits_{\alpha=m+1}^{-j}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}}

\displaystyle=(-1)\sum\limits_{j=1}^{-m+1}\frac{\prod\limits_{\beta=m+1}^{-j}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}{\prod\limits_{\alpha=m+1}^{-j}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}\underbrace{\left[1+\frac{\gamma_{m\to m-1}}{\gamma_{m\to m+1}}\right]}_{\frac{\gamma_{m\to m-1}+\gamma_{m\to m+1}}{\gamma_{m\to m+1}}}+\underbrace{\left[1+\frac{\gamma_{m\to m-1}}{\gamma_{m\to m+1}}\right]}_{\frac{\gamma_{m\to m-1}+\gamma_{m\to m+1}}{\gamma_{m\to m+1}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\gamma_{m\to m-1}+\gamma_{m\to m+1}\right)\underbrace{\left((-1)\sum\limits_{j=1}^{-m}\frac{\prod\limits_{\beta=m+1}^{-j}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}{\prod\limits_{\alpha=m}^{-j}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}\right)}_{Y_{*}^{(m)}}

”
\subseteq
”

Let
\boldsymbol{Z}_{*}\in\widetilde{\text{kern }(\Gamma)}
. We first show that the following quantity

\displaystyle c^{(z)}:=Z_{*}^{(z)}\,\gamma_{z\to z+1}-Z_{*}^{(z+1)}\,\gamma_{z+1\to z}

(77)

is independent of
z\in\mathbb{Z}
:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\left(\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{Z}_{*}\right)^{(z)}=Z_{*}^{(z-1)}\,\gamma_{z-1\to z}-Z_{*}^{(z)}(\gamma_{z\to z-1}+\gamma_{z\to z+1})+Z_{*}^{(z+1)}\,\gamma_{z+1\to z}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow c^{(z)}

\displaystyle=Z_{*}^{(z)}\,\gamma_{z\to z+1}-Z_{*}^{(z+1)}\,\gamma_{z+1\to z}=Z_{*}^{(z-1)}\,\gamma_{z-1\to z}-Z_{*}^{(z)}\,\gamma_{z\to z-1}=c^{(z-1)}.

Let us now fix
z_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
. We can now write
Z_{*}^{(z_{0}\pm 1)}
in terms of
Z_{*}^{(z_{0})}
and
c

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle=Z_{*}^{(z_{0})}\,\gamma_{z_{0}\to z_{0}+1}-Z_{*}^{(z_{0}+1)}\,\gamma_{z_{0}+1\to z_{0}}

(78)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow Z_{*}^{(z_{0}\pm 1)}

\displaystyle=Z_{*}^{(z_{0})}\frac{\gamma_{z_{0}\to z_{0}\pm 1}}{\gamma_{z_{0}\pm 1\to z_{0}}}\mp\frac{c}{\gamma_{z_{0}\pm 1\to z_{0}}}

and by induction, it follows that for all
z\in\mathbb{Z}
and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\displaystyle Z_{*}^{(z+n)}

\displaystyle=Z_{*}^{(z)}\,\frac{\prod\limits_{\alpha=z}^{z+n-1}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}{\prod\limits_{\beta=z+1}^{z+n}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}-c\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\prod\limits_{\alpha=z+j}^{z+n-1}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}{\prod\limits_{\beta=z+j}^{z+n}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}\text{ and}

(79)

\displaystyle Z_{*}^{(z-n)}

\displaystyle=Z_{*}^{(z)}\,\frac{\prod\limits_{\beta=z-n+1}^{z}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}{\prod\limits_{\alpha=z-n}^{z-1}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}+c\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\prod\limits_{\beta=z-n+1}^{z-j}\gamma_{\beta\to\beta-1}}{\prod\limits_{\alpha=z-n}^{z-j}\gamma_{\alpha\to\alpha+1}}

(see lemma
64
in the appendix for a proof). From this expression we can deduce that
\boldsymbol{Z}_{*}=\frac{Z_{*}^{(0)}}{X_{*}^{(0)}}\,\boldsymbol{X}_{*}-c\,\boldsymbol{Y}_{*}
is a linear combination of
\boldsymbol{X}_{*}
and
\boldsymbol{Y}_{*}
, as the calculation in lemma
65
shows.

∎

We note that
\boldsymbol{Y}_{*}\notin l^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
, since for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\displaystyle Y_{*}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\gamma_{n\to n-1}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\prod\limits_{\epsilon=j}^{n-1}\frac{\gamma_{\epsilon\to\epsilon+1}}{\gamma_{\epsilon\to\epsilon-1}}\overset{j=n}{\geq}\frac{1}{\gamma_{n\to n-1}}\xlongrightarrow{n\to\infty}\infty\text{ and }

\displaystyle Y_{*}^{(-n)}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)}{\gamma_{-n\to-n+1}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\prod\limits_{\epsilon=-n+1}^{-j}\frac{\gamma_{\epsilon\to\epsilon-1}}{\gamma_{\epsilon\to\epsilon+1}}\overset{j=-n}{\leq}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{-n\to-n+1}}\xlongrightarrow{n\to\infty}-\infty.

This means, that we have

\displaystyle\text{kern }(\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\widetilde{\text{kern }(\Gamma)}}_{\text{span }(\boldsymbol{X}_{*},\boldsymbol{Y}_{*})}\,\cap\,l^{1}(\mathbb{Z}){\mathrel{\to}^{\boldsymbol{Y}_{*}\notin l^{1}(\mathbb{Z})}}\text{span }\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{*}}{\|\boldsymbol{X}_{*}\|_{1}}\right)

(KernOfTheGenOnZ)

11
The infinite dimensional hypercube

In this section we have a look at the infinite dimensional hypercube, in the case that the network fulfills the - generalized - detailed balance condition (
gen. det. bal.
). When on top of that, the conditions of equations (
suff.cond.det.bal.
) and (
suff.cond.pos.rec.
) are satisfied, it is possible to write down an explicit expression for the stationary solution.

The infinite dimensional hypercube is defined as
\mathcal{S}_{[Q_{\infty}]}=(\Omega_{[Q_{\infty}]},\mathcal{E}_{[Q_{\infty}]})
, with and

\displaystyle\Omega_{[Q_{\infty}]}

\displaystyle:=\{0,1\}^{\infty}=\{\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\,:\,|\{i\in\mathbb{N}\,:\,\omega^{(i)}\neq 0\}|<\infty\}\text{ and }

(80)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{[Q_{\infty}]}

\displaystyle:=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in(\Omega_{[Q_{\infty}]})^{2}\,:\,\text{there exists a natural number }i\in\mathbb{N}\,:\,\alpha-\beta=\pm\boldsymbol{E}_{i}\}

It can be interpreted as an infinite set of agents, each being able to occupy two states (spin up / down, or being susceptible / infected), while all but finitely many being in one of theses states.

While it is always possible to write down the stationary solution of the
finite
dimensional hypercube (compare equation
(
95
)), in order to guarantee that the steady
states remain normalizable as the dimension tends to infinity, we need to make sure that the rates to higher dimensions decline rapidly enough: .

With the stationary solution of
finite
dimensional hypercubes be given by equation (
95
), viewing
Q_{N}
as a finite subsystem of
Q_{\infty}
(with
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{[Q_{N}]}=\boldsymbol{p}^{[Q_{N}]}\times\{0\}^{\infty}
), we can compute the norm of the difference of two stationary solutions of two ‘consecutive’ hypercubes to

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\underbrace{\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{[Q_{N+1}]}}_{\frac{(1,q^{c^{(N+1)}})}{1+q^{c^{(N+1)}}}\bigotimes\boldsymbol{p}_{*}^{[Q_{N}]}\times\{0\}^{\infty}}-\underbrace{\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{[Q_{N}]}}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\bigotimes\boldsymbol{p}_{*}^{[Q_{N}]}\times\{0\}^{\infty}}\Bigr\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\|\underbrace{\frac{\left(1,q^{c^{(N+1)}}\right)}{1+q^{c^{(N+1)}}}-(1,0)}_{\frac{(-q^{c^{(N+1)}},q^{c^{(N+1)}})}{1+q^{c^{(N+1)}}}}\Bigr\|_{1}\cdot\underbrace{\|\boldsymbol{p}_{*}^{[Q_{N}]}\|_{1}}_{1}

(81)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\,q^{c^{(N+1)}}}{1+q^{c^{(N+1)}}}

Now let
N,M\in\mathbb{N}
be two arbitrary large natural numbers and
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{[Q_{N+M}]},\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{[Q_{N}]}
be the stationary solutions of the hypercube
Q_{N+M}
and
Q_{N}
, repectivly.

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\underbrace{\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{[Q_{N+M}]}}_{\boldsymbol{p}_{*}^{[Q_{N+M}]}\times\{0\}^{\infty}}-\underbrace{\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{[Q_{N}]}}_{\bigotimes\limits_{i=1}^{M}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\otimes\boldsymbol{p}_{*}^{[Q_{N}]}\times\{0\}^{\infty}}\Bigr\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\|\boldsymbol{p}_{*}^{[Q_{N+M}]}-\bigotimes\limits_{i=1}^{M}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\otimes\boldsymbol{p}_{*}^{[Q_{N}]}\pm\sum\limits_{k=1}^{M}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{M-k}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\bigotimes\boldsymbol{p}_{*}^{[Q_{N+k}]}\times\{0\}^{\infty}\Bigr\|_{1}

(82)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{k=0}^{M-1}\left\|\underbrace{\bigotimes_{i=1}^{M-k}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\bigotimes\boldsymbol{p}_{*}^{[Q_{N+k}]}-\bigotimes_{i=1}^{M-(k+1)}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\bigotimes\boldsymbol{p}_{*}^{Q_{N+k+1}}}_{\bigotimes\limits_{i=1}^{M-(k+1)}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\bigotimes\left(\mathrm{e}_{1}\bigotimes\boldsymbol{p}_{*}^{[Q_{N+k}]}-\boldsymbol{p}_{*}^{Q_{N+k+1}}\right)}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{M-1}\underbrace{\left\|\bigotimes_{i=1}^{M-(k+1)}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\right\|_{1}}_{1}\,\cdot\,\underbrace{\left\|\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\bigotimes\boldsymbol{p}_{*}^{[Q_{N+k}]}-\boldsymbol{p}_{*}^{[Q_{N+k+1}]}\right\|_{1}}_{\frac{2\,q^{c^{(N+k+1)}}}{1+q^{c^{(N+k+1)}}}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\,\sum\limits_{k=N+1}^{N+M}q^{c^{(k)}}.

For the right choice of
\boldsymbol{c}
,
\left(\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{[Q_{N}]}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence.

Similarly to equation (
suff.cond.pos.rec.
), we get

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{[Q_{N+M}]}-\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{[Q_{N}]}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=2\,\sum\limits_{k=N+1}^{N+M}q^{c^{(k)}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2\,\sum\limits_{k=N+1}^{N+M}q^{c}=q^{c}\,M\xlongrightarrow{N,M\to\infty}\infty&\text{ , if }\boldsymbol{c}=c\cdot\boldsymbol{1}\\
\\
2\,\sum\limits_{k=N+1}^{N+M}q^{k}=q^{N+1}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{M-1}q^{k}\xlongrightarrow{N,M\to\infty}0&\text{ , if }\boldsymbol{c}=(n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}.\end{cases}

12
A network without detailed balance

In this section we look at a network without detailed balance as an example of a network, where the time limit of the
infinite
dimensional systems exists, but the corresponding limiting state can not be approximated by stationary solutions of
finite
subsystems in the thermodynamic limit.

\dots
-3
-2
-1
0
1
2
3
\dots
\gamma_{-3\to-2}
\gamma_{-2\to-1}
\gamma_{-1\to 0}
\gamma_{0\to 1}
\gamma_{1\to 2}
\gamma_{2\to 3}
\gamma_{1\to-1}
\gamma_{2\to-2}
\gamma_{3\to-3}

Figure 16
:
Example of a network without detailed balance

Let
F_{N}:=\{-N,-N+1,\dots,-1,0,1,\dots,N\}
. The components
\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F_{N}}\bigr)^{(z)}
of the stationary solution are given by the sum of the weights of all in-trees rooted in state
z

[
MG13
,
FD22
]
:

\displaystyle(P_{*}^{F_{N}})^{(-n)}

\displaystyle\propto\frac{1}{\gamma_{-n\to-n+1}}\,\prod\limits_{i=-N}^{n-1}\gamma_{i\to i+1}\prod\limits_{i=n}^{N-1}\underbrace{(\gamma_{i\to i+1}+\gamma_{i\to-i})}_{\left(1+\frac{\gamma_{i\to-f(i)}}{\gamma_{i\to i+1}}\right)\,\gamma_{i\to i+1}}\,\gamma_{N\to-N}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{a_{i}=\frac{\gamma_{i\to-f(i)}}{\gamma_{i\to i+1}}}}\left(\prod\limits_{i=-N}^{N-1}\gamma_{i\to i+1}\right)\,\gamma_{N\to-N}\,\frac{\left(\prod\limits_{i=n}^{N-1}(1+a_{i})\right)}{\gamma_{-n\to-n+1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\prod\limits_{i=-N}^{N-1}\gamma_{i\to i+1}\right)\,\gamma_{N\to-N}\,\left(\prod\limits_{i=n}^{N-1}(1+a_{i})\right)}{\left(\prod\limits_{k=1}^{n-1}(1-a_{k})\right)\,\gamma_{-n\to-n+1}}

\displaystyle\text{ for $n\in\{1,\dots,N\}$ }

\displaystyle(P_{*}^{F_{N}})^{(n)}

\displaystyle\propto\prod\limits_{i=-N}^{n-1}\gamma_{i\to i+1}\,\prod\limits_{i=n+1}^{N-1}\underbrace{(\gamma_{i\to i+1}+\gamma_{i\to-i})}_{\left(1+\frac{\gamma_{i\to-f(i)}}{\gamma_{i\to i+1}}\right)\,\gamma_{i\to i+1}}\,\gamma_{N\to-N}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod\limits_{i=-N}^{N-1}\gamma_{i\to i+1}\right)\,\gamma_{N\to-N}\,\frac{\left(\prod\limits_{i=n+1}^{N-1}(1+a_{i})\right)}{\gamma_{n\to n+1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\prod\limits_{i=-N}^{N-1}\gamma_{i\to i+1}\right)\,\gamma_{N\to-N}\,\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{N-1}(1+a_{i})\right)}{\left(\prod\limits_{k=1}^{n}(1-a_{k})\right)\,\gamma_{n\to n+1}}

\displaystyle\text{ for $n\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}$ }

\displaystyle(P_{*}^{F_{N}})^{(N)}

\displaystyle\propto\prod\limits_{i=-N}^{N-1}\gamma_{i\to i+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\prod\limits_{i=-N}^{N-1}\gamma_{i\to i+1}\right)\,\gamma_{N\to-N}\,\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{N-1}(1+a_{i})\right)}{\gamma_{N\to-N}\,\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{N-1}(1+a_{i})\right)}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F_{N}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}^{F_{N}}}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\left(\frac{1}{\gamma_{z\to z+1}\,\prod\limits_{k=1}^{|z|-1}(1+a_{k})}\right)_{z\in\{-N,\dots,-1\}},\left(\frac{1}{\gamma_{z\to z+1}\,\prod\limits_{k=1}^{|z|}(1+a_{k})}\right)_{z\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}},\frac{1}{\gamma_{N\to-N}(1+a)^{N}},\boldsymbol{0}\right)

For a special choice of rates, in particular

\displaystyle\gamma_{z\to z+1}

\displaystyle=q^{|z|}\text{ for all $z\in\mathbb{Z}$ and some $q\in(0,1)$ and }

(83)

\displaystyle\gamma_{k\to-k}

\displaystyle=a\,q^{k}\text{ for all $k\in\mathbb{N}$ and some $a>0$},

we get:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{F_{N}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}^{F_{N}}}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\left(\frac{1+a}{[(1+a)\,q]^{|z|}}\right)_{z\in\{-N,\dots,-1\}},\left(\frac{1}{[(1+a)\,q]^{|z|}}\right)_{z\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}},\frac{1+a}{[(1+a)\,q]^{N}},\boldsymbol{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{[F_{N}]}+\frac{a}{[(1+a)\,q]^{N}}\,\frac{\boldsymbol{E}_{N}-\boldsymbol{P}^{[F_{N}]}}{\mathcal{Z}^{F_{N}}}\xlongrightarrow[\|\cdot\|_{1}]{N\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}_{*}

\displaystyle\text{ with }\boldsymbol{P}_{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\,\left(\left(\frac{1+a}{[(1+a)\,q]^{|z|}}\right)_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{<\,0}},\left(\frac{1}{[(1+a)\,q]^{|z|}}\right)_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq\,0}}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+a)\,(1+q)}{(1+a)\,q-1}.

and
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}\in l^{1}(\Omega)\iff(1+a)\,q>1
.

It can easily be verified that
\boldsymbol{P}_{*}
indeed lies in the kernel of the generator matrix:

\displaystyle\bigl(\Gamma\,\boldsymbol{P}_{*}\bigr)^{(z)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{s\in\mathbb{Z}}\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{(s)}\gamma_{s\to z}-\boldsymbol{P}_{*}^{(z)}\gamma_{z\to}=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{z=-n<0}}\underbrace{P_{*}^{(-n-1)}}_{\frac{(1+a)}{[(1+a)\,q]^{n+1}}}\,\underbrace{\gamma_{-n-1\to-n}}_{q^{n+1}}+\underbrace{\gamma_{n\to-n}}_{a\,q^{n}}-\underbrace{P_{*}^{(n)}}_{\frac{1}{[(1+a)\,q]^{n}}}\,\underbrace{P_{*}^{(-n)}}_{\frac{(1+a)}{[(1+a)\,q]^{n}}}\,\underbrace{\gamma_{-n\to-n+1}}_{q^{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{[(1+a)]^{n}}+\frac{a}{[(1+a)]^{n}}-\frac{1+a}{[(1+a)]^{n}}=0,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{z=0}}\underbrace{P_{*}^{(-1)}}_{\frac{1}{q}}\,\underbrace{\gamma_{-1\to 0}}_{q}-\underbrace{P_{*}^{(0)}}_{1}\,\underbrace{\gamma_{0\to 1}}_{q^{0}}=0,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\to}^{z=n>0}}\underbrace{P_{*}^{(n-1)}}_{\frac{1}{[(1+a)\,q]^{n-1}}}\,\underbrace{\gamma_{n-1\to n}}_{q^{n-1}}-\underbrace{P_{*}^{(n)}}_{\frac{1}{[(1+a)\,q]^{n}}}\,\underbrace{\left[\overbrace{\gamma_{n\to n+1}}^{q^{n}}+\overbrace{\gamma_{n\to-n}}^{a\,q^{n}}\right]}_{(1+a)\,q^{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1+a)^{n-1}}-\frac{(1+a)}{(1+a)^{n}}=0.

Hence, we can conclude that the time limit of the network depicted in figure
16
exists (that is
\boldsymbol{P}(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0})\xlongrightarrow[\|\cdot\|_{1}]{t\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}_{*}
) due to theorem
29
, since it is both
irreducible
and - if the rates are as in equation (
83
) -
positive recurrent
.

However, the
thermodynamic
limit
\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}^{F}
does
not
exist, since for every finite set
F_{\epsilon}
, it is possible to choose a larger finite set
F\supseteq F_{\epsilon},F\in\text{Fin}(\Omega)
such that the limiting state coincides with some unit vector,
\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}^{F}=\boldsymbol{E}_{N}
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

13
The linear chain with one open end and one trapping state

In this section we look at a network, where the thermodynamic limit of finite subsystems exists, but the time limit of the
infinite
dimensional system does not. In other words, the finite subsystems wrongly suggest a candidate for a stationary solution, since they a lacking an essential feature of the infinite dimensional system.

0
1
2
3
4
\dots
p\,q
p\,q^{2}
p\,q^{3}
(1-p)\,q^{4}

(1-p)\,q^{3}
(1-p)\,q^{2}
(1-p)\,q

Figure 17
:
Network of a linear chain with an open end on one side and a trapping state on the other, for
q\in(0,1)
and
p\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
.

First, we note that the subnetwork
\mathbb{N}
is
recurrent
for
p\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
transient
for
p>\frac{1}{2}
(compare section
9
).

Now, what is the effect of adding the trapping state
0
? First and most obviously, the network is no longer irreducible. The existence of the time limit now depends on the parameter
p\in(0,1)
: For
p\leq\frac{1}{2}
, the subnetwork
\mathbb{N}
is
recurrent
, which means every trajectory visits the state
1
infinitely often. With the trapping state
0
, this means that every trajectory is eventually being captured by
0
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\text{trajectory is \emph{not} captured after the n-th return})

\displaystyle=(1-q_{1\to 0})^{n}\xlongrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

On the other hand, if
p>\frac{1}{2}
, then the state
1
is visited only finitely often, which means that there is a non-zero chance of escaping ‘towards infinity’, that is
\boldsymbol{P}(t)\xlongrightarrow[\text{pointwise}]{t\to\infty}(p_{*},\boldsymbol{0})
with some
p_{*}\in(0,1)

[
Sen06
]
. This means that the time limit of the
infinite
dimensional system does
not
exist.

However, for every
\epsilon>0
we can choose a finite set
F_{\epsilon}\supseteq\{0,\dots,N_{\epsilon}\}
such that
\bigl(\boldsymbol{P}_{0}\bigr)\Bigl(\{0,\dots,N_{\epsilon}\}\Bigr)\geq 1-\epsilon
, which results in
\|\underbrace{\boldsymbol{P}_{*}}_{\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}-\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}^{F}\|_{1}<\epsilon
for all finite sets
F\supseteq F_{\epsilon}
. This mean, that the
thermodynamic limit
for
finite
subsystems exists, even though the time limit for the
infinite
dimensional system does not. This is of course due to the fact that in a finite system, the probability is restricted within a finite set and cannot escape ‘towards infinity’.

14
Discussion and Conclusion

We studied the differential Chapman-Kolmogorov equation - which is often referred to as the
master equation
for a discrete, countable infinite system. While this is well understood in the finite dimensional case
[
FD22
,
Van92
]
, solving an
infinite dimensional
master equation is often avoided and one uses either approximations or entirely different methods.

While section
3
serves as a reminder that master equations are closely related to continuous-time Markov chains, section
4
focuses on the actual
solution
, which can be shown to exist, if the rates
\gamma_{i\to j}
satisfy certain assumptions. Moreover, it can be shown that - similarly to the finite dimensional case - the solutions remains a probability vector for all times and it is - element wise - strictly positive, for irreducible networks.

From a physical perspective, one is not only interested in a unique solution, but also, if for a given finite time, one is able to approximate the behavior of an
infinite
dimensional system by a corresponding
finite
one. This
thermodynamic limit
is addressed in section
5
.

Another important aspect is the
limiting
behavior of the master equation (see section
7
), that is the existence of the limit
t\to\infty
, as this determines the state that a system is after a long time. While this limit is always guaranteed in the finite dimensional case, this is not trivial in the Banach space
l^{1}(\Omega)
. We were able to show that the limit
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\mathrm{e}^{t\,\Gamma}\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0}
exists with respect to the
\|\cdot\|_{1}
-norm, if and only if the
Cesaro mean
(a.k.a. the
ergodic mean
) of the stochastic Markov transition matrix
\mathrm{e}^{\Gamma}
exists. This mean ergodic space can explicitly be characterized as the direct sum of of its stationary states and the closure of the image of the transition matrix minus the identity:

\displaystyle l^{1}_{\text{m.e.}}(\Omega,\mathrm{e}^{\Gamma})

\displaystyle=\text{kern }(\mathbb{1}-\mathrm{e}^{\Gamma})\oplus\overline{\text{image }(\mathbb{1}-\mathrm{e}^{\Gamma})}.

This mean ergodic space coincides with the whole space, if the corresponding system if both
irreducible
and
positive recurrent
(see theorem
29
).

Chapter
8
gives us a sufficient condition for the thermodynamic limit of
stationary
solutions for finite subsystems, which is especially easy to verify if the system satisfies the - generalized -
detailed balance
condition. Various examples are discussed in the sections
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
and
13
.

A natural question to ask, is if and how it is possible to generalize our results. When we were considering the time limit for infinite dimensional system, we required our system to be both
irreducible
and
positive recurrent
. While positive recurrence is required to guarantee the stationary solution (compare lemma
23
), the condition of irreducibility can be relaxed to some extend. Since
P^{(\omega)}(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}_{0})\xlongrightarrow{t\to\infty}0
for every transient state
\omega\in\Omega

[
Sen06
]
, the theorems still work if the network has finitely many transient states. For arbitrary many transient state, further research is needed.

It is also possible to have more than one strongly connected component (or more precise: more than one
minimal absorbing set

[
FD22
]
). Instead of requiring positive recurrence for the whole network, we now need to have positive recurrence for every minimal absorbing set. However, we note that minimal absorbing sets need not exist for infinite networks.

Table
1
summarizes all possible cases, of whether the consecutive limits of the time- and system size exist
\checkmark
,
not
exist
\times
and coincide.

existence of
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t)

existence of
\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t)

if they exist, do the limits coincide ?

(i)

\checkmark

\checkmark

\checkmark

(ii)

\checkmark

\times

\times

(iv)

\checkmark

\times

Table 1
:
Listing all possible cases of the (non)-existence of the two consecutive limits: time- and system size

While this paper has mainly focused on case (i), that is providing sufficient conditions, which guarantee that both limits exist and commute, there are interesting cases, when they do not. The case (iii) is covered by the linear chain with one open end, in the case that
\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\gamma_{0\rightsquigarrow n}}{\gamma_{0\leftlsquigarrow n}}=\infty
(see section
9
).

Section
12
addresses case (iv): A network without detailed balance, where the probability flows from left to right and is ‘reshuffled’ back to the left side (see figure
18
for a reminder). The time limit for this infinite dimensional system exists for a suitable choice of rates (see equation (
83
)) - but the thermodynamic limit of the stationary solutions does not, due to topological reasons (compare figure
2
).

This view can be challenged, by noting that it is possible to choose another increasing sequence of finite subsets
(F_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\text{Fin}(\Omega)
, such that
\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}^{F_{N}}\xlongrightarrow{N\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}_{\infty}
. So one could say, that this is just matter of how we defined our thermodynamic limit. We further believe, that if the time limit for the infinite dimensional system exists, it is always possible to choose such an increasing sequence of finite sets but this is just a hypothesis.

In section
13
we discussed case (v): A linear chain with an open end on one side and a trapping state on the other. The thermodynamic limit of the stationary solutions of the finite system always exists (
\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t)=\boldsymbol{E}_{0}
), but the time limit of the
infinite
network only exist, when the subnetwork
\mathbb{N}\subsetneq\Omega\hat{=}\mathbb{N}_{0}
is

(that is, when
p\leq\frac{1}{2}
, compare figure

). This has certain similarities with the phase transition in the Ising model, where the two limits (magnetization to zero and system size to infinity) only commute, when the inverse temperature is below a critical values:
\beta\leq\beta_{c}

[
FV18
]
.

Figure 19
:
Network
19(a)
and generator matrix
19(b)
of a linear chain with an open end on one side, and a trapping state on the other, for

.
The corresponding time limit and the thermodynamic limit are depicted for different values of

: For
p\leq\frac{1}{2}
(figure
19(c)
) the diagram commutes, while for
p>\frac{1}{2}
(figure
19(d)
) the limit for the infinite dimensional system does not exist:
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t)=\lightning
, but the thermodynamic limit of the stationary solutions of
finite
systems, does:
\lim\limits_{|F|\to\infty}\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t)=\boldsymbol{E}_{0}
.

The case that we are still missing is case (ii). The question of whether it is possible that both consecutive limits exist, but do
not
agree, is still open and while we could not find a network displaying this behavior, we also can not rule out that such a network exists.

Another interesting question would be to ask for sufficient condition, of when the iterated limit
\lim\limits_{{t\to\infty}\atop{|F|\to\infty}}\boldsymbol{P}^{F}(t\,|\,\boldsymbol{P}^{[F]}_{0})
exists. From a physical point of view, this can be interpreted as gradually increasing the system as time passes. The existence of the iterated limit would mean that no matter how fast or slowly or in which way you increase your system, it would eventually approach a steady state. However, this too is a question for which we need future research.

Appendix A
